Open-closed Deligne–Mumford field theories: geometric foundations
Amanda Hirschi, Kai Hugtenburg

Abstract
. We construct global Kuranishi charts for moduli spaces of pseudo-holomorphic maps of arbitrary genus with boundary on an embedded Lagrangian submanifold. We then build the geometric foundations required for obtaining compatible chain-level operations, which are employed in follow-up work to construct an open-closed Deligne–Mumford field theory.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Context

The success of invariants based on curves with boundary has been clear since the construction of Lagrangian Floer theory,
[
15
]
,
[
17
]
, and the Fukaya category,
[
51
]
. However, while the construction of enumerative invariants based on closed curves in a closed symplectic manifold
(X,\omega)
was established relatively early on, the same program in the open setting, that is, considering bordered curves with Lagrangian boundary conditions, was faced with two problems, the possible non-orientability of the relevant moduli spaces and the fact that they, respectively their compactifications have codimension
1
strata.

There are several approaches to dealing with these boundary strata and the way they obstruct invariants. One way is to work in a situation where they do not occur. This was pursued by Liu in
[
42
]
, defining invariants from curves of any complexity in the presence of a circle action on the symplectic manifold that left the Lagrangian invariant. In a similar vein,
[
25
]
constructs invariants based on pseudo-holomorphic disks if the Lagrangian is the fixed point locus of an anti-symplectic involutions.
Another ansatz, restricted to dimension
4
and
6
, is given in
[
60
,
61
]
, following the seminal work
[
59
]
. The construction which can be described as ‘counting intelligently’, that is, one defines an invariant explicitly in terms of certain counts and shows that the resulting numbers do not depend on the choices required for the count. While very geometric, this approach requires geometric assumptions on the Lagrangian, restricting the class of examples.

In
[
22
,
23
]
, Fukaya took a very different approach to construct genus
0
open Gromov–Witten invariants. Concretely, he considers the algebraic structure arising from the boundary of the moduli space in its own right and extracts invariants from this structure for Lagrangians in Calabi-Yau
3
-folds. This viewpoint is further developed in
[
17
,
19
,
18
,
20
]
.
Building on their insights, Solomon–Tukachinsky established an algebraic framework to define genus
0
open Gromov–Witten invariants in any dimension as long as the relevant moduli spaces were sufficiently regular,
[
54
,
55
,
56
]
. Whereas the invariants defined by Solomon-Tukachinsky are real numbers, Haney,
[
26
]
, constructs rational open Gromov–Witten invariants for monotone Lagrangians, based on a Morse model for the Fukaya algebra as discussed in
[
8
]
. The main difficulty of this strategy is to construct compatible
chain-level
operations based on moduli spaces of curves with boundary.

A fourth approach was outlined in
[
21
]
. It is based on the observation that boundary degenerations of curves, when looking at how their boundary circles degenerate, correspond exactly to string topology operations such as the string bracket and cobracket. In the non-equivariant setting, this was proven by Irie in
[
34
,
35
]
, showing that moduli spaces of disks define an Maurer-Cartan element for the BV algebra structure on the homology of the loop space of the Lagrangian. A similar approach is taken in
[
14
]
, where they construct invariants in the skein module for Lagrangians in Calabi-Yau
3
-folds.

However, to work with moduli spaces of curves with boundary one has to deal with the lack of transversality that is ubiquitous in symplectic geometry outside of special cases. In particular, the boundary mentioned above should be understood in the derived sense - often the moduli space is highly singular. A very general framework, using the language of derived orbifolds, to describe these moduli spaces is developed in
[
47
]
, while Kuranishi structures for moduli spaces of disks were developed in
[
22
,
23
,
16
,
19
,
18
]
in order to construct the chain-level operations described above, while
[
42
]
constructs local Kuranishi charts for moduli spaces of curves in higher genus. Other approaches are given by
[
38
]
using polyfolds and
[
52
]
, the methods of
[
11
]
were adapted to disks. For closed holomorphic curves, the foundational work
[
3
]
, followed by
[
4
]
,
[
27
]
, resolved these problems using the language of derived orbifolds/global Kuranishi charts. This paper extends these results to the case of holomorphic maps with a Lagrangian boundary condition.

1.2.
Main results

This paper constructs global Kuranishi charts for moduli spaces of curves with boundary on a Lagrangian and describes how to get compatible systems of such charts.
The first result is a generalisation of the global Kuranishi chart construction of
[
27
]
to open stable maps. To state it, let
(X,\omega)
be a closed symplectic manifold and let
L\subset X
be an embedded Lagrangian submanifold. Fix an almost complex structure
J
tamed by
\omega
.
We denote by
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}^{J,\beta}(X,L)
the moduli space of stable
J
-holomorphic maps on bordered Riemann surfaces of genus
g
equipped with
k
interior marked points and
\ell
boundary marked points as well as an ordering of the
h
boundary components. See
[
48
]
for a similar construction in the case of discs.

Theorem A
.

For any
g,h\geq 0
and
k,\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{h}\geq 0
the following holds.

a)

\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{J,\beta}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L)
admits a rel-
C^{\infty}
global Kuranishi chart
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E},s)
with boundary whose virtual dimension agrees with the expected dimension and which is unique up to equivalence of global Kuranishi charts.

b)

The orientation sheaf of the global Kuranishi chart is canonically isomorphic to the orientation sheaf of the virtual tangent bundle of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{J,\beta}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L)
.

c)

If
J^{\prime}
is another
\omega
-tame almost complex structure, then we can choose auxiliary data for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{J}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L;\beta,\mu)
and
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{J^{\prime}}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L;\beta,\mu)
so that the resulting global Kuranishi charts are (oriented) cobordant.

Both the forgetful (or stabilisation) map

\text{st}\colon\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\,J,\,\beta}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L)\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}

(when the latter is nonempty) and the evaluation map

ev\colon\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\,J,\,\beta}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L)\to X^{k}\times L^{|\ell|}

can be lifted to smooth maps on the thickening
\mathcal{T}
. Replacing the global Kuranishi chart by a stabilisation, the evaluation map can moreover be assumed to a be a submersion, cf.
[
3
]
. The smooth structure of
\mathcal{K}
is not canonical. We first construct a relative smooth structures as in
[
27
]
and upgrade this to a smooth structure in §
C.3
, using a relative version of the smoothing theory of Lashof,
[
41
]
. The advantage of this refinement is that we can achieve smoothness of the stabilisation map and the evaluation map.

In contrast to the case of moduli spaces of stable maps from closed surfaces, the global Kuranishi charts of Theorem
A
have boundary and corners. On the level of moduli spaces, the boundary strata are given by fibre products of moduli spaces of lower complexity. We lift this statement to the level of global Kuranishi charts.

Theorem B

(Theorem
3.5
)
.

The restriction of
\mathcal{K}
to a boundary stratum of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}^{\,J,\,\beta}(X,L)
is oriented equivalent, up to an explicitly computed sign, to the respective fibre product of global Kuranishi charts.

Theorem
3.5
also determines the orientation signs of the clutching maps with respect to the boundary orientation. This recursive structure of the boundary is the geometric basis underlying all algebraic structures arising from these moduli spaces. Having a sufficiently strong equivalence of the global Kuranishi charts over boundary strata will be crucial in the construction of compatible chain-level operations such as in the Fukaya algebra. This is considerably more subtle for curves with genus because the boundary strata are more complicated and different types of nodes appear.

Remark 1.1
.

To our knowledge, this is the first time that these orientation signs, even for moduli spaces of open stable curves, have been worked out in higher genus. We use the orientation construction of
[
9
]
and their results on behaviour of orientation lines under degenerations; similar results have been shown in
[
58
,
16
]
. We recall the constructions of
[
9
]
in §
B
.

While it is not always explicitly phrased that way, the key point of the obtaining algebraic structures from moduli spaces in
[
22
]
and
[
56
]
is to consider moduli spaces as correspondences, which define push-pull operations. Concretely, given a moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
whose marked points are partitioned into incoming and outgoing, a global Kuranishi chart
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E},s)
as in Theorem
A
,
equipped with a Thom form of the obstruction bundle
, can be considered as ‘virtual’ correspondence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{k^{-}}\times L^{\ell^{-}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{k^{+}}\times L^{\ell^{+}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev^{+}}

and thus yields a pull-push operation

\displaystyle\Omega^{*}(X^{k^{-}}\times L^{\ell^{-}})

\displaystyle\to\Omega^{*}(X^{k^{+}}\times L^{\ell^{+}})

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\mapsto ev^{+}_{*}({ev^{-}}^{*}(\alpha)\wedge s^{*}\tau_{\mathcal{E}}).

This can be defined in any setting where (certain) morphisms define an exceptional pushforward (or pullback). For many applications it is necessary that the operation associated to a boundary stratum
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\times_{L}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
(respectively
\mathcal{K}
) agrees with the the composition of the operations coming from
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
and
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
. This is immediate for
[
55
]
since they work directly with the moduli spaces themselves. It holds in closed Gromov-Witten theory (
[
30
]
) because these operations are cohomological and the virtual fundamental class only depends on the equivalence class of global Kuranishi charts.

In
[
6
]
and
[
49
]
, global Kuranishi charts were constructed inductively to ensure compatibility in the setting of Hamiltonian Floer theory; cf.
[
2
]
.
In
[
5
]
, perturbation data were assigned to chains to obtain a strict framed
E_{2}
action on a chain-model of symplectic cohomology.
Either approach is in the operadic setting, respectively in genus zero. We instead opt for incorporating the differences between choices into the operation itself to obtain strict compatibility.
To state the theorem, recall that boundary strata of moduli space are indexed by dual graphs
\Gamma
. Their precise definition is given in §
4.3
.

Theorem C

(Theorem
4.1
)
.

Given a choice of auxiliary data for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h}^{J,\beta}(X,L)
for each
g,h\geq 0
and
\beta\in H_{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z})
, respectively
H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
if
h=0
, there exists a global Kuranishi chart
\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}
for
I^{E(\Gamma)}\times\partial_{\Gamma}\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{J,\beta}_{g,h}(X,L)
for each stable map graph
\Gamma
so that

\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}|_{(0,\dots,0)}=\partial_{\Gamma}\mathcal{K}_{g,h}^{\beta}\qquad\quad\text{and}\qquad\quad\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}|_{(1,\dots,1)}\subset\prod\limits_{v\in V(\Gamma)}{\mathcal{K}_{g_{v},h_{v},k_{v},\ell_{v}}^{\beta_{v}}}

is the fibre product. Moreover, we can construct compatible Thom forms for their obstruction bundles.

The last assertion is Proposition
4.12
, which requires the extension result of §
D
. Note that we can construct these system for
all
moduli spaces of stable maps at once.
Taking the sum of the operations associated to the global Kuranishi charts
\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}
, appropriately weighted, gives operations that are strictly compatible for almost all boundary strata.

1.3.
Applications

The purpose of this paper is to build the geometric foundations required for chain-level operations obtained from moduli spaces of open stable maps. We describe several applications here.

1.3.1.
Open-closed Deligne–Mumford field theories

The structure of an
A_{\infty}
algebra on a vector space
\mathscr{A}
is most often described as the data of linear operations
\mu^{k}\colon\mathscr{A}^{\otimes k}\to\mathscr{A}
that satisfy the
A_{\infty}
relations, cf.
[
51
]
, or equivalently as an action of the
A_{\infty}
operad. Another formulation, which is closer to the notion of a TCFT as in
[
12
]
, is to see an
A_{\infty}
structure as a dg functor

(1.1)

\mathscr{F}\colon C_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,1;0,\bullet+1})\to\text{Ch}_{R}

from the category of chains on moduli spaces of stable disks with boundary marked points. An open-closed Deligne–Mumford field theory (DMFT) is a generalisation of this to chains on moduli spaces of stable curves of arbitrary complexity, that is, we allow any genus and any number of boundary components. In practise, we cannot achieve strict compatibility with all the gluing of surface - but we can construct explicit homotopies. This leads to the following definition.

Definition 1.2
.

An
open-closed Deligne–Mumford field theory
is an
A_{\infty}
functor
\mathscr{F}\colon C_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc})\to\text{Ch}_{R}
.
\mathscr{F}
is
strict
if
\mathscr{F}
is a dg functor,
semi-strict
if
\mathscr{F}
restricts to a dg functor on
C_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{st}_{oc})
and
operadic
if
\mathscr{F}
restricts to a dg functor on
C_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{opr}_{oc})
.

Here
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}
denotes the category of possibly bordered Riemann surfaces with both interior and boundary marked points. We refer the reader to the follow-up paper
[
29
]
for a precise definition.

The target category
\text{Ch}_{\mathbb{R}}
is the category of chain complexes of
\mathbb{R}
-vector spaces, enriched over itself. More generally, one can take any coefficient ring with a valuation. In a second paper, we show that moduli spaces of stable maps can be used to construct such an open-closed DMFT valued in chain complexes of modules over the Novikov ring. Concretely, we use the global Kuranishi charts constructed here to define an open-closed DMFT.

Theorem 1.3

(
[
29
]
)
.

Suppose
L\subset(X,\omega)
is a relatively spin embedded Lagrangian. Then, there exists a curved open-closed DFMT
\mathscr{F}_{L}
whose closed part recovers the Gromov–Witten theory of
X
and whose associated
A_{\infty}
algebra is the Fukaya algebra of
L
. If
L
is equipped with a weak bounding cochain, this can be deformed to an uncurved open-closed DMFT.

1.3.2.
Open Gromov-Witten invariants

Taking the fundamental chain of the moduli spaces of stable discs, we extend the
\mathfrak{q}
-operations of Solomon–Tukachinsky,
[
55
]
, which were defined under strong regularity assumption, to the general setting. Using the algebraic framework of
[
54
]
, this allows us to define open Gromov-Witten invariants in genus
0
for compact, weakly unobstructed Lagrangians. Here the strict compatibility of operations in the operadic setting, that is, where there is at most one outgoing marked point, makes the proofs significantly easier. The proof of the properties of these invariants, in particular their invariance under change of the almost complex structure or Hamiltonian isotopies is delegated to future work.

Theorem D

(
[
28
]
)
.

Associated to any weakly unobstructed closed Lagrangian
L
in
(X,\omega)
are genus
0
open Gromov–Witten invariants that are invariant under changes of the almost complex structure and Hamiltonian isotopies and which satisfy the open Gromov-Witten axioms of
[
54
, Theorem 4]
.

The invariance is obtained by studying the relations coming from (weakly unobstructed embedded) Lagrangian cobordisms. We expect that our geometric constructions are sufficient for defining higher genus open Gromov–Witten invariants, but more work is needed to algebraically extract invariants. We have been informed of work in-progess on numerical open Gromov–Witten invariants by Kedar–Solomon. A construction of higher-genus string-valued open Gromov-Witten invariants generalising to the work of Irie and Fukaya is given in
[
13
]
. It would be interesting to see whether the techniques of
[
1
]
could be used in conjunction with our results to define open Gromov–Witten invariants valued in complex bordism.

2.
Global Kuranishi charts for moduli spaces of open stable maps

Let
(X^{2n},\omega)
be a closed symplectic manifold and
L\subset X
a closed Lagrangian submanifold. Let
J\in\mathcal{J}_{\tau}(X,\omega)
be arbitrary. Given
g,h,k,\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{h}\geq 0
, as well as

\beta=\beta_{X}\oplus(\beta_{i})_{1\leq i\leq h}\in H_{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z})\oplus H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})

with
\partial\beta_{X}=\sum_{i=1}^{h}\beta_{i}
, define

\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\,J,\,\beta}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L)

to be the space of
J
-holomorphic maps
u\colon(C,\partial C)\to(X,L)
such that

•

C
is a bordered nodal Riemann surface of arithmetic genus
g
with
h^{\prime}\leq h

ordered
boundary components
S_{1},\dots,S_{h^{\prime}}
,

•

x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in C\setminus\partial C
are
k
distinct interior marked points

•

y_{i,1},\dots,y_{i,\ell_{i}}
are
\ell_{i}
distinct boundary marked points on
S_{i}
,

•

u_{*}[C]=\beta_{X}
and
u_{*}[S_{i}]=\beta_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq h
,

•

\text{Aut}(u,C,x_{1},\dots,x_{k},y_{1,1},\dots,y_{h,\ell_{h}})
is finite,

Remark 2.1
.

The ordering of the boundary circles is crucial for orientability, see e.g. Lemma
2.34
(
3
). However, we will omit it from the notation throughout.

By
[
62
]
, respectively
[
42
, Theorem 1.1]
, the moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{J,\,\beta}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L)
endowed with the Gromov topology is compact and Hausdorff. The main result of this section is Theorem
A
, rephrased as Proposition
2.22
and Proposition
2.31
. In order to proceed as linearly as possible, we relegate the description of the thickening, obstruction bundle and section to §
2.2
.

2.1.
Base space and framings

As in any other global Kuranishi chart construction, the key first step is the construction of a suitable base space for the thickening of the chart. This allows us to rigidify the stable maps, which is required to be able to perturb suitably. We first define the base space abstractly in §
2.1.1
, whose elements we call
framings
. In §
2.1.2
, we associate framings to open stable maps, and in §
2.1.3
we define the parametrised infinite-dimensional moduli space out of which we cut the thickening by a perturbed Cauchy–Riemann equation.

2.1.1.
Base space

Equip
\mathbb{C}P^{N}
with the standard complex structure. By
[
42
, Theorem 1.2]
and Lemma
2.2
, the moduli space

(2.1)

\mathcal{B}:=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;0,0}^{*}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathbb{R}P^{N};m)\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;0,0}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathbb{R}P^{N};m)

of maps whose complex double is a regular non-degenerate embedding into
\mathbb{C}P^{N}
is a topological manifold of real dimension

\dim(\mathcal{B})=\mu_{\mathbb{R}P^{N}}(m)+(N-3)(2-2g-h).

We make the following observation.

Lemma 2.2
.

Suppose
u\colon(C,\partial C)\to(Y,N)
is a
J_{Y}
-holomorphic map, where
Y
admits an anti-holomorphic involution
\varphi
and
L=\text{Fix}(\varphi)
. Let
u_{\mathbb{C}}\colon C_{\mathbb{C}}\to Y
be its complex double. Then
u_{\mathbb{C}}
is regular if and only if
u
is regular. Moreover, given any collection of smooth points
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in C
, we have

\text{Aut}(u,C,\partial C,x_{*})\leq\text{Aut}(u_{\mathbb{C}},C_{\mathbb{C}},x^{\mathbb{C}}_{*}).

Proof.

The first claim follows from the fact that
\varphi
and the canonical symmetric structure
\sigma
on
C_{\mathbb{C}}
induce an anti-holomorphic involution
\varphi_{u}
on
H^{1}(C,u^{*}T_{Y})
with

H^{1}((C,\partial C),u^{*}(T_{X},T_{L}))=H^{1}(C_{\mathbb{C}},u_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}T_{Y})^{\varphi_{u}}.

If
\rho
is an automorphism of
(u,C,x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
preserving the boundary components set-wise, it extends to an automorphism of
u_{\mathbb{C}}
.
∎

We need the following stronger result.
1
1

1

Liu shows that the moduli space of all bordered maps to
\mathbb{C}P^{N}
admits a Kuranishi structure; however, their transition functions are not naturally smooth.

Proposition 2.3
.

\mathcal{B}
admits the structure of a smooth orientable manifold with corners, so that the doubling map
\mathcal{B}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{g}}^{*}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},2m)
is smooth.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{c}\colon\mathbb{C}P^{n}\to\mathbb{C}P^{n}
be the real structure given by complex conjugation and define

\mathbb{R}\mathcal{B}:=\mathbb{R}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{g}}^{*}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},2m)

to be the fixed point locus of the induced anti-holomorphic involution on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{g}}^{*}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},2m)
, where
\tilde{g}=2g+h-1
. Then
\mathbb{R}\mathcal{B}
is a smooth manifold, and we can decompose it as

\mathbb{R}\mathcal{B}=\bigcup\limits_{\phi}{\mathbb{R}\mathcal{B}^{\phi}},

where
\phi
is an anti-holomorphic involution on a surface of genus
\tilde{g}
and
\mathbb{R}\mathcal{B}^{\phi}
is the closure of

\{u\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tilde{g}},\mathbb{C}P^{N})\mid\bar{\partial}u=0,\;u\phi=\mathfrak{c}u\}/\sim

in
\mathbb{R}\mathcal{B}
. Denote by
\phi_{*}
the unique anti-holomorphic involution such that

\Sigma_{\tilde{g}}\setminus\text{Fix}(\phi_{*})=\text{int}(\Sigma_{g,h})\sqcup\text{int}(\overline{\Sigma}_{g,h})

with
\phi_{*}
interchanging the two components.
The doubling construction induces a natural surjective map
\Phi\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathbb{R}\mathcal{B}^{\phi_{*}}.

It is injective as we consider real maps and not just closed stable maps to
\mathbb{C}P^{N}
. By the definition of
\mathcal{B}
, the induced map from the quotient is the restriction of a map between compact moduli spaces. Hence,it is proper and thus closed. It follows that
\Phi
is a homeomorphism. Declaring it to be a diffeomorphism, we obtain a canonical smooth structure (with corners) on
\mathcal{B}
.

In order to prove orientability, let
\mathcal{V}
be the preimage of
\mathcal{B}
under the map

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;0,0}^{\,m}(\mathcal{O}(1)^{\oplus N+1},\mathcal{O}(1)_{\mathbb{R}}^{\oplus N+1})\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;0,0}^{\,m}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathbb{R}P^{N}),

where we identify relative homology classes on
\mathcal{O}(1)^{\oplus N+1}
with those on
\mathbb{C}P^{N}
.
By assumption on
\mathcal{B}
, the map
\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{B}
is a vector bundle, and by
[
24
, Corollary 1.10]
,

(2.2)

\det(T\mathcal{B})\cong\det(\mathcal{V})

over the locus of maps with smooth domain whose complement is of codimension
1
. To see that
\mathcal{V}
, and thus
\det(\mathcal{V})
, is trivial, let
\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{N}\colon(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathbb{R}P^{N})\to(\mathcal{O}(1),\mathcal{O}(1)_{\mathbb{R}})
be the standard sections and write
\sigma_{j}^{(i)}
for the
j^{th}
standard section mapping to the
i^{th}
factor of
\mathcal{O}(1)^{\oplus N+1}
. Hence, we can define sections
\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}^{(i)}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{V}
by setting

\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}^{(i)}([\iota,C])=\iota^{*}\sigma_{j}^{(i)},

where
[\iota,C]
denotes the stable holomorphic map
\iota\colon(C,\partial C)\to(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathbb{R}P^{N})
.
These sections are linearly independent at each point since the complex double
\iota_{\mathbb{C}}
of
\iota
is non-degenerate. As

\dim H^{0}((C,\partial C),\iota^{*}(\mathcal{O}(1),\mathcal{O}(1)_{\mathbb{R}}))=N+1,

they form a global frame of
\mathcal{V}
. In particular,
\det(T\mathcal{B})
is trivial as well.
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

The canonical action of
\text{PGL}_{\mathbb{R}}(N+1)
on
\mathcal{B}
is orientation-preserving.

Proof.

If
N
is even, then
\text{PGL}_{\mathbb{R}}(N+1)
is connected, so the claim is immediate. Suppose
N
is odd and let
A\in\text{PGL}_{\mathbb{R}}(N+1)
be (the image of) the matrix interchanging the basis vectors
e_{0}
and
e_{1}
and leaving all other basis vectors invariant. Denote by
\phi_{A}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}
the associated diffeomorphism. Then

\phi_{A}^{*}\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i)}_{j}=\begin{cases}\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i)}_{1}\quad&j=0\\
\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i)}_{0}\quad&j=1\\
\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i)}_{0}\quad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Thus, the determinant of the linear map
\phi_{A}^{*}
with respect to the basis
(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i)}_{j})_{i,j}
is
(-1)^{N+1}=1
.
∎

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\zeta=[\iota,C]
be an element of the top stratum of
\mathcal{B}
. Then there exists a canonical isomorphism

\det(D\bar{\partial}_{J_{0}}(\iota))\cong\det(\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{\mathbb{R}}(N+1))\otimes\det(\mathbb{E}^{\vee}_{\zeta}),

where
\mathbb{E}^{\vee}\to\mathcal{B}
is the dual of the Hodge bundle.

Proof.

Pulling back the Euler sequence
0\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{N}}\to\mathcal{O}(1)^{\oplus N+1}\to T\mathbb{C}P^{N}\to 0
(with its corresponding sequence of real subbundles over
\mathbb{R}P^{N}
) to
C
and taking the long exact sequence in homology, we get an exact sequence

(2.3)

0\to H^{0}(C,(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))\to H^{0}(C,\iota^{*}(\mathcal{O}(1),\mathcal{O}(1)_{\mathbb{R}}))^{\oplus N+1}\to H^{0}(C,\iota^{*}(T\mathbb{C}P^{N},T\mathbb{R}P^{N}))\\
\to H^{1}(C,(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))\to 0.

The index bundle of the trivial bundle
(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R})\to(C,\partial C)
has a canonical orientation by
[
58
, Proposition 4.1.1]
. Furthermore, the basis
\{\iota^{*}\sigma_{j}^{(i)}\}_{i,j}
gives a canonical identification of
\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{\mathbb{R}}(N+1)
with
H^{0}(C,\iota^{*}(\mathcal{O}(1),\mathcal{O}(1)_{\mathbb{R}}))^{\oplus N+1}
, and the image of the first map of (
C.35
) corresponds to the inclusion of the scalar matrices into
\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{\mathbb{R}}(N+1)
. Thus (
2.3
) becomes a natural sequence

0\to\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{\mathbb{R}}(N+1)\to\text{ind}(D\bar{\partial}_{J_{0}}(\iota))\to\mathbb{E}^{\vee}_{\zeta}\to 0,

whence the claim follows by taking determinants.∎

Remark 2.6
.

We observe that the way we obtain the orientation on
\mathcal{B}
in a way which differs from the usual way of orienting moduli spaces of curves with boundary. Indeed, the Lagrangian
\mathbb{R}P^{N}
might not be orientable. However, Lemma
2.5
might seem more natural if we consider that adding the decoration of a framing can be seen as exhibiting the moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;0,0}^{\,J,\,\beta}(X,L)
as a global quotient of the form
\mathcal{N}/\text{PGL}_{\mathbb{R}}(N+1)
. This will be made more precise in the next subsection; compare also with
[
27
, Discussion 3.16]

2.1.2.
Framings of stable maps with boundary

In this subsection, we construct framings for stable maps on bordered Riemann surfaces. For this we use the results of
[
27
]
in the closed case and the complex doubles of our curves described in detail in
[
39
, §3]
.

Lemma 2.7

(Adapted polarisation)
.

Suppose
(Y,\sigma)
is any symplectic manifold and
K\subset Y
is a Lagrangian so that
[\sigma]\neq 0
in
H^{2}(Y,K;\mathbb{R})
. Then, given any compact subset
\mathscr{J}
of
\sigma
-tame almost complex structures on
Y
, there exists a line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{Y}(1)\to Y
such that
\mathcal{O}_{Y}(1)|_{K}
admits a real structure
\mathcal{O}_{K}(1)
, and for any
J\in\mathscr{J}
and any non-constant
J
-holomorphic map
u\colon C\to Y
we have

\mu(u^{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y}(1),u^{*}\mathcal{O}_{K}(1))\geq 1.

We call such a line bundle an
K
-adapted polarisation
.

Proof.

We show first that there exists a symplectic form
\Omega
on
Y
taming each
J
, so that
[\Omega]\in H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
,
\Omega|_{K}=0
, and
[\Omega]\neq 0
in
H^{2}(Y,K;\mathbb{R})
. Denote by
\mathcal{Z}
the set of closed
2
-forms and let
\mathcal{Z}_{s}\subset\mathcal{Z}
be the open subset of symplectic forms. Let
A:=\{\alpha\in\mathcal{Z}\mid\alpha|_{K}=0\}
and let
A_{\mathbb{Q}}
be the subset of
\alpha\in A
with
[\alpha]\in H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Q})
. Then
A_{\mathbb{Q}}
is the preimage of a dense set under the open map
A\rightarrow H^{2}(Y,K;\mathbb{R})
and thus dense. As
\omega\in A\cap\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, it follows that there exists
\Omega^{\prime}\in A_{\mathbb{Q}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{s}
taming
J
. Set
\Omega:=N\Omega^{\prime}
for
N\gg 1
so that
[\Omega]\in H^{2}(Y,K;\mathbb{Z})
.

By
[
27
, Lemma 3.2]
, there exists a Hermitian line bundle
(\mathcal{L},\nabla^{\prime})\to Y
so that
F^{\nabla^{\prime}}=-\frac{1}{2\pi\text{i}}\Omega
. Let
U
be a Weinstein neighbourhood of
K
. Then,
c_{1}(\mathcal{L}|_{U})=0
, so there exists a complex trivialisation
\Phi\colon U\times\mathbb{C}\to\mathcal{L}|_{U}
. In particular, we may write
\Phi^{*}\nabla^{\prime}=d+\frac{\text{i}}{2\pi}\theta^{\prime}
for some
\theta^{\prime}\in\Omega^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
with
\Omega|_{U}=d\theta^{\prime}
. In particular,
\theta^{\prime}|_{K}
is closed. Fix some Riemannian metric on
g
and let
\epsilon>0
be sufficiently small such that for any
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(U)
with
\lVert\alpha\rVert_{C^{1}}<\epsilon
, the differential
d(\theta^{\prime}+\alpha)
is a symplectic form on
U
. Let
\rho
be a bump function that is identically
1
on
K
and supported in
U
. Since
U
deformation retracts onto
K
and
H^{1}(K;\mathbb{Q})
is dense in
H^{1}(K;\mathbb{R})
, we can find a closed form
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(U)
with
\lVert\alpha\rVert_{C^{1}}<\frac{\epsilon}{2\lVert\rho\rVert_{C^{1}}}
so that
[(\theta^{\prime}+\alpha)|_{K}]\in H^{1}(K;\mathbb{Q})
. Set
\theta:=N^{\prime}(\theta^{\prime}+\rho\alpha)
for some sufficiently large integer
N^{\prime}
. Then,
\theta|_{K}
is closed with
[\theta|_{K}]=N^{\prime}[\theta^{\prime}|_{K}+\alpha|_{K}]\in H^{1}(K;\mathbb{Z})
. Since
\lVert\rho\alpha\rVert_{C^{1}}\leq 2\lVert\rho\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\alpha\rVert_{C^{1}}<\epsilon
, the derivative
d\theta
is symplectic. Thus, we may replace
{\nabla^{\prime}}^{\otimes N^{\prime}}
on
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes N^{\prime}}
by the connection
\nabla
given by
d+\theta
on
U
and by
{\nabla^{\prime}}^{\otimes N^{\prime}}
away from
U
. By construction,
-\frac{1}{2\pi\text{i}}F^{\nabla}
is still a symplectic form vanishing on
K
. We set
\mathcal{O}_{Y}(1):=\mathcal{L}^{\otimes N^{\prime}}
.

We can now construct the real subbundle
\mathcal{O}_{K}(1)
. Fix for each component
B
of
K
a base point
y_{B}
and (using the above fixed trivialisation) let
\mathcal{O}_{K}(1)_{y}
be the image of
\Phi(\{y_{B}\}\times\mathbb{R})
under the parallel transport along some path from
y_{B}
to
y
. The choice of path is irrelevant since the holonomy along any loop
\gamma
on
L
is given by
e^{-\int_{\gamma}\theta}=e^{-2\pi\text{i}k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. In particular,
\mathcal{O}_{K}(1)
is a well-defined smooth real subbundle of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}(1)|_{K}
and preserved by the parallel transport of
\nabla
. Therefore, we can apply
[
10
, Theorem 3.1]
to obtain that

\mu(u^{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y}(1),u^{*}\mathcal{O}_{K}(1))=2\langle{[\Omega],u_{*}[C]}\rangle>0

As the left-hand side is an integer, the claim follows.
∎

Given a stable smooth map
u\colon(C,\partial C)\to(X,L)
and a complex structure
\mathfrak{j}
on
C
, denote by

(C_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{j}_{\mathbb{C}}):=(C,\mathfrak{j})\cup_{\partial C}(C,-\mathfrak{j})

the
complex double
of
C
. It has arithmetic genus
g_{\mathbb{C}}=2g+h-1
by
[
39
, §3.3]
. There exists a canonical anti-holomorphic involution
\sigma_{C}\colon C_{\mathbb{C}}\to C_{\mathbb{C}}
with fixed-point locus given by
\partial C
. By
[
39
, Theorem 3.3.13]
and the argument at the bottom of p.16 op. cit., the line bundle pair
(u^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}(1),(u|_{\partial C})^{*}\mathcal{O}_{L}(1))
extends to a holomorphic line bundle
u^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}(1)_{\mathbb{C}}
on
C_{\mathbb{C}}
which admits an anti-holomorphic involution
\widetilde{\sigma}_{C}
covering
\sigma_{C}
. We define

\mathfrak{L}_{u,\mathbb{C}}=\omega_{C_{\mathbb{C}}}\otimes u^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}(1)_{\mathbb{C}}^{\otimes 3}

and set

(2.4)

\mathfrak{L}_{u}:=\mathfrak{L}_{u,\mathbb{C}}|_{C}.

Moreover
\mathfrak{L}_{u,\mathbb{C}}
admits a canonical lift of
\sigma_{C}
given by combining the pullback
{\sigma_{C}}^{*}
and
\widetilde{\sigma}_{C}
.
By
[
39
]
,

\deg(\mathfrak{L}_{u,\mathbb{C}}|_{C^{\prime}})\geq-2+3\mu(u^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}(1)|_{C^{\prime}\cap C},u^{*}\mathcal{O}_{L}(1)|_{C^{\prime}\cap\partial C})>0,

for any irreducible component
C^{\prime}
of
C_{\mathbb{C}}
, on which
u
is non-constant, while
\omega_{C_{\mathbb{C}}}
is positive on any doubling of a component of
C
on which
u
is constant.

Remark 2.8
.

If
X
is a non-compact manifold and
L\subset X
is a closed embedded Lagrangian so that
[\omega]=0
in
H^{2}(X,L;\mathbb{R})=0
, then any pseudo-holomorphic stable map
u\colon(C,\partial C)\to(X,L)
is a constant and thus
C
stable. In this case, we can define
\mathfrak{L}_{u}
to be the dualising line bundle of the domain (possibly twisted by marked points) to obtain a line bundle of positive degree on each component.

Thus, by
[
27
, Lemma 3.2]
2
2

2

If
\omega(\beta)=0
, the construction has to be adapted as in
[
27
, Remark 3.14]
.
,
there exists
p\gg 1
, depending only on the topological type of
C
(in fact, of
C_{\mathbb{C}}
) and
d:=\langle{\Omega,\beta}\rangle
, so that
\mathfrak{L}_{u,\mathbb{C}}^{\otimes p}
is very ample and
H^{1}(C_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{L}_{u,\mathbb{C}}^{\otimes p})=0
. Note that if
p^{\prime}
satisfies this condition, then so does any
p>p^{\prime}
. Thus we can choose
p
to be divisible by
4
. We have

m:=p\,\deg(\mathfrak{L}_{u})=p\Bigl(3\mu(u^{*}(O_{X}(1),O_{L}(1))+2(2g+h-2)\Bigr)

The space of global holomorphic sections
H^{0}(C_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{L}_{u,\mathbb{C}}^{\otimes p})
admits an anti-complex linear involution. A real basis of
H^{0}(C_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{L}_{u,\mathbb{C}}^{\otimes p})^{\mathbb{Z}/2}
defines a complex basis of
H^{0}(C_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{L}_{u,\mathbb{C}}^{\otimes p})
. We call a complex basis
F=(s_{0},\dots,s_{N})
of
H^{0}(C_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{L}_{u,\mathbb{C}}^{\otimes p})

real admissible
if
s_{0},\dots,s_{N}
lie in
H^{0}(C_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{L}_{u,\mathbb{C}}^{\otimes p})^{\mathbb{Z}/2}
. In this case,
F
induces a regular holomorphic embedding

\iota_{F,\mathbb{C}}\colon(C_{\mathbb{C}},\partial C)\to(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathbb{R}P^{N}),

where

(2.5)

N=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^{0}(C_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{L}_{u,\mathbb{C}}^{\otimes p})-1=p\deg(\mathfrak{L}_{u,\mathbb{C}})-2g-h+1.

Remark 2.9
.

A real framing
\iota\colon(C,\partial C)\to(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathbb{R}P^{N})
is determined by
[\iota_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}\mathcal{O}(1)]\in\Pic(C_{\mathbb{C}})

up to the
\text{PGL}_{\mathbb{R}}(N+1)
-action on the space of holomorphic real stable maps
C_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}P^{N}
.

2.1.3.
Reducing the structure group

We will take a middle path between the approaches of
[
4
]
and
[
27
]
to this problem. The aim is to simplify the construction of the cross-section
\lambda_{\mathcal{U}}
in
[
27
]
, while not constructing domain-dependent metrics as in
[
4
]
and instead using a perturbation scheme similar to
[
27
]
. To this end, abbreviate

\mathcal{G}:=\text{PGL}_{\mathbb{R}}(N+1)\qquad\qquad G:=\text{PO}(N+1)

and denote by
\mathcal{B}
the space defined in (
2.1
). As
\mathcal{B}
is a manifold, it admits a universal curve
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}\times\mathbb{C}P^{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev}

By construction, the boundary of a fibre of
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{B}
is mapped to
\mathbb{R}P^{N}
. Similarly to
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{F}}
defined in
[
4
, §4.3]
, let
\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{B}
be the family of maps
u\colon(\mathcal{C}|_{[\iota]},\partial\mathcal{C}|_{[\iota]})\colon(X,L)
with
[\iota]\in\mathcal{B}

such that

•

\int_{C^{\prime}}u^{*}\omega\geq 0
for any irreducible component
C^{\prime}
of
C
,

•

\int_{C^{\prime}}u^{*}\omega\geq\hbar
for any unstable irreducible component
C^{\prime}
of
C
.

Equip
\mathcal{Z}
with the Gromov-Hausdorff metric on the graphs in
\mathbb{C}P^{N}\times X
and with the obvious
\mathcal{G}
-action lifting the
\mathcal{G}
-action on
\mathcal{B}
.
Recall that the action of a topological group
H
on space
W
is
Palais proper
if for any
w\in W
and any subset
V\subset W
there exists a neighbourhood
U
of
w
so that
\{h\in H\mid h\cdot U\cap V\neq\emptyset\}
has compact closure in
H
.

Lemma 2.10
.

The
\mathcal{G}
action on
\mathcal{Z}
is Palais proper. In particular, it admits
\mathcal{G}
-invariant partitions of unity subordinate to
\mathcal{V}
for any
\mathcal{G}
-invariant open cover
\mathcal{V}
.

Proof.

By verbatim the same proof as the one of
[
4
, Lemma 4.13]
, one shows that the action of
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{Z}
is Palais proper. Given this, we obtain the claim by
[
4
, Corollary 3.12]
, respectively
[
45
, Theorem 4.3.4]
.∎

Remark 2.11
.

In particular, given any closed
\mathcal{G}
-invariant subset
Z
of
\mathcal{Z}
and any
\mathcal{G}
-invariant open covering
\mathcal{V}
thereof, we can find a collection of continuous
\mathcal{G}
-invariant functions
\{\chi_{V}\}_{V\in\mathcal{V}}
subordinate to said open cover such that for each
x\in Z
we have
\chi_{V}(x)>0
for some
V
.

We adapt the notion of a good covering in
[
27
, Definition 3.10]
to our setting.

Definition 2.12

(Good covering)
.

A
good covering

\mathcal{U}=\{(U_{i},D_{i},\chi_{i})\}_{i\in\Lambda}
is a finite set of tuples such that the following holds.

•

Each
U_{i}
is an open subset of
\mathcal{Z}
and
D_{i}\subset X\setminus L
is a submanifold with boundary of codimension
2
satisfying for any
(u,C,\iota)\in U_{i}
that

(1)

the preimage
u^{-1}(D_{i})
consists of exactly
3d
distinct non-nodal points of
C
and

\displaystyle\#(u^{-1}(D_{i})\cap C^{\prime})=3\langle[\Omega],u_{*}[C^{\prime}]\rangle.

(2)

we have
u(C)\cap\partial D_{i}=\varnothing
and
u
is transverse to
D_{i}
.

for any bordered stable map
(u,C,\iota)\in U_{i}
. In particular, to each
(u,C,\iota)\in U_{i}
, we can associate
\text{st}_{D_{i}}(u,C,\iota)\in\mathcal{B}_{[3d]}
, by adding
u^{-1}(D_{i})
to
C
as a set of
3d
unordered interior marked points.

•

Each
\chi_{i}:\mathcal{Z}\to[0,1]
is a continuous function with support contained in
U_{i}
.

•

For any
[u,C]
in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h}^{J,\mu}(X,L;\beta)
there exists
\iota\colon(C,\partial C)\to(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathbb{R}P^{N})
and
i\in\Lambda
such that
(u,C,\iota)\in U_{i}
and
\chi_{i}(u,C,\iota)>0
.

By
[
46
, Lemma 9.4.3]
and Lemma
2.10
, good coverings exist. Fixing one such covering
\mathcal{U}
, let
V_{\mathcal{U}}\subset\mathcal{Z}
be the open subset where
\sum_{i\in\Lambda}\chi_{i}
is strictly positive. Assume that we are additionally given a smooth
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant map

\displaystyle\lambda:\mathcal{B}_{[3d]}\to\mathcal{G}/G,

where
\mathcal{B}_{[3d]}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;3d,0}^{\,m}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathbb{R}P^{N})/S_{3d}
is contained in the locus of regular embedded curves whose domains are stable. The action of the symmetric group
S_{3d}
is given by permuting the marked points.

Lemma 2.13
.

Set
\mathfrak{p}(N+1):=\{A\in\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{\mathbb{R}}(N+1)\mid A^{t}=A,\;\text{trace}(A)=0\}
. Then the composition

\mathfrak{p}(N+1)\xrightarrow{\exp}\text{GL}_{\mathbb{R}}(N+1)\to\mathcal{G}/G

is an isomorphism, which we denote by
\exp
as well, abusing notation.

Proof.

This follows from the polar decomposition,
\text{GL}_{\mathbb{R}}(N+1)\cong\text{O}(N+1)\times\text{P}(N+1),
where
\text{P}(N+1)
is the Lie algebra of positive definite symmetric matrices.
∎

2.2.
Construction

We now are able to define a global Kuranishi chart with corners for the moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;0,0}^{\,J,\,\beta}(X,L)
. It requires the choice of certain auxiliary data.

Definition 2.14
.

An
auxiliary datum
for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;0,0}^{\,J,\,\beta}(X,L)
is a tuple
(\nabla^{X},\mathcal{O}_{X}(1),p,\mathcal{U},\lambda,r)
where

(i)

\nabla^{X}
is a J-linear connection on the tangent bundle
TX
,

(ii)

(\mathcal{O}_{X}(1),\nabla)\to X
is an
L
-adapted polarisation as in Lemma
2.7
with
d:=\langle{\Omega,\beta}\rangle
.

(iii)

p\geq 1
is a positive multiple of
4
,

(iv)

\mathcal{U}
is a good covering in the sense of Definition
2.12
and

\lambda\colon\mathcal{B}_{[3d]}\to\mathcal{G}/G

is an equivariant smooth map on the moduli space
\mathcal{B}_{[3d]}
defined in §
2.1.3
,

(v)

r\geq 1
is an odd integer.

Remark 2.15
.

The restriction on the parity of
p
and
r
serves to make the discussion of orientability more convenient.

Given such an auxiliary datum, define

(2.6)

E_{r}:=\overline{\text{Hom}}_{\mathbb{C}}(p_{1}^{*}T{\mathbb{C}P^{N}},p_{2}^{*}TX)\otimes p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{O}(r)\otimes\overline{H^{0}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathcal{O}(r))}

over
\mathbb{C}P^{N}\times X
and let

F_{r}:=\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(p_{1}^{*}T{\mathbb{R}P^{N}},p_{2}^{*}TL)\otimes p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{R}P^{N}}(r)_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes\overline{H^{0}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathcal{O}(r))}^{\mathbb{Z}/2}

be the subbundle over the Lagrangian
\mathbb{R}P^{N}\times L
, where
\mathcal{O}(r)_{\mathbb{R}}
is the tautological line bundle over
\mathbb{R}P^{N}
. Note that the Hermitian structures on
\mathcal{O}(r)
and
\mathcal{O}(r)_{\mathbb{R}}
induce maps

\langle{\cdot}\rangle\colon E_{r}\to\overline{\text{Hom}}_{\mathbb{C}}(p_{1}^{*}T{\mathbb{C}P^{N}},p_{2}^{*}TX)

and

\langle{\cdot}\rangle\colon F_{r}\to\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(p_{1}^{*}T{\mathbb{R}P^{N}},p_{2}^{*}TL).

Denote the induced complex structure on
E_{r}
by
J_{E}
. We define an almost complex structure
\widetilde{J}
on the total space of
E_{r}
as follows. Write

TE_{r}\cong T\mathbb{C}P^{N}\oplus TX\oplus E_{r},

using the canonical connection on
E_{r}
.
Then define

\widetilde{J}_{(w,x,e)}(\hat{w},\hat{x},\hat{e})=(J_{0}\hat{w},J_{x}\hat{x}+\langle{e}\rangle(\hat{w}),{J_{E}}_{e}(\hat{e}))

with respect to this splitting. Since
L
is totally real with respect to
J
, we have
J_{x}\langle{e}\rangle(\widehat{w})\in T_{x}L
only if
\langle{e}\rangle(\widehat{w})=0
. Hence,
F_{r}
is totally real with respect to
\widetilde{J}
.

Construction 2.16

(Pre-thickening)
.

The
pre-thickening

\mathcal{T}^{\text{pre}}_{r}
consists of equivalence classes of tuples
(u,C,\iota,\gamma,\eta)
where

i)

([\iota,C],u)\in\mathcal{Z}
, which we will often write as
(\iota,u)
;

ii)

\alpha\in H^{1}(C,(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))
satisfies

[\iota^{*}\mathcal{O}(1)]\otimes[\mathfrak{L}_{u}^{\otimes-p}]=\exp(\gamma)

in
\Pic(C)
, where the line bundle
\mathfrak{L}_{u}
is defined by (
2.4
);

iii)

\eta\in H^{0}(C,(\iota,u)^{*}(E_{r},F_{r}))
satisfies the perturbed Cauchy-Riemann equation

(2.7)

\bar{\partial}_{J}\widetilde{u}+\langle{\eta}\rangle\circ d\widetilde{\iota}=0

on the normalisation
\widetilde{C}
of
C
, where
\widetilde{u}
and
\widetilde{\iota}
are the pullbacks to
\widetilde{C}
.

We say
(u,C,\iota,\gamma,\eta)
and
(u^{\prime},C^{\prime},\iota^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
are
equivalent
if there exists a biholomorphism
C\to C^{\prime}
preserving the order of the boundary components as well as all other structures. Note that each point of
\mathcal{T}^{\text{pre}}_{r}
has a canonical representative where the domain is given by the image of
\iota
.

Let
\mathfrak{f}\colon\mathcal{T}^{\text{pre}}_{r}\to\mathcal{Z}
be the forgetful map. Replacing
\mathcal{T}^{\text{pre}}_{r}
by
\mathfrak{f}^{-1}(V_{\mathcal{U}})
, define
\lambda_{\mathcal{U}}\colon\mathcal{T}^{\text{pre}}_{r}\to\mathfrak{p}(N+1)
by

(2.8)

\lambda_{\mathcal{U}}(y)=-\text{i}\sum\limits_{j\in\Lambda}{\chi_{j}(\mathfrak{f}(y))\exp^{-1}(\lambda_{j}(\text{st}_{\ell_{j},D_{j}}(y)))}.

In order to make use of standard gluing results, Theorem
A.4
, we will exhibit
\mathcal{T}^{\text{pre}}_{r}
(almost) as a family
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;0,0}^{*,\widetilde{J}_{E},(m,\beta)}(\pi;E_{r},F_{r})
of pseudo-holomorphic curves to
(E_{r},F_{r})
over the base
\mathcal{B}
. Let
\mathbb{E}^{\vee}
be the dual of the Hodge bundle of
\pi\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{B}
and denote its pullback to
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;0,0}^{*,\widetilde{J}_{E},(m,\beta)}(\pi;E_{r},F_{r})
by the same symbol.

Lemma 2.17
.

There exists an injection
\mathcal{T}^{\text{pre}}_{r}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{E}^{\vee}
.

Proof.

This is a straightforward adaptation of
[
27
, Lemma 4.16]
.
∎

In particular, this equips
\mathcal{T}^{\text{pre}}_{r}
with a topology so that the forgetful map
\mathfrak{f}
is continuous.

Remark 2.18
.

By
[
39
, Theorem 3.3.8]
, the vector bundle
(\iota,u)^{*}E_{r}
equipped with the induced real structure over
\partial C
extends to a holomorphic vector bundle
E_{\iota,u}\to C_{\mathbb{C}}
. By
[
39
, Theorem 3.3.3]
, there exists a doubling map

H^{0}(C,(\iota,u)^{*}(E_{r},F_{r}))\xrightarrow{\simeq}H^{0}(C_{\mathbb{C}},E_{\iota,u})^{\mathbb{Z}/2}

where
\mathbb{Z}/2
acts via the induced anti-holomorphic involution on
H^{0}(C_{\mathbb{C}},E_{\iota,u})
.

Construction 2.19

(Obstruction bundle and section)
.

The
obstruction bundle

\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{T}_{r}^{\text{pre}}
has fibres

(2.9)

\mathcal{E}_{(u,C,\iota,\gamma,\eta)}=H^{0}(C,(\iota,u)^{*}(E_{r},F_{r}))\oplus H^{1}(C,(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))\oplus\mathfrak{p}(N+1)

The
(preliminary) obstruction section

s_{0}\colon\mathcal{T}_{r}^{\text{pre}}\to\mathcal{E}
is given by

s_{0}(u,C,\iota,\gamma,\eta)=(\eta,\gamma,\lambda_{\mathcal{U}}(\iota,u)).

where
\lambda_{\mathcal{U}}
is defined by (
2.8
). The
covering group
is
G:=\text{PO}(N+1)
; it acts by post-composition on the framings and the perturbations.

It remains to show that
\mathcal{T}^{\text{pre}}_{r}
is a manifold near
s_{0}^{-1}(0)
and that
\mathcal{E}
is in fact a vector bundle. For this, not any auxiliary datum will do.

Definition 2.20
.

We call an auxiliary datum
\alpha=(\nabla^{X},\mathcal{O}_{X}(1),p,\mathcal{U},\lambda,r)

unobstructed
if for any
[u,C]\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{J,\beta}_{g,h;n,\ell}(X,L;\mu)
there exists a framing
\mathcal{F}
of
\mathfrak{L}_{u}^{\otimes p}
so that
\lambda_{\mathcal{U}}(\iota,u)=0
and

i)

the linearised operator associated to (
2.7
) is surjective when restricted to

(2.10)

C^{\infty}(C,u^{*}(TX,TL))\oplus H^{0}(C,(\iota,u)^{*}(E_{r},F_{r}));

ii)

H^{1}(C,(\iota,u)^{*}(E_{r},F_{r}))=0
.

In this case, we define the
thickening

\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{T}^{\text{pre}}_{r}
to be the subset of elements
(u,\iota,\gamma,\eta)
, where the linearisation of (
2.7
) restricted to (
2.10
) is surjective and for which (
ii
) holds.

Remark 2.21
.

By the analogue of
[
27
, Lemma 4.17]
, an auxiliary datum
\alpha
is unobstructed if and only if the canonical map
\mathcal{T}^{\text{pre}}_{r}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;0,0}^{\,\widetilde{J}_{E},(m,\beta)}(E_{r},F_{r})

maps to the locus of regular curves.

We summarise the existence of a global Kuranishi chart and its relevant properties in the following result. Compare with
[
27
, Proposition 5.1]
.

Proposition 2.22
.

Unobstructed auxiliary data exist. Given an unobstructed auxiliary datum
\alpha
, the following holds.

1)

There exists a section
s\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{E}
so that
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}=(G,\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{B},\mathcal{E},s)
is a rel–
C^{\infty}
global Kuranishi chart for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h}^{J,\beta}(X,L)
of the correct virtual dimension.

2)

\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{B}
is a rel–
C^{\infty}
-vector bundle over
\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{B}
and
s
is relatively smooth.

3)

G
acts by rel–
C^{\infty}
diffeomorphisms on
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{E}
.

Proof of Proposition
2.22
.

For the first claim, it remains to show that our perturbations suffice to achieve regularity once
r
is sufficiently large. This is a straightforward consequence of
[
27
, Lemma 3.18]
and the discussion in
[
39
, §3.4]
, and is summarised in the following lemma. Note that the locus of
J
-holomorphic curves
(u,C,\iota,0,0)
with
\lambda_{\mathcal{U}}(u,\iota)=0
is independent of
r
.

Lemma 2.23
.

There exists
r\gg 1
so that the following holds. For any
y=(u,C,\iota)\in s_{0}^{-1}(0)
, the linearised operator

D_{u}+\langle{\cdot}\rangle\circ d\tilde{\iota}\colon C^{\infty}(C,u^{*}(TX,TL))\oplus H^{0}(C,(\iota,u)^{*}(E_{r},F_{r}))\to\Omega^{0,1}_{J}((\widetilde{C},\partial\widetilde{C}),\widetilde{u}^{*}(TX,TL))

is surjective and
H^{1}(C,(\iota,u)^{*}(E_{r},F_{r}))=0.

Proof.

Given
y
as in the statement, let
C_{\mathbb{C}}
be the complex double of its domain and let
u^{*}(TX,TL)_{\mathbb{C}}
be the complex double of the Riemann-Hilbert bundle
(u^{*}TX,(u|_{\partial C})^{*}TL)
as in
[
39
, Theorem 3.3.13]
. The first statement is equivalent to the surjectivity of

\Phi_{y}\colon H^{0}(C,(\iota,u)^{*}(E_{r},F_{r}))\to H^{1}(C,u^{*}(TX,TL)):\eta\mapsto[\langle{\eta}\rangle\circ d\iota].

As
\iota
doubles to a holomorphic map
\iota_{\mathbb{C}}
, we have a commutative square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(C,(\iota,u)^{*}(E_{r},F_{r}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(C,u^{*}(TX,TL))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(C_{\mathbb{C}},E_{\iota,u})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(C_{\mathbb{C}},u^{*}(TX,TL)_{\mathbb{C}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{y,\mathbb{C}}}

where
\Phi_{y,\mathbb{C}}
is defined analogously. The vertical maps are induced by the doubling construction of
[
39
, Theorem 3.3.13]
and biject onto the fixed point locus under the anti-holomorphic involutions. By the Riemann-Roch theorem,
[
43
, Theorem C.1.1]
and the argument of
[
3
, Proposition 6.26]
, there exists a minimal
0\leq r_{y}<\infty
so that
\Phi_{y,\mathbb{C}}
is surjective for any
r\geq r_{y}
; note that they denote the parameter by
k
instead of
r
. To see that
\sup r_{y}<\infty
, one can argue as in
[
27
, §4.3]
, using the (linear) gluing analysis of §
A
. Concretely, one shows that the two conditions of the statement are satisfied for
y\in\mathcal{T}_{r}
if and only if its image in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;0,0}^{\,\widetilde{J}_{E},(m,\beta)}(E_{r},F_{r})
is regular. Thus, one can use that regularity is an open condition, even when gluing, thanks to Proposition
A.14
.
∎

Lemma 2.24
.

The image
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{T}
in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h}^{\widetilde{J}_{E},(m,\beta)}(E,F)
is an open subset of the locus of automorphism-free regular curves. In particular, it is a topological manifold with boundary. Moreover, it admits a canonical rel–
C^{\infty}
structure with corners over
\mathcal{B}
.

Proof.

Define the functor
\mathfrak{F}\colon(C^{\infty}/\cdot)^{\text{op}}\to\text{Set}
by letting
\mathfrak{F}(Y/T)
be the set of diagrams

(2.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathbb{P}^{N}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{T}}

with the following properties.

(1)

The diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is the pullback of
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{B}
along a continuous map
T\to\mathcal{B}
and the first square is cartesian.

(2)

The morphism
(F,f)\colon\mathcal{C}_{Y}/\mathcal{C}_{T}\to E/\mathbb{C}P^{N}
is of class rel–
C^{\infty}
and maps
\partial\mathcal{C}_{Y}\to F/\mathbb{R}P^{N}
.

(3)

For each
y\in Y
mapping to
t\in T
, the restriction

\displaystyle F|_{y}\colon\pi_{T}^{-1}(t)\to E

is a regular pseudo-holomorphic stable map to
E
representing
\tilde{\beta}
. Moreover, the projection map

\ker(D\bar{\partial}(F|_{y}))\to\ker(D\bar{\partial}(f|_{t}))

is surjective.

The proof of
[
27
, Proposition 3.4]
carries over verbatim to our setting. The only adaptation to be made is replacing
[
27
, Theorem 2.19]
, itself a recollection of
[
57
]
, by Theorem
A.4
.
∎

Lemma 2.25
.

\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{T}
is a rel–
C^{\infty}
vector bundle.

Proof.

Recall the decomposition

(2.12)

\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathbb{E}^{\vee}\oplus\mathfrak{p}

of the obstruction bundle, where
\mathcal{H}
has fibre
H^{0}(C,(\iota,u)^{*}(E_{r},F_{r}))
over
(u,\iota,C,\gamma,\eta)\in\mathcal{T}
.
The fact that
\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{T}
is of class rel–
C^{\infty}
and the section
\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{H}
is relatively smooth follows from a straightforward adaption of
[
27
, Theorem 2.22]
. Meanwhile
\mathbb{E}^{\vee}\oplus\mathfrak{p}\to\mathcal{T}
is the pullback of a smooth vector bundle on the base space and thus admits a canonical rel–
C^{\infty}
structure.
∎

We defined the obstruction section
s_{0}\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{E}
to be

s_{0}(\iota,u,\gamma,\eta)=(\eta,\gamma,\text{i}\log\lambda_{\mathcal{U}}(\iota,u)).

The first two components are relatively smooth by
[
27
, Theorem 2.22]
and the definition of the rel–
C^{\infty}
structure on
\mathcal{T}
through
[
27
, Proposition 5.6]
. However, the map
\lambda_{\mathcal{U}}
is
a priori
only continuous. To see this, recall the three ingredients in the definition of this map: the stabilisation maps
\text{st}_{U_{i}}\colon U_{i}\to\mathcal{B}_{[3d]}
, the map
\lambda\colon\mathcal{B}_{[3d]}\to\mathcal{G}/G
and the bump functions
\chi_{i}
. The maps
\text{st}_{U_{i}}
are relatively smooth by
[
27
, Proposition 5.7]
, respectively a straightforward adaptation thereof and the map
\lambda
is smooth by construction. The bump functions
\chi_{i}
are, however, only continuous per se.

Lemma 2.26
.

There exists a rel–
C^{\infty}

G
-invariant function
\vartheta\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathfrak{p}
so that

\vartheta^{-1}(0)\cap\{\gamma=0,\eta=0\}=s_{0}^{-1}(0).

Proof.

Our argument is inspired by
[
4
, Lemma 4.55]
. Let
\widehat{s}\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathfrak{p}
be the projection of
s_{0}
onto the last summand of
\mathcal{E}
. All functions we define from now on will be rel–
C^{\infty}
unless specified otherwise. Endow
\mathcal{T}
with a
G
-invariant metric
d\colon\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and choose
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small so that the ball
B_{2\epsilon}(x)
admits a (possibly non
G
-invariant) rel–
C^{\infty}
chart
\phi_{x}\colon B_{2\epsilon}(x)\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
for any
x\in s_{0}^{-1}(0)
. Fix
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
so that
B_{\epsilon}(x_{1}),\dots,B_{\epsilon}(x_{m})
cover
s_{0}^{-1}(0)
. Shrinking
\mathcal{T}
, we may assume without loss of generality that they form an open cover of
\mathcal{T}
. Let
\{f_{1},\dots,f_{m}\}
be a not necessarily
G
-invariant partition of unity subordinate to this open cover, and fix a smooth bump function
T\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\to[0,1]
with
T(0)=1
,
\text{supp}(T)\subset B_{1}(0)
and
\int_{B_{1}(0)}Tdy=1
. Given
r>0
, set
T_{r}(y)=r^{-k}T(\frac{1}{r}x)
. Let
\delta\colon\mathcal{T}\setminus s_{0}^{-1}(0)\to(0,\epsilon)
be a function so that

(2.13)

\lVert\widehat{s}(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}T_{\delta(x)}(\phi_{x_{j}}(x)-\phi_{x_{j}}(y))\widehat{s}(\phi_{x_{j}}^{-1}(y))dy\rVert<\frac{1}{2m}\lVert\widehat{s}(x)\rVert.

for any
1\leq j\leq m
and
x\in B_{\epsilon}(x_{j})\cap\{\gamma=0,\eta=0\}\setminus s_{0}^{-1}(0)
.
The function
\widetilde{\vartheta}_{j}\colon B_{\epsilon}(x_{j})\to\mathfrak{p}
given by

\widetilde{\vartheta}_{j}(x)=f_{j}(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}T_{\delta(x)}(\phi_{x_{j}}(x)-\phi_{x_{j}}(y))\widehat{s}(\phi_{x_{j}}^{-1}(y))dy

extends to a rel–
C^{\infty}
function on all of
\mathcal{T}
. Denoting the normalised Haar measure on
G
by
\mu_{G}
, we can define
\widetilde{\vartheta}
by

\widetilde{\vartheta}(x):=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{G}\widetilde{\vartheta}_{j}(g^{-1}\cdot x)\,d\mu_{G}(g)

to obtain a
G
-invariant function satisfying for
x\in\{\gamma=0,\eta=0\}\setminus s_{0}^{-1}(0)
the inequality

\displaystyle\lVert\widehat{s}(x)-\widetilde{\vartheta}(x)\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{G}f_{j}(g^{-1}\cdot x)\lVert\widehat{s}(g^{-1}\cdot x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}T_{\delta(x)}(\phi_{x_{j}}(g^{-1}\cdot x)-\phi_{x_{j}}(y))\widehat{s}(\phi_{x_{j}}^{-1}(y))dy\rVert\,d\mu_{G}(g)

\displaystyle<\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{G}f_{j}(g^{-1}\cdot x)\frac{1}{2}\lVert\widehat{s}(g^{-1}\cdot x)\rVert d\mu_{G}(g)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\lVert\widehat{s}(x)\rVert\int_{G}\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}(g^{-1}\cdot x)d\mu_{G}(g)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\lVert\widehat{s}(x)\rVert.

In particular,
\widetilde{\vartheta}(x)\neq 0
if
\widehat{s}(x)\neq 0
for
x\in\{\gamma=0,\eta=0\}
.
Let
\rho\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a
G
-invariant map so that
\rho
vanishes along
s_{0}^{-1}(0)
with all derivatives and
\rho^{-1}(0)=s_{0}^{-1}(0)
. Then
\vartheta:=\rho\widetilde{\vartheta}
is the desired function.
∎

Define the final obstruction section
s\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{E}
by

s(\iota,u,\gamma,\eta)=(\eta,\gamma,\vartheta(\iota,u,\gamma,\eta)).

By the same argument as in
[
27
, §5.2]
, the group action of
G
on
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{E}
is continuous and by rel–
C^{\infty}
diffeomorphisms. Finally, the reasoning in the proof of
[
27
, Lemma 5.10]
shows that
(G,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E},s)
is indeed a global Kuranishi chart for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;0,0}^{\,J\,\beta}(X,L)
.∎

2.3.
Orientation lines

In contrast to the closed case, where the global Kuranishi chart is canonically stably complex,
[
27
]
, orientability in the open setting is not always given and depends on additional choices, as described in detail in
[
24
]
. This is the reason for our specific choice of integers in the definition of auxiliary datum, Definition
2.14
. Recall that the
orientation line
of a global Kuranishi chart is given by

(2.14)

\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K})=\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{T})\otimes\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathfrak{g})^{\vee}\otimes\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{E})^{\vee},

see also Definition
B.4
.

Proposition 2.27
.

For each
[u,C]\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;0,0}^{\,J,\,\beta}(X,L)
with lift
\widehat{u}\in s^{-1}(0)
, there exists a canonical isomorphism

(2.15)

\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{\alpha})_{\widehat{u}}\cong\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(D\bar{\partial}_{J}(u))\otimes\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\partial}_{\,TC}^{\vee})

of
\mathbb{Z}/2
torsors. Thus, if
(V,\gamma_{)}
is a relative spin structure for
L
, then
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
is canonically oriented.

Lemma 2.28
.

The first summand
\mathcal{H}
in the decomposition (
2.12
) has even rank.

Proof.

We use the Riemann-Roch theorem,
[
43
]
, to compute, at a point
(u,C,\iota,\alpha,\eta)\in\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle\text{rank}(\mathcal{H})

\displaystyle=\text{rank}(F)\chi(C)+\mu(C,(\iota,u)^{*}(E,F))

\displaystyle=\binom{N+r}{r}\Bigl(Nn(2-2g-h)+N\mu(C,u^{*}(TX,TL))+nc_{1}(\iota_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}(T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{N}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{O}(r))\Bigr)

\displaystyle\equiv\binom{N+r}{r}\Bigl(N\mu_{L}(\beta)+n(N+1)(r+1)m\Bigr)

\displaystyle\equiv\binom{N+r}{r}N\mu_{L}(\beta)\quad(\text{mod }2)

If
N
is even, then the last expression vanishes. If
N
is odd, then
\binom{N+r}{r}
is even due to the Vandermonde convolution
\binom{n}{k}=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom{m}{j}\binom{n-m}{k-j}
(for any
0\leq m\leq n
) since
r
is odd.
∎

Lemma 2.29
.

The thickening
\mathcal{T}
is orientable exactly if the vector bundle
\mathcal{H}
is orientable. If this is the case, the
G
-action on the total space of
\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{T}
is orientation-preserving.

Proof.

We first show that there exists a canonical isomorphism

(2.16)

\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T_{\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{B}})\cong\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\partial}_{J})\otimes\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{H}).

Indeed, the vertical tangent space of
\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{B}
at
y
is given by the kernel of

(D_{u}+\langle{\cdot}\rangle\circ d\widetilde{\iota})\oplus\bar{\partial}_{(\iota,u)^{*}(E,F)}
,
which agrees with the kernel of

D_{u}+\langle{\cdot}\rangle\circ d\widetilde{\iota}\colon C^{\infty}(C,u^{*}(TX,TL))\oplus H^{0}(C,(\iota,u)^{*}(E,F))\to\Omega^{0,1}(\widetilde{C},\widetilde{u}^{*}(TX,TL)).

Thus the claim follows from Lemma
B.12
. Hence, the first assertion follows from this isomorphism combined with Lemma
2.5
and Lemma
2.28
. The second claim follows from Lemma
2.4
and the fact that any block matrix of the form
\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&A\end{pmatrix}
has positive determinant.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.27
.

We have the canonical isomorphisms

\displaystyle\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K})

\displaystyle\cong\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T_{\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{B}})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{B})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathfrak{g})^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{E})^{\vee}

\displaystyle\cong\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\partial}_{J})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{H})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{B})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathfrak{g})^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathfrak{p})^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{E}^{\vee})^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{H})^{\vee}

\displaystyle\cong\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\partial}_{J})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{E}^{\vee})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\overline{\mathcal{M}})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathfrak{g})^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathfrak{p})^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{E}^{\vee})^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{H})^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{H})

(2.17)

\displaystyle\cong\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\partial}_{J})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\overline{\mathcal{M}})

where we are throughout using the Koszul sign rule and the isomorphism
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(V)^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(V)\cong\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(0)
.
Thus, if
L
is equipped with a relative spin structure and the boundary components of the curves are ordered, we obtain a canonical orientation of
\mathcal{K}
by §
B
.
∎

Remark 2.30
.

Suppose
L
is relatively spin. It will be useful to have a global Kuranishi chart
\widehat{\mathcal{K}}=(\widehat{G},\widehat{\mathcal{T}}/\widehat{\mathcal{B}},\widehat{\mathcal{E}},\widehat{s})
for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}^{\,J,\,\beta}(X,L)
, so that

(1)

\widehat{\mathcal{T}}
is orientable and the covering group
\widehat{G}
acts by orientation-preserving rel–
C^{\infty}
diffeomorphisms,

(2)

\dim(\widehat{\mathcal{B}})-\dim(G)\equiv\dim(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell})
(mod
2
)

(3)

\dim(\widehat{\mathcal{T}})\equiv\text{vdim}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}^{\,J,\,\beta}(X,L))
mod
2
or, equivalently,
\text{rank}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}})
is even.

We obtain this from the global Kuranishi chart
\mathcal{K}
above by letting
\widehat{\mathcal{K}}
be the stabilisation of
\mathcal{K}
by the bundle
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{T}
. It follows from the previous sections that
\widehat{\mathcal{K}}
has Properties (
1
)-(
3
). Moreover, the evaluation and stabilisation maps pullback to
\widehat{\mathcal{K}}
. In
[
29
]
, it will be useful to use
\widehat{\mathcal{K}}
instead of
\mathcal{K}
.

2.4.
Moduli space of maps with marked points

Given an unobstructed auxiliary datum
\alpha
for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{J,\beta}_{g,h;0,0}(X,L)
, let
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}=(G,\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}/\mathcal{B},\mathcal{E}_{\alpha},s_{\alpha})
be the global Kuranishi chart with corners constructed in §
2.2
. Let

\mathcal{B}_{k,\ell}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}^{\,m}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathbb{R}P^{N})

be the preimage of
\mathcal{B}
under the map
\pi_{\underline{k},\underline{\ell}}
that forgets all marked points. As constant discs and spheres are unobstructed,
\mathcal{B}_{k,\ell}
is a smooth manifold with corners of the expected dimension.

Proposition 2.31
.

The pullback global Kuranishi chart

\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,k,\ell}:=\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}_{k,\ell}=(G,\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}_{k,\ell}/\mathcal{B},\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}_{k,\ell},s_{\alpha}\times\text{id})

is a rel–
C^{\infty}
global Kuranishi chart with corners for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{J,\beta}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L)
.

1)

(Forgetful maps) Forgetting a marked point induces a morphism

\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,k,\ell}\to\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,k-1,\ell}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathcal{K}_{k,\ell}\to\mathcal{K}_{k,\ell-1},

of rel–
C^{\infty}
global Kuranishi charts as in
[
30
, Definition A.1]
. They are rel–
C^{\infty}
submersions away from subsets of codimension
2
, respectively codimension
1
.

2)

(Evaluation maps) The evaluation map
ev
on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}^{J,\beta}(X,L)
lifts to a relatively smooth
G
-invariant map
ev\colon\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k,\ell}\to X^{k}\times L^{\ell}
.

Proof.

As the forgetful map
\mathcal{B}_{k,\ell}\to\mathcal{B}
is smooth,
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,k,\ell}
is a rel–
C^{\infty}
global Kuranishi chart. It remains to show that the forgetful map

s_{\alpha,k,\ell}^{-1}(0)/G\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{J,\beta}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L)

is a homeomorphism. The argument is the same as in the proof of
[
27
, Lemma 5.2]
. The assertion about the forgetful maps is a consequence of the fact that the forgetful maps on the level of base spaces are smooth.
The isomorphism (
2.17
) yields a canonical isomorphism

\displaystyle\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,k,\ell})

\displaystyle\cong\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\partial}_{J})\otimes\bigotimes\limits_{i=1}^{k}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(TC)\otimes\bigotimes\limits_{i=1}^{h}\bigotimes\limits_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T(\partial C)_{i})\otimes\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\partial}_{\,TC})^{\vee}

(2.18)

\displaystyle\cong\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\partial}_{J})\otimes\bigotimes\limits_{i=1}^{h}\bigotimes\limits_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T(\partial C)_{i})\otimes\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\partial}_{\,TC})^{\vee},

which orients the global Kuranishi charts canonically and so proves Theorem
A
(
b
).

We now turn to the evaluation maps. By construction, the
interior
and
boundary evaluation maps

evi_{j}\colon\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{J,\beta}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L)\to X

and

evb_{i}\colon\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{J,\beta}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L)\to L

lift to continuous
G
-invariant maps
evi_{j}\colon\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k,\ell}\to X
and
evb_{i}\colon\mathcal{T}_{k,\ell}\to L
. They are relatively smooth by the same argument as used in
[
27
]
.
∎

A single
boundary
evaluation map is a submersion when the auxiliary datum is chosen appropriately as in
[
30
, Proposition 3.7]
.
However, this joint evaluation map is usually not a submersion.
To circumvent this problem, we use a general trick for global Kuranishi charts, cf.
[
3
, Lemma 4.25]
.

Lemma 2.32
.

After stabilising
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,k,\ell}
, we can extend the evaluation map
ev
to a relative submersion.

Proof.

Stabilise the global Kuranishi chart
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,k,\ell}
by
\mathcal{W}^{k,\ell}_{\alpha}:=ev^{*}(TX^{\bigoplus k}\boxplus TL^{\boxplus\ell})
. Fix a Riemannian metric
g
on
X
with respect to which
L
is totally geodesic and let
\exp
be the exponential map of
g
. Then we define the ‘new’ evaluation maps to be

(2.19)

evi_{j}(y,v_{*},v^{\prime}_{*})=\exp_{evi_{j}(y)}(v_{j})\qquad\qquad evb_{i}(y,v_{*},v^{\prime}_{*})=\exp_{evb_{i}(y)}(v^{\prime}_{i}),

which manifestly yield a submersion to
X^{k}\times L^{\ell}
on the total space of
\mathcal{W}^{k,\ell}_{\alpha}
.
∎

Remark 2.33
.

Stabilising by the tangent bundle of
X
and
L
yields very strong submersivity properties. The cost of this is that even operations at energy zero, which could have been defined without relying on a global Kuranishi chart construction, end up being deformed.

We now discuss the change in orientation that occurs when permutating boundary components, respectively interior and boundary marked points.

Lemma 2.34
.

Let
\alpha
be an unobstructed auxiliary datum for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;0,0}^{J,\,\beta}(X,L)
and
n:=\dim(L)
.

1)

Let
\tau=(i,i+1)
be a transposition in the symmetric group
S_{h}
. The associated isomorphism
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,k,\ell}\to\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,k,\tau_{*}\ell}
of global Kuranishi charts changing the ordering of the boundary components has orientation sign

(-1)^{n+1+\ell_{i}\ell_{i+1}}.

2)

The isomorphism
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,k,\ell}\to\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,k,\ell}
of global Kuranishi charts, which changes the label of two interior marked points, is orientation-preserving.

3)

The isomorphism
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,k,\ell}\to\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,k,\ell}
of global Kuranishi charts that changes the label of the two marked points
x^{b}_{i,j_{1}}
and
x^{b}_{i,j_{2}}
with
j_{1}<j_{2}
, has orientation sign
(-1)^{\text{sign}(\tau_{j_{1},j_{2}})}
, where
\tau_{j_{1}j_{2}}
is the transposition of
j_{1}
and
j_{2}
.

Proof.

The first claim, in the case of
(k,\ell)=(0,0)
, follows from the definition of the orientation on the thickening, in particular, the isomorphism (
2.17
), and the way the ordering of the boundary components induces an orientation on the index bundle of a Cauchy-Riemann operator, see
[
9
, CROrient
1\mathfrak{o}\mathfrak{s}
(2)]
or the discussion before Example 2.3.10 in
[
58
]
. In the case of marked points, we can write an open subset of
\mathcal{T}_{k,\ell}
as

(2.20)

U\cong V\times(C\setminus\partial C)^{k}\times\prod_{j=1}^{h}(\partial C)_{j}^{\ell_{j}}

for any open subset
V\subset\mathcal{T}
. The additional term
\ell_{i}\ell_{i+1}
thus arises due to the permutation of the factors of the product.
The other two cases can be deduced from the action on
U
in (
2.20
).
∎

2.5.
Uniqueness up to equivalence and cobordism

We will need a somewhat more general notion than that of equivalence between global Kuranishi charts, possibly with boundary, see also
[
27
, §2.3]
. Let us thus make the following definition.

Definition 2.35
.

Let
Y
be a smooth manifold. A
global Kuranishi chart over
Y
is a rel–
C^{\infty}
global Kuranishi chart
\mathcal{K}
equipped with a
G
-invariant rel–
C^{\infty}
map
f\colon\mathcal{T}\to Y
.

Definition 2.36
.

We call two oriented rel–
C^{\infty}
global Kuranishi charts
(\mathcal{K},f)
and
(\mathcal{K}^{\prime},f^{\prime})

equivalent
if they are related by the equivalence relation generated by

(i)

(germ equivalence)
if
U\subset\mathcal{T}
is a
G
-invariant open neighbourhood of
s^{-1}(0)
, then

(\mathcal{K},f)\sim(\mathcal{K}|_{U}):=(G,U,\mathcal{E}|_{U},s|_{U});

(ii)

(isomorphism)
if there exists an isomorphism
G\to G^{\prime}
, an equivariant rel–
C^{\infty}
diffeomorphism
\psi\colon\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{T}^{\prime}/\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and an equivariant
fibrewise affine
isomorphism
\widetilde{\psi}\colon\mathcal{E}\to\psi^{*}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
, which is linear over
s^{-1}(0)\cup\psi^{-1}({s^{\prime}}^{-1}(0))
, so that
\widetilde{\psi}\circ s=s^{\prime}\circ\psi
, then
\mathcal{K}\sim\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
;

(iii)

(stabilisation)
if
p\colon\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{T}
is a
G
-vector bundle that is rel–
C^{\infty}
with respect to
\mathcal{B}
, then

\mathcal{K}\sim(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{W}},f_{\mathcal{W}}):=(G,\mathcal{W}/\mathcal{B},p^{*}\mathcal{E}\oplus p^{*}\mathcal{W},p^{*}s\oplus\Delta_{\mathcal{W}});

(iv)

(group enlargement)
if
q\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{T}
is a principal
G^{\prime}
-bundle rel–
C^{\infty}
with respect to
\mathcal{B}
and equipped with a compatible
G
-action, then

\mathcal{K}\sim(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}},f_{\mathcal{P}}):=(G\times G^{\prime},\mathcal{P},q^{*}\mathcal{E},q^{*}s);

(v)

(base modification)
if the map
\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{B}
factors through a smooth submersion
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\to\mathcal{B}
so that
f\colon\mathcal{T}/\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\to Y
is still a relative submersion, then

\mathcal{K}\sim\mathcal{K}_{/\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}:=(G,\mathcal{T}/\widetilde{\mathcal{B}},\mathcal{E},s).

In each case, there exists, according to our conventions in §
B.1
, a canonical isomorphism

(2.21)

\det(\mathcal{K})\cong\det(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})

for equivalent global Kuranishi charts
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
. We call them
oriented equivalent
if
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
are oriented and the isomorphism (
2.21
) maps one orientation to the other.

Proposition 2.37
.

Suppose
\alpha_{i}=(\nabla^{X,i},\mathcal{O}_{X,i}(1),p_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i},\lambda_{i},r_{i})
is an unobstructed auxiliary datum for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h}^{J,\beta}(X,L)
for
i\in\{0,1\}
. Then
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{0}}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{1}}
are rel–
C^{\infty}
equivalent. If
L
admits a relative spin structure, then they are oriented equivalent.

Proof.

Define the doubly thickened rel–
C^{\infty}
global Kuranishi chart

\mathcal{K}_{01}=(G_{0}\times G_{1},\mathcal{T}_{01}/\mathcal{B}_{01},\mathcal{E}_{01},s_{01})

by letting
\mathcal{B}_{01}
be the preimage of
\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{1}
under the map

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h}^{(m_{0},m_{1})}(\mathbb{C}P^{N_{0}}\times\mathbb{C}P^{N_{1}},\mathbb{R}P^{N_{0}}\times\mathbb{R}P^{N_{1}})\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h}^{m_{0}}(\mathbb{C}P^{N_{0}},\mathbb{R}P^{N_{0}})\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h}^{m_{1}}(\mathbb{C}P^{N_{1}},\mathbb{R}P^{N_{1}}).

By the same argument as in Proposition
2.3
and
[
27
, Lemma 3.2]
,
\mathcal{B}_{01}
is a smooth manifold (with boundary) of the expected dimension and the natural forgetful maps
\pi_{i}\colon\mathcal{B}_{01}\to\mathcal{B}_{i}
are submersions with

(2.22)

\ker(d\pi_{0}([\iota,C]))=\ker(D\bar{\partial}(\iota_{1}))

and similarly for
\pi_{1}
.
In particular, by Lemma
2.5
, we see from the short exact sequence

0\to\ker(d\pi_{0})\to T\mathcal{B}_{01}\to\pi_{0}^{*}T\mathcal{B}_{0}\to 0

that
\mathcal{B}_{01}
is orientable and we have an isomorphism

\Lambda^{\text{max}}(T_{[\iota,C]}\mathcal{B}_{01})\cong\det(\bar{\partial}_{TC})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\Lambda^{\text{max}}(\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{\mathbb{R}}(N_{0}+1))\otimes\det(\mathbb{E}^{\vee})\otimes\Lambda^{\text{max}}(\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{\mathbb{R}}(N_{1}+1))\otimes\det(\mathbb{E}^{\vee}),

which is canonical up to multiplication by positive scalars.
Define
\mathcal{Z}_{01}\to\mathcal{B}_{01}
as in §
2.1.3
. Then

\mathcal{Z}_{01}=\mathcal{B}_{01}\times_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\mathcal{Z}_{i}

for
i\in\{0,1\}
.
Let
(E_{i},F_{i})
be defined by (
2.6
) using the unobstructed auxiliary datum
\alpha_{i}
. Then, the thickening
\mathcal{T}_{01}
consists of tuples
\{(\zeta,\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},u,\eta_{0},\eta_{1})\}
, where

•

(\zeta,u)\in\mathcal{Z}_{01}
with
\zeta
mapping to
[\iota_{0},C]
, respectively
[\iota_{1},C]
;

•

\gamma_{i}\in H^{1}(C,(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))
satisfies

[\iota_{i}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{N_{i}}}(1)]\otimes[\mathfrak{L}_{i,u}]^{\otimes-p_{i}}=\exp(\gamma_{i}),

where
\exp\colon H^{1}(C,(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))\to\Pic(C,\partial C)
is the exponential map,

•

\eta_{i}\in H^{0}(C,(\iota_{i},u)^{*}(E_{i},F_{i}))
, and
\eta_{0}
and
\eta_{1}
satisfy

\bar{\partial}_{J}\widetilde{u}+\langle{\eta_{0}}\rangle\circ d\widetilde{\iota}_{0}+\langle{\eta_{1}}\rangle\circ d\widetilde{\iota}_{1}=0

on the normalisation
\widetilde{C}\to C
.

The fibre of
\mathcal{E}_{01}
over
(\zeta,\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},u,\eta_{0},\eta_{1})
is given by

(2.23)

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i=0,1}\left(H^{0}(C,(\iota_{i},u)^{*}(E_{i},E_{i}^{\prime}))\oplus H^{1}(C,(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))\oplus\mathfrak{p}(N_{i}+1)\right)

and we set

s_{01}(\zeta,\gamma,u,\eta)=(\gamma_{i},\eta_{i},\vartheta_{i}(\zeta,u,\gamma,\eta))_{i=0,1},

where
\vartheta=(\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1})\colon\mathcal{T}_{01}\to\mathfrak{p}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{p}_{1}
is defined as follows. Let
U_{i}\subset\mathcal{T}_{01}
be an open
G_{0}\times G_{0}
-invariant neighbourhood of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{i}:=\{\gamma_{j}=0,\eta_{j}=0\}
, where
\{i,j\}=\{0,1\}
, so that there exists an equivariant rel–
C^{\infty}
retraction
r_{i}\colon U_{i}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
. Let
\widetilde{\vartheta}_{i}
be the pullback of the section
\vartheta_{i}
on
\mathcal{T}_{i}
constructed in Lemma
2.26
.
Let
t_{i}\colon\mathcal{T}_{01}\to[0,1]
be a
G_{01}
-invariant rel–
C^{\infty}
map that is identically
1
in a neighbourhood of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
and is supported in
U_{i}
. We claim that
\vartheta:=(t_{0}r_{0}^{*}\widetilde{\vartheta}_{0},t_{1}r_{1}^{*}\widetilde{\vartheta}_{1})
has the desired properties in a neighbourhood of
s_{0}^{-1}(0)=s_{1}^{-1}(0)\subset\mathcal{T}_{01}
. Indeed, restricting to the interior of the support of
t_{0}
and
t_{1}
, we see that

\vartheta^{-1}(0)\cap\{\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}=0,\eta_{0}=0,\eta_{1}\}=\widetilde{\vartheta}_{0}^{-1}(0)\cap\widetilde{\vartheta}_{0}^{-1}(0)\cap\{\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}=0,\eta_{0}=0,\eta_{1}\}

which is exactly the zero locus we need by Lemma
2.26
.

By abuse of notation, we denote the vector bundle summands for
i=0,1
from (
2.23
) by
\mathcal{E}_{i}
and denote the projection of
s_{01}
to the respective summand by
s_{i}
. The group
G_{0}\times G_{1}
acts on
\mathcal{T}_{01}\to\mathcal{B}_{01}
and
\mathcal{E}_{01}
in the evident way.
The arguments of Proposition
2.22
show that
\mathcal{T}_{01},\mathcal{E}_{01}
and
s_{01}
are rel–
C^{\infty}
with respect to the forgetful maps to
\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
\mathcal{B}_{1}
. By symmetry, it remains to show that
\mathcal{K}_{0}
and
\mathcal{K}_{01}
are equivalent. By (base modification), we may replace
\mathcal{K}_{01}
by
\mathcal{K}:=(G_{0}\times G_{1},\mathcal{T}_{01}/\mathcal{B}_{0},\mathcal{E}_{01},s_{01})
. The proof of
[
27
, Lemma 6.1]
carries over to our setting, showing that the vertical linearisation

Ds_{1}\colon T_{\mathcal{T}_{01}/\mathcal{B}_{0}}\to\mathcal{E}_{1}

is surjective in a neighbourhood
U
of
s_{01}^{-1}(0)
. Then,

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}:=(G_{0}\times G_{1},U\cap{s^{\prime}}^{-1}(0)/\mathcal{B}_{0},\mathcal{E}_{0},s_{0})

is a rel–
C^{\infty}
global Kuranishi chart, and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
is related to
\mathcal{K}_{0}
by (group enlargement). Finally,
\mathcal{K}_{01}
and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
are related by (stabilisation) via
\mathcal{E}_{1}\to s_{1}^{-1}(0)
by Lemmas
2.38
and
2.39
since the tubular neighbourhood theorem generalises to the relatively smooth setting.

Suppose now that
L
admits a relative spin structure
(V,s)
. Then, by the same argument as in Lemma
2.29
,
\mathcal{K}_{01}
is orientable. We will show that the horizontal maps in the diagram

(2.24)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{01})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(D\bar{\partial}_{J})\otimes\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\partial}_{TC})^{\vee}}

have the same sign when we endow the upper row via an orientation of
\det(D\bar{\partial}_{J})\otimes\det(\bar{\partial}_{TC})^{\vee}
. The orientation on
\mathcal{K}_{0}
at a point
y\in\mathcal{K}_{0}
corresponding to
\widehat{y}\in\mathcal{K}_{01}
is given by

\mathfrak{o}_{1}:=\mathfrak{o}_{D}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathbb{E}^{\vee}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\bar{\partial}^{\vee}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{E}_{1}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathfrak{g}_{1}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathfrak{g}_{1}}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathfrak{g}_{0}}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{E}_{1}}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}^{\vee}

while the orientation induced via the vertical map on
\mathcal{K}_{01}
is
