\mathfrak{o}_{2}:=\mathfrak{o}_{D}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathbb{E}^{\vee}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{1}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathbb{E}^{\vee}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\bar{\partial}^{\vee}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathfrak{g}_{1}}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathfrak{g}_{0}}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{E}_{1}}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}^{\vee}.

Thus it suffices to compare their first parts, determining an orientation on the thickening
\mathcal{T}_{01}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{o}_{D}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathbb{E}^{\vee}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\bar{\partial}^{\vee}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{E}_{1}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathfrak{g}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{o}_{D}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathbb{E}^{\vee}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\bar{\partial}^{\vee}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathbb{E}}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathfrak{g}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{o}_{D}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathbb{E}^{\vee}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\bar{\partial}^{\vee}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathbb{E}^{\vee}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{o}_{D}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathbb{E}^{\vee}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\bar{\partial}^{\vee}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathbb{E}^{\vee}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{1}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\text{ind}(\bar{\partial}_{\,TC})((N_{1}+1)^{2}-1+\text{rank}(\mathbb{E}^{\vee}))+\text{rank}(\mathbb{E}^{\vee})((N_{0}+1)^{2}-1)}\mathfrak{o}_{2}^{\prime}

where
\mathfrak{o}_{2}^{\prime}
is the orientation of
\mathcal{T}_{01}
induced by
\mathfrak{o}_{2}
. Here the first equality just uses the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, while the second equality holds because
\text{rank}(\mathcal{H}_{1})
is even by Lemma
2.28
. We have

\text{rank}(\mathbb{E}^{\vee})=2g+h-1\equiv h-1\;(\text{mod }2),

while by (
2.5
)

(N_{i}+1)^{2}-1\equiv N_{i}\equiv h-1\;(\text{mod }2).

Therefore,
\mathfrak{o}_{1}^{\prime}=(-1)^{h-1}\mathfrak{o}_{2}^{\prime}
and the first horizontal map in (
2.24
) has sign
(-1)^{h-1}
. In order to determine the sign of the second horizontal map, we also have to compute the parity of

\delta=\dim(G_{0})\dim(G_{1})+\text{rank}(\mathcal{E}_{0})\text{rank}(\mathcal{E}_{1})+(\text{rank}(\mathbb{E}^{\vee})+\dim\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{0})(\text{rank}(\mathbb{E}^{\vee})+\dim\dim\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{1})

as the sign
(-1)^{\delta}
arises from the fact that the ordering of the groups and obstruction bundle summands given by
\mathfrak{o}_{2}
in
\mathcal{K}_{01}
is not oriented equivalent to
\mathcal{K}_{1}
. By the computation above and again Lemma
2.28
, we see that

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle\equiv\dim(G_{0})\dim(G_{1})+(\text{rank}(\mathbb{E}^{\vee})+\dim\mathfrak{p}_{0})(\text{rank}(\mathbb{E}^{\vee})+\dim\mathfrak{p}_{1})

\displaystyle\equiv\dim(G_{0})\dim(G_{1})+(N+\dim\mathfrak{p}_{0})(N+\dim\mathfrak{p}_{1})

\displaystyle\equiv 0\;(\text{mod }2)

As the sign is independent of the auxiliary datum,
\mathcal{K}_{0}
and
\mathcal{K}_{1}
are oriented equivalent.
∎

Lemma 2.38
.

Suppose
\pi\colon\widetilde{W}\to B
is a rel–
C^{\infty}
vector bundle and
t\colon B\to\widetilde{W}
is a section with
t\pitchfork 0
and
X:=t^{-1}(0)
. Given any rel–
C^{\infty}
retraction
r\colon B\to X
, there exist rel–
C^{\infty}
diffeomorphisms
\psi\colon U\subset B\to W:=\widetilde{W}|_{X}
and
\widetilde{\psi}\colon\widetilde{W}|_{U}\to\pi^{*}W
so that
\widetilde{\psi}\circ t|_{U}=\Delta_{W}\circ\psi
. Moreover, if a compact Lie group
G
acts on
B
,
\pi
is a
G
-vector bundle and
t
and
r
are
G
-equivariant, then we can choose
\psi
and
\widetilde{\psi}
to be
G
-equivariant as well.

Proof.

Let
\Phi\colon\widetilde{W}|_{U}\to r^{*}W
be an equivariant isomorphism and let
p^{\prime}\colon r^{*}W\to W
be the canonical projection. Define
\psi\colon B\to W
by
\psi(y)=p^{\prime}(\Phi(t(y)))
. As
\psi|_{X}
is the zero section and
d\psi(x)
is an isomorphism for each
x\in X
by assumption on
s
, we can find a neighbourhood
U
of
X
so that
\psi|_{U}
is an open embedding.
Replacing
U
by
\bigcap\limits_{g\in G}{g\cdot U}
, we may assume that
U
is
G
-invariant. Setting

\widetilde{\psi}\colon\widetilde{W}|_{U}\to\pi^{*}W:(x,w)\mapsto(p^{\prime}(\Phi(t(y))),p^{\prime}(\Phi(w))),

we obtain an equivariant isomorphism satisfying, by construction,
\widetilde{\psi}\circ t|_{U}=\Delta_{W}\circ\psi
.∎

Lemma 2.39
.

Given the situation of Lemma
2.38
, suppose additionally that there exists a vector bundle
\rho\colon\widetilde{E}\to B
with a section
\widetilde{s}
and with
E:=\widetilde{E}|_{X}
and
s:=\widetilde{s}|_{X}
. Given any isomorphism
\phi\colon\widetilde{E}\to r^{*}E
, let
s^{\prime}
be the composite
B\xrightarrow{t}\widetilde{E}\to r^{*}E\to E
. Then, we can find a fibrewise affine isomorphism

\Psi\colon\pi^{*}E\to\pi^{*}E

which is the identity on
(\pi^{*}E)|_{X}
and with
\Psi(s^{\prime}(e))=s(\pi(e))
for
e\in\pi^{*}E
.

Proof.

Simply set
\Psi(w,e)=(w,e+s(\pi(w))-s^{\prime}(w))
.
∎

Remark 2.40
.

Suppose
\Phi\colon V\to V
is an affine transformation of an orbibundle bundle
p\colon V\to B
so that
\lVert\Phi-\text{id}\rVert
is bounded in some Finsler norm on
V
. Then, if
\eta
is a Thom form for
V
, the pullback
\Phi^{*}\eta
is still a Thom form since it is closed, has compact vertical support and
p_{*}\Phi^{*}\eta=p_{*}\eta
by
[
55
, Proposition 2.1(4)]
.

We also record the following ‘invariance’ under changes of the tamed almost complex structure.

Lemma 2.41
.

Suppose
J^{\prime}
is another
\omega
-tame almost complex structure. Then we can choose unobstructed auxiliary data
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
so that the associated global Kuranishi charts are oriented cobordant.

Proof.

Except for the orientability claim, the proof is verbatim the same as in
[
27
, §6.2]
. The claim about orientations follows from the isomorphism (
2.17
).
∎

3.
Boundary strata and orientation signs

This section investigates the boundaries of the moduli spaces of stable maps and the changes in orientation sign, Theorem
3.5
. Recall that
(X,\omega)
is a closed symplectic manifold and
L\subset X
is an embedded Lagrangian with
n=\dim(L)
. The Lagrangian is equipped with a relative spin structure
(V,\mathfrak{s})
with background class
w_{\mathfrak{s}}
. Proposition
2.22
(
a
) implies that any boundary stratum of the thickening is an open subset of the preimage of the boundary stratum of the base space, defined and discussed in §
2.1.1
. We thus first determine the possible boundary strata of the base spaces. Recall that
\mathcal{B}_{k,\ell}
is the preimage of
\mathcal{B}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;0,0}^{\,J_{0},\,m}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathbb{R}P^{N})
defined in §
2.1.1
under the forgetful map.

Lemma 3.1
.

Any boundary stratum of
\mathcal{B}_{k,\ell}
is the image of one of the following maps

1)

the clutching map

\varphi_{\mathbb{R}P^{N}}\colon\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{0},h_{0};k_{0},\ell_{0}+\delta_{h_{0}}}^{\,m_{0}}(\mathbb{R}P^{N})\times_{evb_{i},evb_{j}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{1},h_{1}+1;k_{1},\ell_{1}+\delta_{1}}^{\,m_{1}}(\mathbb{R}P^{N})\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\,m}_{g,h;k,\ell}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathbb{R}P^{N})

where
x_{0}+x_{1}=x
for
x\in\{g,h,k,\ell\}
.

2)

the self-clutching maps

\psi_{\mathbb{R}P^{N}}^{(1)}\colon\Delta_{\mathbb{R}P^{N}}\times_{evb_{a,i}\times evb_{a,j}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h-1;k,\ell+2\delta_{a}}^{\,m}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathbb{R}P^{N})\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}^{\,m}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathbb{R}P^{N}),

where the marked points that are to be identified lie on the same boundary circle, and

\psi_{\mathbb{R}P^{N}}^{(2)}\colon\Delta_{\mathbb{R}P^{N}}\times_{evb_{a,i}\times evb_{b,j}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-1,h+1;k,\ell+\delta_{a}+\delta_{b}}^{\,m}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathbb{R}P^{N})\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}^{\,m}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathbb{R}P^{N}),

where
a\neq b
, i.e., the two marked points at which we clutch lie on different boundary circles;

3)

the ‘collapse’ map

\rho_{\mathbb{R}P^{N}}\colon ev_{k+1}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}P^{N})\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h-1;k+1,\ell}^{\,m}(\mathbb{C}P^{N})\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}^{\,m}(\mathbb{R}P^{N}).

restricted to the respective preimage of
\mathcal{B}_{k,\ell}
.

The map
\varphi_{\mathbb{R}P^{N}}
is an embedding unless
k=\ell=0
,
\beta_{0}=\beta_{1}
and
h_{0}+1=h_{1}
, in which case it has degree
2
. Meanwhile,
\psi_{\mathbb{R}P^{N}}^{(1)}
and
\psi_{\mathbb{R}P^{N}}^{(2)}
are local embeddings of degree
2
, and
\rho_{\mathbb{R}P^{N}}
is always an embedding.

Proof.

This follows from the corresponding statement for the maps between moduli spaces of closed stable maps using the argument of
[
55
, Example 1.5]
, respectively Proposition
2.3
. That these images enumerate all boundary components follows from the classification of bordered surfaces with one boundary node,
[
42
, §3.2]
.
∎

As the subscripts suggest, each of these maps is also defined between the respective moduli spaces of stable pseudo-holomorphic maps with boundary on an arbitrary Lagrangian. We denote the domain of
\varphi_{L}
by
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}^{\,J,\beta}(X,L;\varphi)
and similarly for the other maps as well as the base space. If the Lagrangian is clear from context or does not matter, we omit the subscript from the map. These maps are associated to the different types of boundary nodes in the following way

•

\varphi
creates an (H3) node,

•

\psi^{(1)}
and
\psi^{(2)}
create an (H1), respectively an (H2) node,

•

\rho
creates a node of type (E).

There exist similar clutching maps at marked point whose images are strata of codimension
2
, which therefore are not part of the boundary. Their normal bundles are canonically complex lines bundles, so these strata obtain an orientation from the ambient space (if that is oriented). We first discuss how this ‘normal orientation’ compares to the orientation of the domain of the clutching map.

Proposition 3.2
.

Given an unobstructed auxiliary datum
\alpha
for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,0}(\beta)
, there exists an open and closed subchart
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\vartheta,\beta}
of

\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\vartheta}:=(G,\mathcal{B}(\vartheta)\times_{\mathcal{B}_{k,\ell}}\mathcal{T},\mathcal{B}(\vartheta)\times_{\mathcal{B}_{k,\ell}}\mathcal{E},\text{id}\times s)

that defines a global Kuranishi chart for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\,J,\,\beta}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L;\vartheta)
. It is oriented equivalent to the global Kuranishi chart of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\,J,\,\beta}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L;\vartheta)
given by

(\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{0},k_{0}+1,\ell_{0}}\times_{evb_{i},evb_{1}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{1},k_{1}+1,\ell_{1}})^{(-1)^{\diamondsuit}},

where
\alpha_{i}
is a
(k_{i}+1,\ell_{i})
-unobstructed auxiliary datum for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{i},h_{i};0,0}^{\,J,\,\beta_{i}}(X,L)
and

(3.1)

\diamondsuit=\begin{cases}\omega_{\mathfrak{s}}(\beta_{0})\quad&h_{0}=0,\;h_{1}>0\\
\omega_{\mathfrak{s}}(\beta_{1})\quad&h_{1}=0,\;h_{0}>0\\
|\ell_{1}|h_{0}\quad&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

if in the ordering of the boundary circles in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\,J,\,\beta}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L)
, those boundary circles ”coming from”
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{0},h_{0};k_{0}+1,\ell_{0}}^{\,J,\,\beta_{0}}(X,L)
are ordered first and if the order is preserved for both factors.

Proof.

The equivalence between
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\vartheta,\beta}
and the fibre product global Kuranishi chart follows from a double thickening construction as in Proposition
2.37
, except that we have to stabilise the evaluation map unless one chart consists of curves with no boundary. The details are given in the case of boundary clutching below. It remains to check the statement about the orientation signs, which by the isomorphism (
2.15
) reduces to a discussion of the orientation of the moduli space of stable curves and of the orientations of the Cauchy–Riemann operators. For the latter, fix a stable map
u\colon(C,\partial C)\to(X,L)
in the image of

\vartheta_{L}\colon\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{0},h_{0};k_{0}+1,\ell_{0}}^{\,J,\,\beta_{0}}(X,L)\times_{X}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{0},h_{1};k_{1}+1,\ell_{1}}^{\,J,\,\beta_{1}}(X,L)\;\longrightarrow\;\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\,J,\,\beta}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L).

In the language of §
B
(borrowed from
[
9
]
), we have to determine the difference between the intrinsic and limiting orientation of the Cauchy-Riemann operator
D\bar{\partial}_{J}(u)
. Thus,
[
9
, CROrient 7C(1)]
yields the sign in Equation
3.1
. The sign discrepancy between the case of
h_{i}=0
and
h_{i}>0
is due to the fact that we orient the index of
u^{*}TX|_{C^{\prime}}
on a closed irreducible component
C^{\prime}
of
C_{i}
with the complex orientation if
C_{i}
is a closed curve. However, if
C_{i}
has boundary, we equip the index of
u^{*}TX|_{C^{\prime}}
with the complex orientation if and only if
w_{\mathfrak{s}}(u_{*}[C^{\prime}])=0
. The orientation sign of the clutching map on the level of the moduli space of stable curves follows from the same computation, using that the spin structure of the boundary of a Riemann surface has vanishing background class. In the case where
h_{0},h_{1}>0
, the boundary marked points on curves in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{1},h_{1};k_{1}+1,\ell_{1}}
have to be permuted past the index bundle of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{0},h_{0};k_{0}+1,\ell_{0}}
, whence we obtain the last sign in (
3.1
).
∎

We now turn to determining the difference between the fibre product and the boundary orientation of a (normalised) boundary stratum. For simplicity, we make the following assumptions on ordering of the boundary components and boundary marked points.

Assumption 3.3
.

We first assume that all boundary marked point are ordered anti-clockwise on each boundary circle according to their labelling.

(1)

For
[\iota,C]=\varphi([\iota_{0},C_{0}],[\iota_{1},C_{1}])
we have that

(a)

the ordering
\mathfrak{i}
of the boundary components of
C
is given by
\mathfrak{i}=(\mathfrak{i}_{0},\mathfrak{i}_{1})
,

(b)

evb_{i}
is the evaluation at the
i^{th}
marked point of the last component in
\mathfrak{i}_{0}

(c)

evb_{j}
is the evaluation at the first marked point on the first component in
\mathfrak{i}_{1}
,

(d)

the ordering of the boundary marked points on the clutched component is given as by

(3.2)

for
\ell_{0,h_{0}+1}=5
,
\ell_{1,1}=2
and
i=6
, while the ordering of all other boundary marked points remains the same.

(2)

For
\psi^{(1)}
we require

(a)

if
[\iota,C]
is a smooth surface in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}^{\,m}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathbb{R}P^{N})
, then the two boundary circle that are clutched in the image of
\psi^{(1)}
are adjacent in the ordering of the boundary components of
C
,

(b)

i<j

(c)

we have the following order of the boundary marked points on the nodal boundary component as shown for
i=2
and
j=6
:

(3.3)

while the ordering of all other boundary marked points remains the same; for
\psi^{(2)}
we assume that

(i)

b=a+1
,

(ii)

j=1
, i.e.,
evb_{b,j}
is the evaluation at the first marked point on
b^{th}
boundary component,

(iii)

the boundary marked points on the image of curves under
\psi^{(2)}
are ordered as in the case of
\varphi
;

(3)

\rho
preserves the ordering of all boundary marked points and the boundary component that is collapsed comes last in the ordering of boundary components.

Remark 3.4
.

We observe that if
L
is orientable, then
\mu_{L}
takes values in
2\mathbb{Z}
. Indeed, if
v\colon(C,\partial C)\to(X,L)
is a map from a surface with nonempty boundary, then

\mu_{L}([v])=w_{1}(v|_{\partial C}^{*}TL)=(v|_{\partial C})^{*}w_{1}(TL)\equiv 0\quad(\text{mod }2)

while for
\beta\in\text{im}(H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z}))
we have
\mu_{L}(\beta)=2\langle{c_{1}(TX),\beta}\rangle
.

If
L
is not relatively spin, the following statement is still true when removing any mention of orientations.

Theorem 3.5
.

Given
\vartheta\in\{\varphi,\psi^{(2)},\psi^{(1)},\rho\}
and an unobstructed auxiliary datum
\alpha
for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,0}(\beta)
, there exists an open and closed subchart
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\vartheta,\beta}
of

\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\vartheta}:=(G,\mathcal{B}(\vartheta)\times_{\mathcal{B}_{k,\ell}}\mathcal{T},\mathcal{B}(\vartheta)\times_{\mathcal{B}_{k,\ell}}\mathcal{E},\text{id}\times s)

that defines a global Kuranishi chart for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\,J,\,\beta}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L;\vartheta)
.
Suppose
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\vartheta,\beta}
is equipped with the boundary orientation from
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
and the same conditions on the ordering of the boundary component and marked points as in Assumption
3.3
hold. Then,
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\vartheta,\beta}
is oriented rel–
C^{\infty}
equivalent to the global Kuranishi chart of

1)

\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\,J,\,\beta}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L;\varphi)
given by

(\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{0},k_{0},\ell_{0}+\delta_{h_{0}}}\times_{evb_{i},evb_{1}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{1},k_{1},\ell_{1}+\delta_{1}})^{(-1)^{\dagger}},

where

(3.4)

\dagger=|\ell_{1}|(h_{0}-1)+|\ell_{0}|-\ell_{0,h_{0}}+\ell_{1,1}(\ell_{0,h_{0}}+1-i)+i-1+n(h+h_{1}(h_{0}+|\ell_{0}|+1))

reducing to
n+\ell_{1}(\ell_{0}+1-i)+i-1\;(\text{mod }2)
in the case of
(g,h)=(0,1)
.

2)

\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\,J,\,\beta}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L;\psi^{(1)})
given by

(\Delta_{L}\times_{evb_{a,i}\times evb_{a,j}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha^{\prime},k_{1},\ell+2\delta_{a}})^{(-1)^{\ddagger_{1}}},

where

(3.5)

\ddagger_{1}=(\ell_{a}-j)(j-i-1)+h-a-1+n(h-a)+\frac{n(n-1)}{2}

respectively,
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\,J,\,\beta}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L;\psi^{(2)})
given by

(\Delta_{L}\times_{evb_{a,i}\times evb_{b,1}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha^{\prime},k_{1},\ell+\delta_{a}+\delta_{b}})^{(-1)^{\ddagger_{2}}},

where

(3.6)

\ddagger_{2}=(h-a)n+|\ell|+a+(\ell_{b}+1)(\ell_{a}+1-i).

3)

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}^{\,J,\,\beta}(X,L;\rho)
given by the fibre product

(L\times_{evi_{k+1}}\mathcal{K}_{\widetilde{\alpha},k+1,\ell})^{(-1)^{\heartsuit}},

with

(3.7)

\heartsuit=(h-1-i)n-1+i+|\ell|+\delta_{1,h}\,\omega_{\mathfrak{s}}(\beta)

where the fibre product is taken over
X
, the collapsed boundary circle is in
i^{th}
position, and
\widetilde{\alpha}
is
(k+1,\ell)
-unobstructed. If
(g,h)=(0,1)
, this reduces to
n+w_{\mathfrak{s}}(\beta)
.

We will first prove the assertion about equivalence in Theorem
3.5
in the case of (
1
). The other cases are similar and left to the interested reader.
As the proof involves a lot of notation, let us describe the general strategy, which is similar to the one in Proposition
2.37
. Explicitly, we construct a doubly-thickened global Kuranishi chart that “interpolates” between
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\vartheta,\beta}
and the respective fibre product of global Kuranishi charts. Due to the isomorphism (
2.17
), the determination of the orientation sign becomes an application of
[
9
]
. See
[
56
, Proposition 2.8 and Proposition 2.11]
for the argument in the regular case when
(g,h)=(0,1)
.

Proof of equivalence in Theorem
3.5
.

We consider the clutching map

\varphi:=\varphi_{L,i}\colon\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{0},h_{0};k_{0},\ell_{0}+\delta_{h_{0}+1}}^{\,J,\,\beta_{0}}(X,L)\times_{ev_{i},ev_{1}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{1},h_{1}+1;k_{1},\ell_{1}+\delta_{1}}^{\,J,\,\beta_{1}}(X,L)\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\,J,\,\beta}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L).

Observe that

\mathcal{B}(\varphi)\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T}\cong\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta^{\prime}_{0}+\beta^{\prime}_{1}=\beta\\
m(\beta^{\prime}_{i})=m_{i}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{T}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}^{*,\,\widetilde{J}_{E},\,(m,\beta)}(E,F)}\Bigl(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{0},h_{0};k_{0}+1,\ell_{0}}^{*,\,\widetilde{J}_{E},\,(m_{0},\beta^{\prime}_{0})}(E,F)\times_{evb_{i},evb_{1}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{1},h_{1};k_{1}+1,\ell_{1}}^{*,\,\widetilde{J}_{E},\,(m_{1},\beta^{\prime}_{1})}(E,F)\Bigr),

where
m(\beta^{\prime}_{i})
is the degree of
\mathfrak{L}_{u}^{\otimes p}
for a stable map
u
in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{i}+1,\ell_{i}}^{J,\,\beta_{i}}(X,L)
3
3

3

To be precise, it is the degree of
\mathfrak{L}_{u^{\prime}}^{\otimes p}
, where
u^{\prime}
is the stabilised map given forgetting all marked points on the domain of
u
and contracting unstable components.
. Set

\widehat{\mathcal{T}}:=\mathcal{T}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}^{*,\,\widetilde{J}_{E},\,(m,\beta)}(E,F)}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{0},h_{0};k_{0}+1,\ell_{0}}^{*,\,\widetilde{J}_{E},\,(m_{0},\beta_{0})}(E,F)\times_{evb_{i},evb_{1}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{1},h_{1};k_{1}+1,\ell_{1}}^{*,\,\widetilde{J}_{E},\,(m_{1},\beta_{1})}(E,F)

and let

(3.8)

\mathcal{K}_{\varphi,\beta}:=(G,\widehat{\mathcal{T}},\widehat{\mathcal{E}}:=\mathcal{E}\times_{\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{T}_{0},\widehat{s}:=s|_{\mathcal{E}_{0}})

be the associated global Kuranishi chart.

Write
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{0},k_{i},\ell_{i}+1}=(G_{i},\mathcal{T}_{i},\mathcal{E}_{i},s_{i})
, with
\mathcal{T}_{i}
mapping homeomorphically to an open subset of

\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(F_{0}):=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{0},h_{0}+1;k_{0},\ell_{0}+\delta_{h_{0}+1}}^{*,\widetilde{J}_{E_{0}},\,(m^{\prime}_{0},\beta_{0})}(E_{0},F_{0})

and

\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(F_{1}):=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{1},h_{1};k_{1},\ell_{1}+\delta_{1}}^{*,\widetilde{J}_{E_{1}},\,(m^{\prime}_{1},\beta_{1})}(E_{1},F_{1})

so that
\mathcal{T}_{0}\times_{L}\mathcal{T}_{1}
admits a codimension-
0
embedding to
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(F_{0})\times_{L}\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(F_{1})
and a submersion to
\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{1}
, where
\mathcal{B}_{i}
is the base space of
\mathcal{K}_{i}
. Let

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{0},h_{0}+1;k_{0},\ell_{0}+\delta_{h_{0}+1}}^{\,(m^{\prime}_{0},m_{0})}(\mathbb{R}P^{N_{0}}\times\mathbb{R}P^{N})\times_{\mathbb{R}P^{N}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{1},h_{1};k_{1},\ell_{1}+\delta_{1}}^{\,(m^{\prime}_{1},m_{1})}(\mathbb{R}P^{N_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}P^{N})

be the preimage of
\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{1}\times\mathcal{B}(\varphi)
under the canonical map to the product of moduli spaces. By the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
2.37
, we deduce that
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
is a smooth orientable manifold with corners. Moreover, the forgetful maps

q\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\to\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
\widehat{q}\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\to\widehat{\mathcal{B}}

are submersions and invariant under the action by
G
, respectively
G_{0}\times G_{1}
. Denote by

\widetilde{E}_{i}:=E_{i}\times_{X}E\qquad\qquad\widetilde{F}_{i}:=F_{i}\times_{L}F

the induced complex, respectively real, vector bundles over
\mathbb{C}P^{N_{i}}\times\mathbb{C}P^{N}\times X
, respectively
\mathbb{C}P^{N_{i}}\times\mathbb{C}P^{N}\times X
. Let
\widetilde{J}_{\widetilde{E}_{i}}
be the almost complex structure on the total space of
\widetilde{E}_{i}
given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{J}_{(y_{i},y,x,e_{i},e)}^{\widetilde{E}_{i}}:=\begin{pmatrix}J_{y_{i}}^{\mathbb{C}P^{N_{i}}}&0&0&0\\
0&J_{y}^{\mathbb{C}P^{N}}&0&0\\
J_{x}&2J_{x}\langle{e_{i}}\rangle&2J_{x}\langle{e}\rangle&0\\
0&0&0&J^{\widetilde{E}_{i}}_{(y_{i},y,x)}\end{pmatrix},

using the splitting
T\widetilde{E}_{i}\cong\mathbb{C}P^{N_{i}}\times\mathbb{C}P^{N}\times X\times\widetilde{E}_{i}
. Abbreviate

\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\widetilde{E}_{i}):=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{i},h_{i}+\delta_{i,0},k_{0},\ell_{i}+\delta^{\prime}_{i}}^{*,\widetilde{J}_{\widetilde{E}_{i}},\,(m^{\prime}_{i},m_{i},\beta_{i})}(\widetilde{E}_{i}^{\prime})

where
\delta^{\prime}_{0}=\delta_{h_{0}+1}
and
\delta^{\prime}_{1}=\delta_{1}
, and let

\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}\cong\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\widetilde{E}_{0})\times_{F}\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\widetilde{E}_{1})

be subset mapping to
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
contained in the regular locus of the forgetful map to
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
. The map
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}
is given by forgetting the parameter
\gamma_{i}
that measures whether the framing
\iota_{i}
pulls back
\mathcal{O}(1)
to the reference line bundle
\mathfrak{L}_{u_{i}}
. We omit it from the notation from now on and identify
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
with
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}
.

Given
\widetilde{u}_{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\widetilde{E}_{i})
, we write
(\iota_{i},\widehat{\iota}_{i},u_{i})
for its composition with the map to
\mathbb{C}P^{N_{i}}\times\mathbb{C}P^{N}\times X
and denote by
\eta_{i}
, and
\widehat{\eta}_{i}
, the associated holomorphic section of
(\iota_{i},u_{i})^{*}E_{i}
and
(\widehat{\iota}_{i},u_{i})^{*}E
. By the definition of
\widetilde{J}_{\widetilde{E}_{i}}
,

(3.9)

\bar{\partial}_{J}u_{i}+\langle{\eta_{i}}\rangle\circ d\iota_{i}+\langle{\widehat{\eta}_{i}}\rangle\circ d\widehat{\iota}_{i}=0

on the normalisation of
C_{i}
. By abuse of notation, we write
\mathcal{E}_{i}
for the bundle
\mathcal{E}_{i}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
with fibre

(\mathcal{E}_{i})_{(\widetilde{u}_{0},\widetilde{u}_{1})}=H^{0}(C_{i},(\iota_{i},u_{i})^{*}(E_{i},F_{i}))\oplus H^{1}(C_{i},(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))\oplus\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{o}(N_{i}+1),

while
\mathcal{E}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
is defined by
\mathcal{E}_{(\widetilde{u}_{0},\widehat{u}_{1})}=V_{(\widetilde{u}_{0},\widehat{u}_{1})}\oplus\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{o}(N+1)
, where

V_{(\widetilde{u}_{0},\widehat{u}_{1})}:=\left\{(\widehat{\eta}_{0},\widehat{\eta}_{1})\in\bigoplus_{i=0,1}H^{0}(C_{i},(\widetilde{\iota}_{i},u_{i})^{*}(E,F))\mid\widehat{\eta}_{0}(x^{b}_{\ell_{0}+1})=\widetilde{\eta}_{1}(x^{b}_{1})\right\}.

Set
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}:=\mathcal{E}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{E}
and let
\widetilde{s}\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}
be the section defined analogously to the obstruction section in Construction
2.19
. The covering group is
\widetilde{G}:=G_{0}\times G_{1}\times G
acting on
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}
via its action on
E_{0},E_{1}
and
E
. Possibly after shrinking
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, the arguments of the proof of Proposition
2.22
show that

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}=(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}/\widetilde{\mathcal{B}},\widetilde{\mathcal{E}},\widetilde{s})

is a global Kuranishi chart with corners for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}^{J,\beta}(X,L;\varphi)
. The arguments of Proposition
2.37
show that
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}
is orientable and equivalent to both
\mathcal{K}_{0}\times_{L}\mathcal{K}_{1}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\varphi,\beta}
, which was defined in (
3.8
).
∎

We now discuss the difference in orientations between the respective fibre product and the boundary stratum.

Proof of Theorem
3.5
(
1
).

We may assume that
\mathcal{N}_{i}:=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{i},h_{i}+\delta_{i,0};k_{i},\ell_{i}+1}
is nonempty for
i\in\{0,1\}
, stabilising by interior marked points if necessary. Set
\mathcal{N}:=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}
. The canonical isomorphism (
2.17
) on the zero locus is also defined for any other point of the respective thickening, so we may work with the index bundles directly.

The fibre product orientation on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}^{\,J,\,\beta}(X,L;\varphi)
at a point
((u_{0},C_{0},x^{0}_{*}),(u_{1},C_{1},x^{1}_{*}))
corresponding to
(u,C,x_{*})
is given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}_{01}=(-1)^{(n+\text{ind}(D_{1}))\dim(\mathcal{N}_{0})}\mathfrak{o}_{D_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{L}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{D_{1}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{N}_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{N}_{1}}

while the boundary orientation is given by (the pullback of)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}_{\partial}=(-1)^{\text{ind}(D)}\mathfrak{o}_{D}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\partial\mathcal{N}}

Here,
D_{i}:=D\bar{\partial}_{J}(u_{i})
and
D:=D\bar{\partial}_{J}(u)
. In the language of
[
9
]
,
\mathfrak{o}_{D_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{D_{1}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{L}^{\vee}
is the
intrinsic orientation
of
\det(D)
, see also §
B.2
, while
\mathfrak{o}_{D}
is the
limiting orientation
, defined loc. cit. and induced by the orientation of the Cauchy-Riemann operator on the smoothed surface. Thus, by
[
9
, CROrient 7H3(a)]
and Assumption
3.3
,

(3.10)

\mathfrak{o}_{D}=(-1)^{nh_{1}+n}\mathfrak{o}_{D_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{D_{1}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{L}^{\vee}=\mathfrak{o}_{D_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{L}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{D_{1}}.

We observe that the first sign differs from the sign given by
[
9
, CROrient 7H3(a)]
by
(-1)^{n}
. The reason is that for the intrinsic orientation (in the regular case), we use the short exact sequence

0\to\ker(D)\to\ker(D_{0})\oplus\ker(D_{1})\xrightarrow{(\xi_{0},\xi_{1})\mapsto\xi_{1}(x_{1,1})-\xi_{0}(x_{\ell_{0},})}T_{u(\text{nd})}L\to 0

by
[
9
, Equation (7.39)]
, while for the fibre product orientation we use the orientation of
\ker(D)
obtained from

0\to\ker(D)\to\ker(D_{0})\oplus\ker(D_{1})\xrightarrow{(\xi_{0},\xi_{1})\mapsto\xi_{0}(x_{\ell_{0},})-\xi_{1}(x_{1,1})}T_{u(\text{nd})}L\to 0

by
[
37
, Convention 7.2(b)]
. Thus, we obtain an orientation difference of
(-1)^{n}
.
In summary, a first part of the sign is given by

\displaystyle\delta_{D}

\displaystyle=\text{ind}(D)+(n+\text{ind}(D_{1}))\dim(\mathcal{N}_{0})

\displaystyle\equiv nh+\mu_{L}(\beta)+(nh_{1}+\mu_{L}(\beta_{1}))(h_{0}+|\ell_{0}|+1)\quad(\text{mod }2)

(3.11)

\displaystyle\equiv n(h+h_{1}(h_{0}+|\ell_{0}|+1))\quad(\text{mod }2)

where the last equality follows from Remark
3.4
. In particular,
\delta_{D}\equiv n\;(\text{mod }2)
if
(g,h)=(0,1)
.

It remains to determine the orientation sign of the map
\varphi\colon\mathcal{N}_{0}\times\mathcal{N}_{1}\to\partial\mathcal{N}
, which we reduce to the computation of the orientation sign in the case of disks. For this recall from the proof of Proposition
3.2
that the clutching at an interior marked point is orientation-preserving (using the canonical orientation of the normal bundle of the divisor stratum). Thus, fix a curve
C_{0}\in\mathcal{N}_{0}
with a single interior node, where the boundary component that is involved in the clutching is bubbled off on a separate disk, i.e.,
C_{0}
is the clutching of the smooth curves
C_{0}^{\prime}
and
C_{0}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbb{D}
at an interior node. Write
\mathcal{N}_{0}^{\prime}
for the moduli space of stable curves containing
C_{0}^{\prime}
and similarly for
C_{0}^{\prime\prime}
. Then, there exists a canonical isomorphism

(3.12)

\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T_{C_{0}}\mathcal{N}_{0})\;\cong\;\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{C})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T_{C^{\prime}_{0}}\mathcal{N}_{0}^{\prime})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T_{C_{0}^{\prime\prime}}\mathcal{N}_{0}^{\prime\prime})

that has orientation sign
(-1)^{(\ell_{0,h_{0}}+1)(h_{0}-1)}
since we had to permute the marked points lying on the boundary of
C_{0}^{\prime\prime}
past
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\partial}_{TC_{0}^{\prime}}^{\vee})
which has degree
h_{0}-1
mod
2
. On the other hand, letting
C_{1}\subset\mathcal{N}_{1}
be a curve with a unique interior node splitting it into a disk
C^{\prime\prime}_{1}
(with boundary the first boundary component) and a curve
C^{\prime}_{1}
, the isomorphism

(3.13)

\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T_{C_{1}}\mathcal{N}_{1})\;\cong\;\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{C})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T_{C^{\prime\prime}_{1}}\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\prime\prime})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T_{C_{1}^{\prime}}\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\prime})

has orientation sign
(-1)^{|\ell_{1}|-\ell_{1,1}}
. Hence, we have a commutative diagram, omitting
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{C})
, of orientation lines
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T_{C_{0}}\mathcal{N}_{0})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T_{C_{1}}\mathcal{N}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T_{C}\mathcal{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{0})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}_{0})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}_{1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{0})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3)}

where
(i)
has orientation sign
(-1)^{\delta_{i}}
with

\displaystyle\delta_{1}\;

\displaystyle=\;(\ell_{0,h_{0}}+1)(h_{0}-1)+|\ell_{1}|-\ell_{1,1}+(h_{0}-1)+|\ell_{0}|-\ell_{0,h_{0}}

\displaystyle=\;\ell_{0,h_{0}}+|\ell_{1}|-\ell_{1,1}+|\ell_{0}|

given by the signs of (
3.12
) and (
3.13
) and the sign from permuting
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})
past
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{0}^{\prime})
, while

\delta_{2}=\ell_{1,1}(\ell_{0,h_{0}}+1-i)+i-1

due to the sign computation of
[
55
, Proposition 2.8]
. There is a shift because they label the first marked point by
0
.
Finally,

\delta_{3}=(\ell_{0,h_{0}}+\ell_{1,1})(h_{0}-1)+(|\ell_{1}|-\ell_{1,1})h_{0}

due to Proposition
3.2
in the case where the target manifold is a point. Then,

(3.14)

\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\varphi)=(-1)^{|\ell_{1}|(h_{0}-1)+|\ell_{0}|-\ell_{0,h_{0}}+\ell_{1,1}(\ell_{0,h_{0}}+1-i)+i-1}

since

\displaystyle\delta\;

\displaystyle=\;\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}

\displaystyle\equiv\;\ell_{0,h_{0}}h_{0}+|\ell_{1}|-\ell_{1,1}+|\ell_{0}|+\ell_{1,1}(\ell_{0,h_{0}}+1-i)+i-1+(\ell_{0,h_{0}}+\ell_{1,1})(h_{0}-1)+(|\ell_{1}|-\ell_{1,1})h_{0}

\displaystyle\equiv\;(|\ell_{1}|-\ell_{1,1})(h_{0}-1)+|\ell_{0}|+\ell_{1,1}(h_{0}-1)

\displaystyle\equiv\;|\ell_{1}|(h_{0}-1)+|\ell_{0}|-\ell_{0,h_{0}}+\ell_{1,1}(\ell_{0,h_{0}}+1-i)+i-1\mod{2}.

If
(g,h)=(0,1)
, this reduces to
\mathfrak{o}(\varphi)=(-1)^{\ell_{1}(\ell_{0}+1-i)+i-1}
, recovering the sign in
[
55
, Proposition 2.8]
for the case
n=0
. Note that they have
i
instead of
i-1
, due to the reason that they index the marked points by
\{0,\dots,\ell_{i}\}
. The final sign is now
(-1)^{\delta_{D}+\delta}
, where
\delta_{D}
was computed in (
3.11
).
∎

We now turn to the proof of the orientation sign in the case of the nodes obtained from self-clutching.

Proof of Theorem
3.5
(
2
).

By adding interior marked points, we may assume that
(g,h,k,\ell)
lies in the stable range. Set
\mathcal{N}:=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h,k,\ell}
and let

\mathcal{N}_{1}:=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h-1;k,\ell+2\delta_{a}}\qquad\quad\text{and}\qquad\quad\mathcal{N}_{2}:=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-1,h+1;k,\ell+\delta_{a}+\delta_{b}}.

The fibre product orientation on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L,\psi^{(1)})
at a point
(u^{\prime},C^{\prime},x^{\prime}_{*})
mapping to
(u,C,x_{*})\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L)
is given by

\mathfrak{o}_{1}:=\mathfrak{o}_{L}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{D^{\prime}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{N}_{1}},

while the boundary orientation is given by

\mathfrak{o}_{\psi^{(1)}}=(-1)^{\text{ind}(D)}\mathfrak{o}_{D}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\partial\mathcal{N}}.

By Proposition
B.11
and the difference between the intrinsic orientation and the fibre product orientation explained below Equation (
3.10
), we have

(3.15)

\mathfrak{o}_{L}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{D^{\prime}}=(-1)^{n(h-a+1)+\text{ind}(D^{\prime})n}\mathfrak{o}_{D}=(-1)^{n\,a}\mathfrak{o}_{D}.

To compare the orientation sign
\psi^{(1)}\colon\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\to\partial\mathcal{N}
, we employ a similar argument as in the previous proof, reducing to the case where the gluing happens on a disk. For this, let
C\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
be a curve with a two interior nodes, splitting into
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{1}
, carrying the first
a-1
boundary components of
C
,
C^{\prime\prime}=\mathbb{D}
, defining an element of
\mathcal{N}_{2}
, where the
a
th boundary component of
C
is the boundary of
C^{\prime\prime}
, and
C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{3}
carrying the last boundary components of
C
. The canonical isomorphism

(3.16)

\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime})\cong\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{C})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{2})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{3})

has orientation sign
(-1)^{\ell_{a}(a-1)+(\ell_{a+1}+\dots+\ell_{h-1})a}
by Proposition
3.2
. Then we have as before a commutative square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{2})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{23})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3)}

where
\mathcal{N}_{23}
is the target of the clutching map on
\mathcal{N}_{2}\times\mathcal{N}_{3}
and
(i)
has orientation sign
(-1)^{\delta_{i}}
with

\displaystyle\delta_{1}\;

\displaystyle=\;\ell_{a}(a-1)+(\ell_{a+1}+\dots+\ell_{h-1})a+\dim(\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\prime})

given by the orientation sign of (
3.16
) and the sign from permuting
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})
past
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\prime})
, while

\delta_{2}=(\ell_{a}-j)(j-i-1)

by Proposition
B.11
(which gives
0
since here the rank of the vector bundle is
1
) and the reordering of the marked points.
Finally,

\delta_{3}=\ell_{a}(a-1)+(\ell_{a+1}+\dots+\ell_{h-1})(a+1)

due to Proposition
3.2
again, noting that in
\mathcal{N}
we have one boundary component more. Thus, the orientation sign of
\psi^{(1)}\colon\mathcal{N}_{1}\to\partial\mathcal{N}
is

(3.17)

\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\psi^{(1)})=(-1)^{(\ell_{a}-j)(j-i-1)+h-a-1}.

The case of an (H2) boundary node, reduces essentially to the case of (
3
) by the definition of the intrinsic orientation, cf. §
B.2
. Write
D^{\prime}
for the Cauchy–Riemann operator of an element
u^{\prime}
of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L,\psi^{(1)})
and
D
for the Cauchy–Riemann operator of its image in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L)
. Then, fibre product orientation at this point is given by

\mathfrak{o}_{2}:=\mathfrak{o}_{L}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{D^{\prime}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{N}_{1}},

while the boundary orientation is given by

\mathfrak{o}_{\psi^{(2)}}=(-1)^{\text{ind}(D)}\mathfrak{o}_{D}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\partial\mathcal{N}}.

Invoking
[
9
, CROrient 7H3(a)]
and the same reasoning as for Equation (
3.10
), we obtain for
b=a+1
that

\mathfrak{o}_{L}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{D^{\prime}}=(-1)^{\text{ind}(D^{\prime})n}\mathfrak{o}_{D^{\prime}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{L}^{\vee}=(-1)^{\text{ind}(D^{\prime})n+(h-a)n+n}\mathfrak{o}_{D}=(-1)^{na}\mathfrak{o}_{D}.

To compute the sign of
\psi^{(2)}\colon\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}\to\partial\mathcal{N}
, we consider a curve
C\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}
with three interior nodes splitting
C
into
C_{1}
carrying the first
a-1
boundary components,
C_{2}=\mathbb{D}
carrying the
a
th boundary component,
C_{3}
carrying the boundary component labelled by
b=a+1
and
C_{4}
carrying the remaining boundary components. Write
\mathcal{N}_{i}
for the moduli space containing
C_{i}
. The isomorphism

(3.18)

\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{C})^{\otimes 3}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{2})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{3})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{4})\;\cong\;\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime})

has sign
(-1)^{(\ell_{a}+1)(a-1)+(\ell_{b}+1)(b-1)+(\ell_{b+1}+\dots+\ell_{h})b}
by Proposition
3.2
. As before, we have a commutative square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{2})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{3})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{4})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{23})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{4})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3)}

where
(i)
has orientation sign
(-1)^{\delta_{i}}
with

\displaystyle\delta_{1}\;

\displaystyle=\;(\ell_{a}+1)(a-1)+(\ell_{b}+1)(b-1)+(\ell_{b+1}+\dots+\ell_{h+1})b+\dim(\mathcal{N}_{1})

given by the orientation sign of (
3.16
) and the sign from permuting
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})
past
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{1})
, while

\delta_{2}=\ell_{b}(\ell_{a}+1-i)+i-1

by
[
55
, Proposition 2.8]
, which we may apply because
C_{2}
and
C_{3}
are disks. As above, the formula is slightly different because we start labelling by
1
instead of
0
as they do. The last exponent is

\delta_{3}=(\ell_{a}+\ell_{b})(a-1)+(\ell_{b+1}+\dots+\ell_{h+1})a

due to Proposition
3.2
again, noting that in
\mathcal{N}
we have one boundary component less. Thus, the orientation sign of
\psi^{(2)}\colon\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}\to\partial\mathcal{N}
is

(3.19)

\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\psi^{(2)})=(-1)^{|\ell|+a+(\ell_{b}+1)(\ell_{a}+1-i)}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.5
(
3
).

Let
(u^{\prime},C^{\prime})
be an element of
L\times_{X}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h-1;k+1,\ell^{\prime}}^{\,J,\,\beta}(X,L)
with image
(u,C)
in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\,J,\,\beta}_{g,h;k,\ell}(X,L)
and let
D^{\prime}
and
D
be their respective Cauchy–Riemann operator. Let
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h-1;k+1,\ell^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{N}=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}
, where
\ell=(\ell^{\prime},0)
. We have to compare

\mathfrak{o}_{\rho}:=\mathfrak{o}_{L}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{D^{\prime}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{N}^{\prime}},

with the boundary orientation given by

\mathfrak{o}_{\partial}=(-1)^{\text{ind}(D)}\mathfrak{o}_{D}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\partial\mathcal{N}}.

By Lemma
2.34
(
3
), we may assume
i=h
. By
[
9
, Corollary 7.3]
, we have

\mathfrak{o}_{D}=(-1)^{\omega_{\mathfrak{s}}(\beta)}\mathfrak{o}_{D^{\prime}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{L}^{\vee}=(-1)^{\omega_{\mathfrak{s}}(\beta)+nh+n\mu_{L}(\beta)}\mathfrak{o}_{L}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{D^{\prime}}.

if
h=1
. If
h>1
, then by
[
9
, Corollary A.13]
and
[
58
, Proposition 3.1.7]
, the stable trivialisation of the pullback of
TL
to the boundary of the surface is determined by its restriction to the smooth boundary circles. Thus, we have

\mathfrak{o}_{D}=(-1)^{\text{ind}(D)n}\mathfrak{o}_{L}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\widetilde{D}}=(-1)^{(h-1)n}\mathfrak{o}_{L}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\widetilde{D}}

by the definition of the intrinsic orientation. We now turn to the orientation sign of the clutching map
\rho\colon\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\to\partial\mathcal{N}
on the level of moduli space of stable curves. Picking up a sign of
(-1)^{h-i}
, we may assume
i=h
. Let
C\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
be a curve with a unique interior node, splitting
C
into
C_{1}\in\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\prime}
and
C_{2}=S^{2}
lying in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{2}
, where
C_{2}
carries the
k+1
st marked point and one other interior marked point (since we may assume without loss of generality that
k>0
). Then the isomorphism
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{C})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\prime})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\prime})\cong\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime})
is orientation-preserving and the isomorphism induced by
\rho
factors as
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\prime})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\prime})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{N}_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3)}

where
\mathcal{N}_{2}
is the moduli space of disks obtained by smoothing of the boundary node and we omit
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{C})
. Then,
(1)
has orientation sign
(-1)^{h-1+|\ell|}
,
(2)
has orientation sign
(-1)
by
[
55
]
and
(3)
has orientation sign
1
by Proposition
3.2
. Hence,

(3.20)

\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\rho)=(-1)^{i+|\ell|}

and the claimed sign follows.
∎

4.
Cubical cobordisms and Thom systems

If a compact space admits an oriented global Kuranishi chart without boundary, it has a virtual fundamental class. In our case, where the boundary is nonempty, one has to work on the level of chains. A literal virtual fundamental chain requires the choice of a
Thom form
, that is, a representative of the Thom class of the obstruction bundle, as well as a fundamental chain of the thickening.
Instead of a virtual fundamental chain, we construct operations based on integration, which can be considered as ‘virtual pushforwards’ of differential forms. These pushforwards need to be constructed compatibly in order to define strict algebraic structures. Instead of making the auxiliary choices necessary for the construction of global Kuranishi charts coherently, we use the equivalence statements of Theorem
3.5
and Proposition
3.2
to interpolate between different choices.

4.1.
Cubical cobordisms

Throughout, let
(X,\omega)
be a closed symplectic manifold and
L\subset X
an embedded Lagrangian with a relative spin structure
(V,\mathfrak{s})
. Define

\mathcal{A}:=\left\{(\beta,g,h,k,\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{h})\mid\beta\in H_{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z}),\,\omega(\beta)\geq 0,\;g,h,k,\ell_{i}\geq 0,\;\beta\in H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})\text{ if }h=0\right\}.

Fixing
J\in\mathcal{J}_{\tau}(X,\omega)
, we abbreviate

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}}(X,L):=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}^{\,J,\,\beta}(X,L)

for
\mathsf{a}=(\beta,g,h,k,\ell)\in\mathcal{A}
and denote by
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(X,L)
the collection
\{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}}(X,L)\}_{\mathsf{a}\in\mathcal{A}}
.
Given a stable map graph
\Gamma
of an open stable map, we can associate to each vertex
v\in V(\Gamma)
a unique element
\mathsf{a}_{v}\in\mathcal{A}
. Denote by

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}(X,L)\subset\prod\limits_{v\in V(\Gamma)}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}_{v}}(X,L)}

the associated fibre product. Its quotient admits an embedding

(4.1)

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}(X,L)/\text{Aut}(\Gamma)\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}}(X,L)

onto a (virtual) boundary or divisor stratum
\partial_{\Gamma}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}}(X,L)
of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}}(X,L)
. The stabilisation map induces the map

(4.2)

\text{st}\colon\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}(X,L)\,\to\,\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma^{\text{st}}}

where
\Gamma^{\text{st}}
is the graph obtained by forgetting the degrees associated to vertices of
\Gamma
and collapsing any vertices that become unstable.

Given
\mathsf{a}=(\beta,g,h,k,\ell)
in
\mathcal{A}
, write
\mathsf{a}_{0}:=(\beta,g,h,0,0)
for the tuple without marked points. Let
\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}_{0}}=\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{\mathsf{a}}}
be the global Kuranishi chart of Proposition
2.22
(
1
) and let
\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}}
be the pullback of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}_{0}}
as in Proposition
2.31
. Given a stable map graph
\Gamma
, we can associate to each vertex
v\in V(\Gamma)
an element
\mathsf{a}_{v}\in\mathcal{A}
and thus obtain a derived orbifold chart

(4.3)

\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma}\subset\prod\limits_{v\in V(\Gamma)}{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}_{v}}}

for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}(X,L)
, where we have used the ‘stabilised’ evaluation maps from Lemma
2.32

except in the case where
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}_{v}}(X,L)
is a moduli space of constant genus zero maps with at most one outgoing marked point, which lies on the boundary if the curves have boundary
. Therefore, the fibre product is a well-defined global Kuranishi chart.

Theorem 4.1
.

Given a choice of unobstructed auxiliary datum
\alpha_{\mathsf{a}}
for each
\mathsf{a}\in\mathcal{A}
, there exists for each stable map graph
\Gamma
a
smooth
global Kuranishi chart
{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}
for
I^{E(\Gamma)}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}(X,L)
so that the following holds

1)

{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\mathsf{a}}}=\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}}
for any
\mathsf{a}\in\mathcal{A}
.

2)

{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}
is oriented.

3)

for any edge
e
of
\Gamma
we have a local smooth embedding

(4.4)

{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}|_{\{t_{e}=0\}}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim\,}}{{\longrightarrow}}\;(\pm 1)\,\partial_{\Gamma}{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma_{e}}}

of degree

d_{1}(\Gamma,\Gamma_{e})=|\{\phi\in\text{Aut}(\Gamma)\mid f\circ\phi=f\},

where
f\colon\Gamma\to\Gamma_{e}
is the underlying contraction of graphs.

4)

The restriction to the other boundary face induces a smooth embedding

(4.5)

{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}|_{\{t_{e}=1\}}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim\,}}{{\longrightarrow}}\;(\pm 1)\,{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma_{1}}}\times_{Y}{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma_{2}}}

where
\Gamma_{1}*_{e}\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma
and
Y=X
or
L
, depending on whether
e
is an interior or a boundary edge.

5)

{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}
admits a smooth submersive lift
ev_{\Gamma}\colon{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}\to X^{k}\times L^{\ell}
of the evaluation map on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}(X,L)
and the (local) embeddings of (
4.4
) and (
4.5
) intertwine these lifts.

Definition 4.2
.

We call the system
\{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}\}_{\Gamma_{*}}
a
cubical cobordism
for
\{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}}(X,L)\}_{\mathsf{a}\in\mathcal{A}}
.

Proof.

The proof relies on the observation that the construction of the double thickening, e.g. in the proof of Proposition
2.37
or Theorem
3.5
(
3
), can be adapted to yield a global Kuranishi chart
\mathcal{K}
with a submersion
\mathcal{T}\to[0,1]
so that for
i=0,1
, the restriction
\mathcal{K}|_{\{i\}}
admits a map

(4.6)

\mathcal{K}|_{\{i\}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim\;}}{{\to}}\mathcal{K}_{i}

i.e., a morphism that is given by a stabilisation and a group enlargement as in Definition
2.36
. Since we stabilised the evaluation map to a relative submersion (Lemma
2.32
), we can take that morphism to preserve the evaluation map.
Recall that, roughly, the tuples of the double thickening of the proof of Theorem
3.5
(
3.14
) are tuples

(\varphi,\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},u,C=C_{1}\vee C_{2},\eta,\eta_{1},\eta_{2}s),

where

•

C_{1},C_{2}
are nodal Riemann surfaces clutched at marked points to form the curve
C

•

u\colon C\to X
is a smooth stable map

•

\varphi\colon C\to\mathbb{C}P^{N}
is a framing of
C
with
\deg(\varphi)=\deg(\mathfrak{L}_{u}^{\otimes p})
,

•

\varphi_{i}\colon C_{i}\to\mathbb{C}P^{N_{i}}
is a framing of
C_{i}
with
\deg(\varphi_{i})=\deg(\mathfrak{L}_{u|_{C_{i}}}^{\otimes p_{i}})
for
i=1,2

•

\eta\in H^{0}(C,(\varphi,u)^{*}E_{k})
and
\eta_{i}\in H^{0}(C_{i},(\varphi_{i},u|_{C_{i}})^{*}E_{k_{i}})
are perturbation terms so that

(4.7)

\bar{\partial}_{J}(u|_{C_{i}})+\langle{\eta}\rangle\circ d\varphi|_{C_{i}}+\langle{\eta_{i}}\rangle\circ d\varphi_{i}=0

for
i=1,2
.

Instead, one can consider tuples
(t,\varphi,\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},u,C=C_{1}\vee C_{2},\eta,\eta_{1},\eta_{2}s)
, where
t\in I=[0,1]
and
(\varphi,u,\eta_{0},\eta_{1})
are the same data as before except that they satisfy the following perturbed Cauchy-Riemann equation

(4.8)

\bar{\partial}_{J}(u|_{C_{i}})+t\,\langle{\eta}\rangle\circ d\varphi|_{C_{i}}+(1-t)\,\langle{\eta_{i}}\rangle\circ d\varphi_{i}=0

for
i=1,2
instead of (
4.7
). This yields the cubical cobordism in the case where the underlying stable map graph
\Gamma
has a single edge.

To define the cubical cobordism
\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}
of global Kuranishi charts for an arbitrary graph
\Gamma
, we define for a contraction
\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
of stable map graphs the function

(4.9)

\chi_{\Gamma^{\prime}}=\chi_{\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}}\colon I^{E(\Gamma)}\rightarrow[0,1]

by

(4.10)

\chi_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(t)=\prod_{e\in E(\Gamma^{\prime})}t_{e}\prod_{e\notin E(\Gamma^{\prime})}(1-t_{e})

using that there exists a canonical embedding
E(\Gamma^{\prime})\hookrightarrow E(\Gamma)
.
We record a few properties of these functions here that will be used later.

(
\chi
1)

If
e
is contracted by
\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma
, then
\chi_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
vanishes on
\{t_{e}=1\}
.

(
\chi
2)

If the map
\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
contracts the edge
e
, then
\chi_{\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}}|_{\{t_{e}=0\}}=\chi_{\Gamma_{e}\to\Gamma^{\prime}}
.

(
\chi
3)

We have for any
\Gamma
that
\sum\limits_{\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}}{\chi_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}\equiv 1
.

(
\chi
4)

If
\Gamma
is the clutching of
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
along the edge
e_{*}
, then we have for any contraction
\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
, which does not contract
e_{*}
, that
\Gamma^{\prime}
is the clutching of contractions
\Gamma_{1}\to\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}\to\Gamma_{2}^{\prime}
and we have for
t\in I^{E(\Gamma)}
with
t_{e_{*}}=1
that

\chi_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(t)=\chi_{\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}}((t_{e})_{e\in E(\Gamma_{1})})\;\chi_{\Gamma_{2}^{\prime}}((t_{e})_{e\in E(\Gamma_{2})}).

The third property follows from induction; the other properties are immediate from the definition. We can now start with the definition of the global Kuranishi charts
\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}
. The construction is divided into three steps.

Step 1:
We first define a rel–
C^{\infty}
global Kuranishi chart
\widehat{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}
for
[0,1]^{E(\Gamma)}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}(X,L)
for each suitable stable map graph
\Gamma
so that all claims of the proposition hold but with relative smoothness instead of smoothness and with the caveat that we do get a ‘uniform’ evaluation map since we stabilise them in a very specific way. Fix a suitable
\Gamma
and define the base space
\widehat{\mathcal{B}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}
to be simply the base space
\mathcal{B}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}
defined above. The thickening
\widehat{\mathcal{T}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}
consists of tuples

(4.11)

(t,\varphi=(\varphi_{\Gamma^{\prime}})_{\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}},u=(u_{v})_{v\in V(\Gamma)},(\gamma_{\Gamma^{\prime}})_{\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}},\eta=(\eta_{\Gamma^{\prime}})_{\Gamma},(\xi_{v,e})_{(v,e)\in F(\Gamma)})

where

•

t\in[0,1]^{E(\Gamma)}
,

•

\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}
, so that
\varphi_{\Gamma}=(\varphi_{v})_{v\in V(\Gamma)}

•

u_{v}\colon C_{v}:=\mathcal{C}_{\varphi_{v}}\to X
is a smooth map of degree
\beta_{v}

•

\xi_{v,e}\in T_{u_{v}(z_{v,e})}Y_{e}
is a tangent vector, where
Y_{e}=X
if
e
is an interior edge and
Y_{e}=L
otherwise,

•

\gamma_{\Gamma^{\prime}}=(\gamma_{\Gamma^{\prime},v^{\prime}})_{v^{\prime}\in V(\Gamma^{\prime})}
is a sequence of elements
\gamma_{\Gamma^{\prime},v^{\prime}}\in H^{1}(C_{v^{\prime}},\mathcal{O}_{C_{v^{\prime}}})
satisfying

[\varphi_{v}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{N_{v}}}(-1)]\otimes[\mathfrak{L}_{u_{v}}^{\otimes p_{v}}]=\exp(\gamma_{\Gamma^{\prime},v})

with
\varphi_{\Gamma^{\prime},v^{\prime}}
,

•

\eta_{\Gamma^{\prime}}=(\eta_{\Gamma^{\prime},v})_{v\in V(\Gamma)}
is a sequence of perturbations with

\eta_{\Gamma^{\prime},v}\in H^{0}(C_{v},(\varphi_{\Gamma^{\prime}}|_{C_{v}},u_{v})^{*}E_{\Gamma^{\prime},f(v)})

where
f\colon\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
is the underlying contraction,

so that

i)

for any edge
e=\{v,v^{\prime}\}
of
\Gamma
we have

\exp_{u_{v}(z_{v,e})}(t_{e}\xi_{v,e})=\exp_{u_{v^{\prime}}(z_{v^{\prime},e})}(t_{e}\xi_{v^{\prime},e})

and

(4.12)

d\exp_{u_{v}(z_{v,e})}(t_{e}\xi_{v,e})\circ\eta_{\Gamma^{\prime},v}(z_{v,e})=d\exp_{u_{v^{\prime}}(z_{v^{\prime},e})}(t_{e}\xi_{v^{\prime},e})\circ\eta_{\Gamma^{\prime},v^{\prime}}(z_{v^{\prime},e})

ii)

any
u_{v}
satisfies the perturbed Cauchy–Riemann equation

(4.13)

\bar{\partial}_{J}\,u_{v}+\sum\limits_{\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}}{\chi_{\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma}(t)\langle{\eta_{\Gamma^{\prime},v}}\rangle\circ d\varphi_{\Gamma^{\prime}}|_{C_{v}}}=0,

where
\chi_{\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}}
is defined in Equation (
4.10
), and the perturbed operator

\displaystyle D\bar{\partial}_{J}(u_{v})+\sum\limits_{\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}}{\chi_{\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma}(t)\langle{\cdot}\rangle\circ d\varphi_{\Gamma^{\prime}}|_{C_{v}}}

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(C_{v},u_{v}^{*}TX)\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}}H^{0}(C_{v},(\varphi_{\Gamma^{\prime}}|_{C_{v}},u_{v})^{*}E_{v^{\prime}})\to\Omega^{0,1}(C_{v},u_{v}^{*}(TX,TL))

is surjective when restricted to
C^{\infty}(C_{v},u_{v}^{*}TX)_{D_{v}}\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}}H^{0}(C_{v},(\varphi_{\Gamma^{\prime}}|_{C_{v}},u_{v})^{*}E_{v^{\prime}})
.

iii)

for any
f\colon\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
, the evaluation map

\displaystyle\bigoplus\limits_{v\in V(\Gamma)}H^{0}(C_{v},(\varphi_{\Gamma^{\prime}}|_{C_{v}},u_{v})^{*}E_{v^{\prime}})\to\Omega^{0,1}(C_{v},u_{v}^{*}(TX,TL))\ \to\ \bigoplus\limits_{e\in E(\Gamma)}T_{\exp_{u_{v}(z_{v,e})}(t\xi_{v,e})}Y_{e}

\displaystyle\widehat{\eta}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\ \mapsto\ (d\exp_{u_{v}(z_{v,e})}(t_{e}\xi_{v,e})(\widehat{\eta}_{\Gamma^{\prime},v}(z_{v,e}))-d\exp_{u_{v^{\prime}}(z_{v^{\prime},e})}(t_{e}\xi_{v^{\prime},e})(\widehat{\eta}_{\Gamma^{\prime},v^{\prime}}(z_{v^{\prime},e})))_{e\in E(\Gamma)}

is surjective.

We define the obstruction bundle
\widehat{\mathcal{E}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}\to\widehat{\mathcal{T}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}
to have fibre

\bigoplus\limits_{\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}}\Bigl(\bigoplus\limits_{v^{\prime}\in V(\Gamma)}H^{1}(C_{v^{\prime}},(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))\oplus\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{o}(N_{v^{\prime}}+1)\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{v\in f^{-1}(v^{\prime})}H^{0}(C_{v},(\varphi_{\Gamma^{\prime}}|_{C_{v}},u_{v})^{*}E_{v^{\prime}})\Bigr)

over the element (
4.11
). We let
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{\Gamma}
be the canonical section. The covering group agrees with the covering group of
\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}
. Since regularity is an open condition, the canonical map
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}^{-1}(0)\to I^{E(\Gamma)}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}(X,L)
induces an isomorphism on the quotient. Applying the mollification procedure of Lemma
2.26
inductively to the obstruction sections, we thus obtain a system of rel–
C^{\infty}
global Kuranishi charts
\widehat{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}
satisfying the first four properties. We define the evaluation map
\widehat{e}v_{\Gamma}\colon\widehat{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}\to X^{k}\times L^{\ell}
by stabilising the natural evaluation maps
except
in the case where
\Gamma
has a constant vertex of genus zero with at most one boundary component and at most one outgoing marked point (which is a boundary marked point in the case of discs). Thus, the system
\left\{\widehat{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}\right\}_{\Gamma}
satisfies all the claimed properties except for smoothness and Property (
5
).

Step 2:
We now equip the cubical cobordisms constructed in the first step with smooth structures. Since the relative smoothing theory discussed in §
C.3
only yields a smooth structure, which is unique up to concordance (Lemma
C.33
), we have to add a bigger outer collar to also interpolate between possibly different choices of smooth structures. These outer collars will also allow us to interpolate between the stabilised and non-stabilised evaluation maps on the global Kuranishi charts for moduli spaces of constant discs and spheres with at most one outgoing marked point (which is on the boundary in the case of discs). To do so, shrink
\widehat{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}
down to the interval
[\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4}]
, rescaling the functions
\chi_{\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}}
appropriately. Then, use Theorem
C.29
to lift the rel–
C^{\infty}
structure on
\widehat{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}\times Y_{\Gamma}
, where
Y_{\Gamma}
denotes the target of the evaluation map, to a smooth structure on
\widehat{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}
. This ensures the evaluation maps are smooth. If
\Gamma
represents a constant map of genus
0
with at most one outgoing marked point, we can just use the usual smooth structure on the trivial global Kuranishi chart
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}
.

Since one can lift a
G
-smoothing through a rel–
C^{\infty}
covering, we obtain for any stable map graph
\Gamma
and any vertex
v
of
[0,1]^{E(\Gamma)}
a relative
G
-smoothing. Using Lemma
C.33
inductively, one can extend these over the strips
[0,\frac{1}{4}]
and
[\frac{3}{4},1]
to obtain the charts
{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}
of the claim. We illustrate this step for the case of
\Gamma=(v_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{1}}}{{-}}v_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{2}}}{{-}}v_{3})
with the following figure over
[0,1]^{2}
.

Figure 1.
Constructing the smooth structure on
{\mathcal{K}^{\square}_{\Gamma}}

Here
\Gamma_{1}
is obtained from
\Gamma
by collapsing the edge
e_{2}
and similarly for
\Gamma_{2}
, while
\mathsf{a}
is the graph obtained from
\Gamma
by collapsing all edges.
We start off with smooth structures on the blue parts which are given by Theorem
C.29
, and are, respectively, pulled back from the respective boundary stratum. Then, we apply Lemma
C.33
a first time to extend the smooth structures from the blue parts (after stabilisation) over the yellow lines and rectangles. Finally, we apply Lemma
C.33
a second time to extend the smooth structure over the white squares. We also use these ‘additional’ ‘collars to interpolate between the stabilisation of the evaluation map and its non-stabilised version on moduli spaces of constant stable maps of genus
0
.

Step 3:
We will modify the construction of the smooth structure on the cubical cobordisms so that the forgetful maps are smooth. For graphs which have a minimal number of marked points to be stable, i.e. those graphs for which the only components with marked points are constant curves of genus
0
or
1
, construct the smooth structure as in Step 2. Then, for any other stable map graph
\Gamma
, let
\Gamma_{0}
be the graph obtained by forgetting all marked points, except those that are needed on constant components. Define the smooth structure on
{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}^{\prime}
by the fibre product

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma_{0}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma_{0}}.}

The evaluation maps on
{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}^{\prime}
might not be smooth. Thus, we define
{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}
to be the stabilisation of
{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}^{\prime}
, and take the smooth structure to be the one induced by Lemma
C.34
. Inducting over all stable map graphs gives the result.
∎

Definition 4.3
.

Let the map
\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow I^{E(\Gamma^{\text{st}})}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\Gamma^{\text{st}}}
be given as follows.
The map
\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\Gamma^{\text{st}}}
is the usual map stabilising the domain of the map.
The map
\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow I^{E(\Gamma^{\text{st}})}
is given by the composition
\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow I^{E(\Gamma)}\rightarrow I^{E(\Gamma^{\text{st}})}
, where the map
I^{E(\Gamma)}\rightarrow I^{E(\Gamma^{\text{st}})}
is defined as follows.
The stable graph
\Gamma^{\text{st}}
is obtained from
\Gamma
by contracting certain edges or by combining edges together (in the case of interior vertices being forgotten).
Suppose
e_{1},\dots,e_{k}\in\Gamma
get combined into the edge
e\in\Gamma^{\text{st}}
. Then the map
I^{E(\Gamma)}\rightarrow I^{E(\Gamma^{\text{st}})}
sends

(t_{e_{1}},\dots,t_{e_{k}})\mapsto t_{e}=1-\prod_{i}(1-t_{e_{i}}).

Moreover it forgets the parameters associated to contracted edges.

Example 4.4
.

Consider the stabilisation of a stable map graph shown in the figure:

All vertices denote Riemann surfaces with one boundary component, and the number in the vertex denotes the genus. The stabilisation map contracts the edge
e_{1}
, and the edges
e_{2}
and
e_{3}
get combined to form the edge
e
.
The map
I^{E(\Gamma)}\rightarrow I^{E(\Gamma^{\text{st}})}
is thus given by

(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})\mapsto t=1-(1-t_{2})(1-t_{3}).

Lemma 4.5
.

Given stable map graphs
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
with marked points labelled by
y\sqcup y^{\prime}
, respectively
y^{\prime}\sqcup y^{\prime\prime}
, there exists a smooth embedding

(4.14)

\psi\,\colon\,{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma_{2}}}{\times}_{X^{y^{\prime}_{c}}\times L^{y^{\prime}_{o}}}{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma_{1}}}\,\to\,{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}},

where
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}*_{y^{\prime}}\Gamma_{2}
, covering the clutching map

I^{E(\Gamma_{1}^{\text{st}})}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma_{1}^{\text{st}}}\times I^{E(\Gamma_{2}^{\text{st}})}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\Gamma_{2}^{\text{st}}}\,\to\,I^{E(\Gamma^{\text{st}})}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma^{\text{st}}}

defined by the clutching map on the moduli spaces of stable curves and by
(t_{1},t_{2})\mapsto(t_{1},t_{2},1)
on the cubes.

Proof.

Replace
{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}}
and
{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}
by the group-enlarged moduli space, where we allow for more framings (see e.g., the first step in the proof of Proposition
2.37
). Then, the clutching map is defined by simply combining the data coming from the two factors. By construction, this is a well-defined smooth embedding.
∎

4.2.
Thom forms

Recall that the virtual fundamental class of an oriented derived orbifold chart
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E},s)
for
Z
is given by

(4.15)

\langle{\gamma,[Z]^{\text{vir}}}\rangle\,=\,\int_{\mathcal{T}}\gamma\cdot s^{*}\text{Th}_{\mathcal{E}},

where
\text{Th}_{\mathcal{E}}
is the Thom class of
\mathcal{E}
. This virtual fundamental class is unique and only depends on the equivalence class of the chart. In the case with boundary, there is no such unique invariant, similar to how manifolds with boundary have a (non-unique) fundamental chain. In our setting, we will also have to choose a representative of the Thom form
\text{Th}_{\mathcal{E}}
. Since these representatives will be ubiquitous in the next section, we make the following definition.

Definition 4.6
.

A
Thom form
is a representative of the Thom class of an orbi-bundle. A Thom form
\eta
of a derived orbifold chart
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E},s)
is a Thom form of its obstruction bundle. We call it
proper
if
s^{*}\eta
has compact support.

Remark 4.7
.

Given a Thom form
\eta
for a derived orbifold chart
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E},s)
and a neighbourhood
U\subset\mathcal{T}
of
s^{-1}(0)
, we can pull back
\eta
to
\mathcal{E}|_{U}
to obtain a Thom form for
\mathcal{K}|_{U}
. However, this operation does not preserve the properness of Thom forms.

We first show that (proper) Thom forms can be chosen to behave compatibly under stabilisations. To this end, let
(W,p^{*}\mathcal{E}\oplus p^{*}W,\widetilde{s}:=p^{*}s\oplus\Delta_{W})
be obtained from
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E},s)
by stabilisation along the orbi-bundle
p:W\rightarrow\mathcal{T}
. We consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}\mathcal{E}\oplus p^{*}W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}\oplus W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

where the map
\Delta_{W}(e,w)=(w,e,w)
for
(e,w)\in\mathcal{E}\oplus W
is induced by the diagonal map.

Lemma 4.8
.

Given Thom forms
\eta_{\mathcal{E}}
and
\eta_{W}
for
\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}
and
W\rightarrow\mathcal{T}
, the form

\widetilde{\eta}:=\pi_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}\eta_{\mathcal{E}}\wedge\pi_{W}^{*}\eta_{W}

is a Thom form for
p^{*}\mathcal{E}\oplus p^{*}W
. It satisfies
p_{*}\widetilde{s}^{*}\widetilde{\eta}=s^{*}\eta_{\mathcal{E}}
. If
\eta_{\mathcal{E}}
is proper, then so is
\widetilde{\eta}
.

Proof.

First note that

\widetilde{s}^{*}\widetilde{\eta}=(p^{*}s)^{*}\pi_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}\eta_{\mathcal{E}}\wedge\Delta_{W}^{*}\pi_{W}^{*}\eta_{W}=p^{*}s^{*}\eta_{\mathcal{E}}\wedge\eta_{W},

whence it has support contained in the support of
\eta_{W}
. Thus
p_{*}(\widetilde{s}^{*}\widetilde{\eta})
is well defined. Next observe that
\widetilde{\eta}
is closed, and for
w\in W
, we have:

\int_{(p^{*}\mathcal{E}\oplus p^{*}W)_{w}}\pi_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}\eta_{\mathcal{E}}\wedge\pi_{W}^{*}\eta_{W}=\left(\int_{\mathcal{E}_{p(w)}}\eta_{\mathcal{E}}\right)\left(\int_{W_{p(w)}}\eta_{W}\right)=1,

deducing that
\widetilde{\eta}
is indeed a Thom form. Finally, we compute

p_{*}\widetilde{s}^{*}\widetilde{\eta}=p_{*}(p^{*}s^{*}\eta_{\mathcal{E}}\wedge\eta_{W})=s^{*}\eta_{\mathcal{E}}\wedge p_{*}\eta_{W}=s^{*}\eta_{\mathcal{E}}.

As the support of
\widetilde{s}^{*}\widetilde{\eta}
agrees with the support of
s^{*}\eta_{\mathcal{E}}
, the last assertion is immediate.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

If
\widetilde{\eta}
is a Thom form for
p^{*}\mathcal{E}\oplus p^{*}W\xrightarrow{\pi_{W}}W
,
the form

\eta_{\mathcal{E}}:=(p_{\mathcal{E}})_{*}\Delta_{W}^{*}\widetilde{\eta}

is a Thom form for
\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}
, satisfying
p_{*}\widetilde{s}^{*}\widetilde{\eta}=s^{*}\eta_{\mathcal{E}}
. If
\widetilde{\eta}
is proper, then so is
\eta_{\mathcal{E}}
and if
\widetilde{\eta}=\pi_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}\widehat{\eta}_{\mathcal{E}}\wedge\pi_{W}^{*}\eta_{W}
, then
\eta_{\mathcal{E}}=\widehat{\eta}_{\mathcal{E}}
.

Proof.

Since
\Delta_{W}
is a section of
p^{*}(\mathcal{E}\oplus W)
,
\Delta_{W}^{*}\widetilde{\eta}
has compact vertical support on
\mathcal{E}\oplus W
. Hence,
(p_{\mathcal{E}})_{*}\Delta_{W}^{*}\widetilde{\eta}
is well defined. Moreover,
\eta_{\mathcal{E}}
is closed as
\widetilde{\eta}
is closed and the map
p_{\mathcal{E}}
has no vertical boundary. The final property of a Thom form can be checked on cohomology, i.e., we need that
[\eta_{\mathcal{E}}]
is the Thom class for the zero section of
\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{W}
. As
\Delta_{W}
intersects the zero section of
p^{*}(\mathcal{E}\oplus W)
transversely in
\mathcal{T}
, it pullbacks back
[\widetilde{\eta}]
to the Thom class of
\mathcal{T}
inside
\mathcal{E}\oplus W
. It follows from the Thom isomorphism theorem that
(p_{\mathcal{E}})_{*}\Delta_{W}^{*}\widetilde{\eta}
is a Thom form for
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{T}
. The second assertion is a consequence of the projection formula,
[
55
, Proposition 2.1(4)]
, while the third assertion is due to the fact that the support of
s^{*}\eta
is contained in the support of
\widetilde{s}^{*}\widetilde{\eta}
. The last assertion follows from the fact that
\pi_{\mathcal{E}}\Delta_{W}=p_{\mathcal{E}}
and
\pi_{W}\Delta_{W}=p_{W}
and
[
55
, Proposition 2.1]
.
∎

Definition 4.10
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a global Kuranishi chart and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
its stabilisation along a vector bundle
p\colon\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{T}
. Let
P\colon\mathcal{E}^{\prime}=p^{*}\mathcal{E}\oplus p^{*}\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{E}
be the canonical projection. Then, we call a Thom form
\eta
of
\mathcal{K}

compatible
with a Thom form
\eta^{\prime}
for
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
if
(p_{\epsilon})_{*}\Delta_{W}^{*}\eta^{\prime}
.

Remark 4.11
.

The upshot of having compatible Thom forms is the following. Suppose we are given maps
g^{\pm}:W\rightarrow Y^{\pm}
that factor through maps
f^{\pm}:\mathcal{T}\rightarrow Y^{\pm}
under the projection
p:W\rightarrow\mathcal{T}
. Then, for compatible Thom forms
\eta_{\mathcal{E}}
and
\widetilde{\eta}
, we have

f^{+}_{*}((f^{-})^{*}\alpha\wedge\eta_{\mathcal{E}})=g^{+}_{*}((g^{-})^{*}\alpha\wedge\widetilde{\eta})

for any
\alpha\in\Omega^{*}(Y^{-})
.

Proposition 4.12
.

Given a system of (enhanced) cubical cobordisms
\{\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}\}_{\Gamma}
and a choice of Thom form
\eta_{\mathsf{a}}
for
\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}}
so that
s_{\mathsf{a}}^{*}\eta_{\mathsf{a}}
is compactly supported, there exists a system of Thom forms
\eta_{\Gamma}
for
\mathcal{E}_{\Gamma}
with the following properties.

1)

The pullback
s_{\Gamma}^{*}\eta_{\Gamma}
is compactly supported in
\mathcal{T}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}
.

2)

If
\Gamma=\mathsf{a}
, then
\eta_{\Gamma}=\eta_{\mathsf{a}}
.

3)

It is compatible with boundary restrictions. More precisely, for any edge
e
of
\Gamma
, we have that

(4.16)

\eta_{\Gamma}|_{\{t_{e}=0\}}\sim\eta_{\Gamma_{e}}|_{\partial_{\Gamma}\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma_{e}}},

and

(4.17)

\eta_{\Gamma}|_{\{t_{e}=1\}}\sim\eta_{\Gamma_{1}}\times\eta_{\Gamma_{2}}|_{\mathcal{T}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma_{1}}\times_{Y}\mathcal{T}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma_{2}}}

if
e
separates
\Gamma
into
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
with
Y=X
or
L
depending on
e
, and

(4.18)

\eta_{\Gamma}|_{\{t_{e}=1\}}\sim\eta_{\Gamma\setminus e}|_{Y\times_{Y^{2}}\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma\setminus e}}

if
e
is a non-separating node and
\Gamma\setminus e
is the graph given by cutting the edge
e
. Finally, if the edge corresponds to a collapsed boundary circle, then

(4.19)

\eta_{\Gamma}\mid_{\{t_{e}=1\}}\sim\eta_{\Gamma\setminus e}|_{L\times_{X}\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma\setminus e}}.

Proof.

We construct the system of Thom forms by a double induction. The outer induction is over the partial order on
\mathcal{A}
and the inner induction, for each
\mathsf{a}\in\mathcal{A}
, is on
\#E(\Gamma)
for stable map graphs
\Gamma
contracting to
\mathsf{a}
. The point is that by the time we consider
\Gamma
we will have already chosen Thom forms for the boundaries appearing in (
4.16
)-(
4.19
). Thus, we can apply Proposition
D.1
to conclude.
Explicitly, for
\Gamma
with
\#E(\Gamma)=0
, we are given a choice of Thom form already. Suppose, thus, we have constructed the system of Thom forms for all
\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
with
\mathsf{a}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}<\mathsf{a}
and a Thom form for each
\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
with
\#E(\Gamma^{\prime})<\#E(\Gamma)
. By construction
\mathcal{E}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}
decomposes as a direct sum

\mathcal{E}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}=\bigoplus_{\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}\to\mathsf{a}}\mathcal{E}_{\Gamma^{\prime}},

where we abuse notation and write
\mathcal{E}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
for the summands appearing in equation (
4.1
). For each
\mathcal{E}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
, we obtain a Thom form over certain faces of
I^{E(\Gamma)}
by stabilising the already obtained Thom forms for the adjacent cubes, or in the case of faces with parameters
t=1
, by stabilising the Thom forms appearing in (
4.17
)-(
4.19
). By construction, these agree on lower dimensional faces, so we can use Appendix
D
to obtain a Thom form for
\mathcal{E}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
over the whole
\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}
. Doing this for all
\Gamma^{\prime}
and taking the wedge product yields the required Thom form.
∎

4.3.
Counting graphs correctly

To each cubical cobordism
\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}
, where we now omit the tilde from the notation, we can associate rel–
C^{\infty}
maps

ev^{\pm}_{\Gamma}\colon\mathcal{T}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}\to X^{k^{\pm}}\times L^{\ell^{\pm}}

with
ev^{+}_{\Gamma}
being a submersion. This allows us to define the so-called
\mathfrak{q}
-operations

\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma}\colon\Omega^{*}(X^{k^{-}}\times L^{\ell^{-}})\,\to\,\Omega^{*}(X^{k^{+}}\times L^{\ell^{+}}).

using the pullback and pushforward along the evaluation maps. In
[
29
]
, we will construct the morphisms of the DMFT of Theorem
1.3
by summing over the operations associated to suitable stable map graphs
\Gamma
. To this end we consider three categories of graphs.

Definition 4.13
.

An
(open-closed) prestable graph

\Gamma
consists of a finite set of vertices
V(\Gamma)
and a finite set of half-edges
H(\Gamma)
together with the data of

•

an involution
\iota\colon H(\Gamma)\to H(\Gamma)

•

a source map
j\colon H(\Gamma)\to V(\Gamma)

•

a genus function
g\colon V(\Gamma)\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

•

a finite (ordered) set of boundary circles
S_{v}
for each
v\in V(\Gamma)
allowing us to decompose
V(\Gamma)
into closed vertices
V_{c}(\Gamma)=\{v\mid S_{v}=\emptyset\}
and open ones
V_{o}(\Gamma)=\{v\mid S_{v}\neq\emptyset\}
,

•

a decomposition
H(\Gamma)=H_{i}(\Gamma)\sqcup H_{b}(\Gamma)
into interior and boundary half-edges together with a function

(4.20)

\widetilde{j}\colon H_{b}(\Gamma)\to\bigcup\limits_{v}{S_{v}}

so that
\widetilde{j}(h)\in S_{j(h)}
for each
h\in H_{b}(\Gamma)
; we set

S_{v}^{\mathrm{o}}\coloneqq\{s\in S_{v}\mid\widetilde{j}^{-1}(\{s\})=\emptyset\}

for
v\in V_{o}(\Gamma)
;

•

an ordering of the boundary half-edges
\widetilde{j}^{-1}(s)
for each
s\in\bigcup\limits_{v}{S_{v}}
,

•

an orientation (or sign)
\epsilon(e,v)\in\{\pm\}
for each
h\in H(\Gamma)
that is either fixed by
\iota
or with
j(v)
stable and
h
being part of a path to another stable vertex.

We call
\Gamma

stable
if each vertex
v\in V(\Gamma)
satisfies the stability condition

(4.21)

2g(v)+|S_{v}|+2H_{v,i}+H_{v,b}>2

or if
(g(v),h(v))=0
and
v
has one interior half-edge that is not fixed by
\iota
. We call such unstable vertices
collapsed boundaries
.
4
4

4

Such unstable vertices do model collapsed boundaries.

Definition 4.14
.

A
morphism of prestable graphs

f\colon\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
is the data of functions

(1)

f_{V}\colon V(\Gamma)\to V(\Gamma^{\prime})
,

(2)

f_{S}\colon\bigcup\limits_{v}{S_{v}}\to\bigcup\limits_{v^{\prime}}{S^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}}
,

(3)

f_{H}\colon H(\Gamma^{\prime})\to H(\Gamma)
respecting the decomposition into interior and boundary half-edges,

and satisfying

•

f_{V}
is surjective and
f_{H}
is injective,

•

f_{H}
intertwines the involutions
\iota
and
\iota^{\prime}
,

•

we have

j^{\prime}=f_{V}\circ j\circ f_{H}\qquad\qquad\widetilde{j}^{\prime}=f_{S}\circ\widetilde{j}\circ f_{H}

on
H(\Gamma^{\prime})
,

•

the genus map satisfies

g^{\prime}(v^{\prime})=b_{1}(\Gamma_{v^{\prime}})+\sum\limits_{v\in f^{-1}(v^{\prime})}{g(v)}

for any
v^{\prime}\in V(\Gamma^{\prime})
, where
