\Gamma_{v^{\prime}}
is the graph that is contracted onto
v^{\prime}
by
f
and
b_{1}
is the first Betti number;

•

for any
v^{\prime}\in V(\Gamma^{\prime})
the map
\bigcup\limits_{v\in f^{-1}(v^{\prime})}{S_{v}}\to S^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}
is surjective and
f(S^{\mathrm{o}}_{v,o})\subset S^{\mathrm{o}}_{f(v),o}
,

•

f_{H}
preserves the ordering of the boundary half-edges as described by Figure
3.2
and Figure
3.3
up to cyclic ordering if all boundary half-edges
h
satisfy
\sigma(h)\neq h
.

If
f_{H}
preserves the orientation sign of those
h\in H_{b}(\Gamma^{\prime})
, which have one, we call it a
orientation-preserving
. It is an
elementary contraction
if
H(\Gamma)\setminus\text{im}(f_{H})
consists of a single
\iota
-orbit. We write
\mathscr{P}\mathscr{G}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})
for the space of morphisms from
\Gamma
to
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\mathscr{P}\mathscr{G}^{+}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})
for the subset of orientation-preserving morphisms.

Remark 4.15
.

In particular, we have two groups of automorphisms of
\Gamma
:
\text{Aut}(\Gamma)
and
\text{Aut}^{+}(\Gamma)
.

Up to the last point, this is simply an abstract formulation of the clutching maps between different moduli spaces. It may be helpful to compare this definition to Lemma
3.1
. We define the category
\mathscr{P}\mathscr{G}
of prestable graphs to with objects prestable graphs,
whose only unstable vertices are of type
(g,h)=(0,1)
, and morphisms of prestable graphs. It contains the full subcategory
\mathscr{S}\mathscr{G}
of stable graphs.

Definition 4.16
.

A
stable map graph

\Gamma=(\Gamma^{ps},\beta)

in
(X,L)
consists of a prestable graph
\Gamma^{ps}
together with functions
V_{c}(\Gamma^{ps})\to H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
and
V_{o}(\Gamma^{ps})\to H_{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z})
so that for any unstable vertex
v
the associated homology class
\beta_{v}
satisfies
\omega(\beta_{v})>0
. We define the
total degree
of
\Gamma
to be

\deg(\Gamma)\coloneqq\sum\limits_{v\in V(\Gamma)}{\beta_{v}},

where we implicitly apply the map
H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z})
if
\Gamma
has both open and closed vertices.

A morphism of stable map graphs
f\colon(\Gamma,\beta)\to(\Gamma^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
is a morphism of underlying prestable graphs so that
\beta^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}=\sum\limits_{f(v)=v^{\prime}}{\beta_{v}}
, where we apply the map
H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z})
if necessary. This yields a third category
\mathscr{S}\mathscr{G}_{m}
of graphs.

Lemma 4.17
.

There exist functors

\mathscr{S}\mathscr{G}_{m}\,\xrightarrow{(-)^{ps}}\,\mathscr{P}\mathscr{G}\,\xrightarrow{(-)^{\text{st}}}\mathscr{S}\mathscr{G}

where the first functor is faithful, while the second one is neither full nor faithful in general.∎

From now on, we fix subsets
\text{SG}\subset\text{obj}(\mathscr{S}\mathscr{G})
and
\text{PG}\subset\text{obj}(\mathscr{P}\mathscr{G})
, so that they contain exactly one representative for each orientation-preserving isomorphism class of graph in the respective category. We define

\text{SG}_{m}\coloneqq\left\{\Gamma\in\text{obj}(\mathscr{S}\mathscr{G}_{m})\mid\Gamma^{ps}\in\text{PG}\right\}.

All graphs that appear in the remainder of this and in the next section will implicitly be taken to be elements of these sets. Given a stable map graph
\Gamma=(\Gamma^{ps},\beta)
, define

\mathscr{P}\mathscr{S}^{+}(\Gamma)\coloneqq\left\{(\Gamma^{ps},\beta^{\prime})\in\text{SG}_{m}\mid(\Gamma^{ps},\beta^{\prime})\cong_{+}\Gamma\right\}

where
\cong_{+}
means that they are related by an orientation-preserving isomorphism.
An easy verification shows that

(4.22)

|\mathscr{P}\mathscr{S}^{+}(\Gamma)|=\frac{|\text{Aut}^{+}(\Gamma^{ps})|}{|\text{Aut}^{+}(\Gamma)|}.

Example 4.18
.

Consider the (closed) stable graph
\Gamma^{ps}
with two vertices
v_{1},v_{2}
of genus
2
, connected by a single edge whose two associated half-edges carry both the sign
+
. If we take
\Gamma=(\Gamma^{ps},\beta)
where
\beta_{v_{1}}\neq\beta_{v_{2}}
, then,
\mathscr{P}\mathscr{S}^{+}(\Gamma)=\{\beta,\beta^{\prime}\}
, where
\beta^{\prime}_{v_{1}}=\beta_{v_{2}}
and
\beta^{\prime}_{v_{2}}=\beta_{v_{1}}
. If one half-edge carries the sign
+
and the other the sign
-
, then
\mathscr{P}\mathscr{S}(\Gamma)=\{\beta,\beta^{\prime}\}
as before but
\mathscr{P}\mathscr{S}^{+}(\Gamma)=\{\beta\}
.

Remark 4.19
.

For any prestable graph
\Gamma
with stabilisation
\overline{\Gamma}
the inclusion
\text{Aut}^{+}(\Gamma/\overline{\Gamma})\hookrightarrow\text{Aut}(\Gamma/\overline{\Gamma})
is an isomorphism. Indeed, any
\phi\in\text{Aut}(\Gamma/\overline{\Gamma})
is given by permuting unstable chains of discs ending in unstable leaves and which are attached to the same stable vertex. By our definition of half-edge orientations, any such automorphism is automatically orientation-preserving.

Define for a prestable graph
\Gamma
and a total degree
\beta
the derived orbifold

(4.23)

\mathcal{K}(\Gamma^{ps},\beta)\coloneqq\scalebox{1.5}{[}\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}ps(\Gamma)=\Gamma^{ps}\\
\deg(\Gamma)=\beta\end{subarray}}\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}/\text{Aut}^{+}(\Gamma^{ps}/\overline{\Gamma})\,\scalebox{1.5}{]}

and for a stable graph
\overline{\Gamma}

(4.24)

\mathcal{K}(\overline{\Gamma},\beta)\,\coloneqq\bigsqcup\limits_{\text{st}(\Gamma^{ps})=\overline{\Gamma}}\mathcal{K}(\Gamma^{ps},\beta)^{\sqcup A^{ps}_{\Gamma}},

where

(4.25)

A_{\Gamma^{ps}}\coloneqq\text{Aut}^{+}(\overline{\Gamma})/\text{im}(\text{Aut}^{+}(\Gamma^{ps})\to\text{Aut}^{+}(\overline{\Gamma}))

is the set of automorphisms of
\overline{\Gamma}
that cannot be lifted to the prestable graph
\Gamma^{ps}
.

Remark 4.20
.

We take this additional disjoint union because we are undercounting prestable graphs by taking only one representative per isomorphism class. However, a prestable graph
\Gamma^{ps}
with stabilisation
\overline{\Gamma}
can admit multiple contractions
\Gamma^{ps}\to\overline{\Gamma}
. Ignoring degrees, the associated clutching maps between moduli spaces (of stable maps) encode different boundary strata if and only if the underlying contractions are
not
related by an automorphism of
\Gamma
. In summary, since

A_{\Gamma^{ps}}\cong\mathscr{P}\mathscr{G}^{+}(\Gamma^{ps},\overline{\Gamma})/\text{Aut}^{+}(\Gamma^{ps})

we have
|A_{\Gamma^{ps}}|
-many such boundary strata of
\mathcal{K}_{\overline{\Gamma},\beta}
.

Notation 4.21
.

Given an edge
e\in E(\overline{\Gamma})
, we let
\mathcal{K}(\overline{\Gamma},\beta)_{|\text{st}^{-1}(e)|=1}\subset\mathcal{K}(\overline{\Gamma},\beta)
be the disjoint union indexed by the prestable graphs
\Gamma^{ps}
where the stabilisation map
\Gamma^{ps}\to\overline{\Gamma}
maps a single edge to the edge
e
. By abuse of notation, we denote said edge by
e
as well.

Lemma 4.22
.

Suppose
\overline{f}\colon\overline{\Gamma}\to\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime}
is an elementary contraction. Then, for any prestable graph
{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{ps}
, any total degree
\beta
and the unique edge
e
contracted by
\overline{f}
, the diagram

(4.26)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}(\overline{\Gamma},\beta)_{|\text{st}^{-1}(e)|=1}|_{t_{e}=0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}(\overline{\Gamma^{\prime}},\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{E(\overline{\Gamma})}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\overline{\Gamma}}|_{t_{e}=0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{E(\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime})}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime}}}

is a Cartesian square of derived orbifolds up to codimension
1
in
\mathcal{K}(\overline{\Gamma},\beta)_{|\text{st}^{-1}(e)|=1}|_{t_{e}=0}
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the claim separately for each
\mathcal{K}({\Gamma^{\prime}}^{ps},\beta)^{\sqcup A_{\Gamma^{ps}}}
, where
{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{ps}
is a prestable graph with stabilisation
\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime}
. Suppose first that
{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{ps}
is stable itself. In this case, the automorphism groups in (
4.24
) and
A_{\Gamma^{ps}}
are trivial. It suffices to show the claim for a single global Kuranishi chart
\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\subset\mathcal{K}(\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\beta)
. Moreover, because the retraction
\overline{f}\colon\overline{\Gamma}\to\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime}
is fixed, any
\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}\subset\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}(\overline{\Gamma},\beta)
is mapped to a unique such
\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
. Thus, we can reduce the claim further to showing that

(4.27)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Gamma\\
\overline{f}(\Gamma)=\Gamma^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}|_{\{t_{e}=0\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{E(\overline{\Gamma})}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\overline{\Gamma}}|_{t_{e}=0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{E(\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime})}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime}}}

is Cartesian up to strata of codimension at least
1
. For this, let
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
be the subchart whose thickening is given by curves whose stabilisation is of type ‘at most
\overline{\Gamma}
’ and let
\mathcal{V}\subset\bigsqcup\limits\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}|_{\{t_{e}=0\}}
be the preimage of
\mathcal{U}
under the canonical clutching map. Then,

\mathcal{U}\,=\,\bigsqcup\limits_{[f]}\,\mathcal{U}_{[f]},

where
f\colon\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
is a retraction covering
\overline{f}
and
[f]=[\widehat{f}]
if there exists an isomorphism
\phi\colon\Gamma\to\widehat{\Gamma}
with
\widehat{f}\circ\phi=f
. Since the underlying prestable graph of
\Gamma
and
\widehat{\Gamma}
is simply
\overline{\Gamma}
, we must have
\phi\in\text{Aut}(\overline{\Gamma})_{\overline{f}}
. Thus, either
\phi
is the identity, or
\phi(\Gamma)\in\mathscr{P}\mathscr{S}(\Gamma)\setminus\{\Gamma\}
and the graph appears in the indexing set of the left top corner of (
4.27
). Thus, the collection of trees we consider classifies the top-dimensional (possibly non-connected) strata of
\mathcal{U}
. The remainder of the proof (of the stable case) is similar to the proof of
[
27
, Lemma 7.3]
. By construction of the cubical cobordisms, it suffices to show that the square

(4.28)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigsqcup\limits_{\Gamma}\mathcal{T}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma,\mathcal{V}}|_{\{t_{e}=0\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\mathcal{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{E(\overline{\Gamma})}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\overline{\Gamma}}|_{\{t_{e}=0\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{E(\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime})}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{e}}

of orbifolds is Cartesian up to strata of codimension
1
, where
\widehat{\mathcal{T}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is the locus of curves
(\varphi,u,\eta)
in the thickening of
\mathcal{V}
with no perturbation from
\mathcal{E}_{\Gamma}
and where
\lambda_{\Gamma}(\varphi_{\Gamma},u)=0
. This orbifold can be identified with an orbifold with the same base space as
\mathcal{T}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
and thus also
\mathcal{T}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\mathcal{U}}
. Note that the assumption on
\mathcal{U}
ensures that the restriction
\mathcal{T}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\mathcal{U}}\to I^{E(\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime})}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime}}
is a submersion. Let
\mathcal{W}
be the fibre product of the square. There exists a canonical map
\widehat{\mathcal{T}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}}_{\mathcal{V}}\to\mathcal{W}
. We can define the inverse morphism
\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{U}
in the category of orbifolds by

((C_{v},\mathfrak{j}_{v},D_{v})_{v\in V(\Gamma)},\phi,(\varphi,u,\eta))\,\to\,(\widehat{\varphi},\widehat{u},\widehat{\eta}),

where
\phi=(\phi_{v^{\prime}})_{v^{\prime}\in V(\Gamma^{\prime})}
is a collection of automorphisms of the clutched surfaces
C^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}
obtained from
(C_{v})_{v}
, and

\widehat{\varphi}_{v}=(\varphi_{f(v)}\circ\phi_{f(v)})|_{C_{v}}\qquad\quad\widehat{u}_{v}=(u_{f(v)}\circ\phi_{f(v)})|_{C_{v}}\qquad\quad\widehat{\eta}_{v}=(\eta_{f(v)}\circ\phi_{f(v)})|_{C_{v}}

for
v\in V(\Gamma)
. By (
4.12
) and (
4.13
) and the reduction we made just below (
4.28
), this is a well-defined map. Note that the composition
\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{U}
does not necessarily map
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}
to
\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\Gamma}
, but possibly to
\mathcal{K}^{\lx@scalerel@obj{\square}}_{\widehat{\Gamma}}
, where
\widehat{\Gamma}
and
\Gamma
are related by an automorphism of
\overline{\Gamma}
preserving the contraction
f\colon\overline{\Gamma}\to\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime}
. In particular, the image of
(\varphi,u,\eta)
under
\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{U}
is related to
(\varphi,u,\eta)
exactly by an automorphism of the stabilisation of its domain.

Suppose now
{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{ps}
is an unstable graph with stabilisation
\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime}
. Let
E^{\prime}\subset E({\Gamma^{\prime}}^{ps})
be the subset of edges that are either combined or vanish under the stabilisation procedure. By the observation about the top-dimensional strata of
\mathcal{U}
in the previous step, it suffices to show the claim restricted to
\mathcal{K}({\Gamma^{\prime}}^{ps},\beta)|_{{t_{E}^{\prime}=0}}
with a similar restriction on the left hand side. Then, we can expand (
4.26
) to

(4.29)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{st}(\Gamma^{ps})=\overline{\Gamma}\\
\text{st}^{-1}(e)=e\\
\Gamma^{ps}_{e}\cong_{+}{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{ps}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{K}(\Gamma^{ps},\beta)^{\sqcup A_{\Gamma^{ps}}}|_{\{t_{e\sqcup E^{\prime}}=0\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}({\Gamma^{\prime}}^{ps},\beta)^{\sqcup A_{{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{ps}}}|_{\{t_{E^{\prime}}=0\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}(\overline{\Gamma},\beta)|_{\{t_{e}=0\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}(\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{E(\overline{\Gamma})}|_{\{t_{e}=0\}}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\overline{\Gamma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{E(\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime})}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime}}}

We observe first that
\text{Aut}({\Gamma^{\prime}}^{ps}/\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime})
can at most permute chains of unstable vertices terminating in leaves of
{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{ps}
(and attached to the same stable vertex); any internal chain of unstable vertices is connected to two stable vertices that cannot be permuted by any such automorphism. In particular, these are exactly the automorphisms that fix the contraction map
{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{ps}\to\Gamma^{\prime}_{E^{\prime}}
(the choice of which is irrelevant). The same is true for the top vertical map on the left. Thus, we have reduced the claim to determining the intersection of boundary strata in
\mathcal{K}(\Gamma^{\prime},\beta)
. If two boundary strata do not agree, their intersection is a stratum of codimension
2
. In the case where they agree, we may conclude by Remark
4.20
.
If
{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{ps}
has internal unstable chains, we can reduce as above to the case where it has no external unstable chains.
∎

Lemma 4.23
.

Suppose
\overline{\Gamma}
is a stable graph obtained from
\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime}
by forgetting an incoming marked point. Then, the square

(4.30)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}(\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}(\overline{\Gamma},\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{E(\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime})}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{E(\overline{\Gamma})}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\overline{\Gamma}}}

is a Cartesian square of derived orbifolds up to codimension
1
strata for any total degree
\beta
.

Proof.

Since we stabilise incoming marked points if and only if the associated vertex has positive degree or negative Euler characteristic, there is no difference between which marked points of
\mathcal{K}(\Gamma^{\prime},\beta)
and of
\mathcal{K}(\Gamma,\beta)
are stabilised. Thus, the argument is a straightforward adaption of the proof of Lemma
4.22
.
∎

Appendix A
Moduli spaces of regular open stable maps

A.1.
Representability

Suppose
(X,\omega)
is a symplectic manifold and
L\subset X
be a Lagrangian submanifold. Fix an
\omega
-tame almost structure
J
and
\beta\in H_{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z})
. Note that we do not assume
X
or
L
to be compact. Fix a family
\pi\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{V}
of nodal curves with
h
(ordered) boundary components and a linear map

P\colon E\to C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{o}}\times X,\Omega^{0,1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{o}}/\mathcal{V}}\boxtimes_{\mathbb{C}}TX)

defined on a finite-dimensional vector space, where
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{o}}\subset\mathcal{C}
is the complement of the critical points of
\pi
and the vertical boundary
\partial^{v}\mathcal{C}
. Given these data, we define the functor

\mathfrak{M}:=\mathfrak{M}^{J}_{P}(\pi,\beta)\colon(C^{\infty}/\cdot)^{\text{op}}\to\text{Set}

by letting
\mathfrak{M}(Y/S)
be the set of diagrams

(A.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}

where

(1)

\varphi
is continuous,

(2)

both squares are cartesian,

(3)

F\colon\mathcal{C}_{Y}/\mathcal{C}_{S}\to X/*
is of class rel–
C^{\infty}
with
F(\partial\mathcal{C}_{Y})\subset L
,

(4)

for each
y\in Y
, the restriction
u_{y}:=F|_{C_{y}}
is a smooth stable map with boundary on
L
representing
\beta
, where
C_{y}
is the fibre of
\mathcal{C}_{Y}\to Y
over
y
. It satisfies

(A.2)

\bar{\partial}_{J}u_{y}+P(w(y))=0

and the (perturbed) linearised Cauchy-Riemann operator at
u_{y}
is surjective.

Remark A.1
.

Here
C^{\infty}/\cdot
is the category of rel–
C^{\infty}
manifolds, defined originally in
[
57
]
. We briefly recall the relevant notions in §
C.1
and apologise for the non-chronological ordering of the appendices.

In order to show this representability result, we adapt the strategy of
[
57
]
to our setting. A key ingredient is the following representability criterion.

Proposition A.2
.

[
57
, Proposition 2.16]
Suppose
X/S
is an
S
-space and
\mathfrak{F}\colon(C^{\infty}/\cdot)^{\text{op}}\to\text{Set}
a functor with the following properties.

(1)

S
represents the functor
\mathfrak{F}_{\text{base}}(T)=\mathfrak{F}(\emptyset/T)
.

(2)

There exists an open cover
\{V_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
S
so that
p^{-1}(V_{i})
represents the functor

(\mathfrak{F}|_{V_{i}})^{\text{top}}:Y\mapsto\mathfrak{F}|_{V_{i}}(Y/Y).

(3)

\mathfrak{F}
is representable near each
x\in X
, that is, for each
x\in X
there exists a neighbourhood
V\subset S
of
p(x)
and
U\subset p^{-1}(V)
of
x
such that
\mathfrak{F}^{U}|_{V}
is representable.

In this case,
X/S
admits a rel–
C^{\infty}
structure with which it represents
\mathfrak{F}
.

Remark A.3
.

Property (
2
) implies that
X
represents
\mathfrak{F}^{\text{top}}
by (the non-relative version of)
[
57
, Lemma 2.15(b)(1)]

Our candidate to represent
\mathfrak{M}
is given by

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h}^{J}(\pi,\beta)^{\text{reg}}:=\left\{(v,u,e)\mid v\in V,\;u\colon(C_{v},\partial C_{v})\to(X,L)\;\bar{\partial}_{J}u+P(e)|_{\mathcal{C}_{v}}=0,\,\eqref{eq:perturbed-pde-representability}\text{ regular}\right\}

considered as a
\mathcal{V}
-space via the obvious forgetful map
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h}^{J}(\pi,\beta)^{\text{reg}}\to\mathcal{V}
. We endow it with the Gromov–Hausdorff metric on the associated graphs in
\mathcal{C}\times X
.

Theorem A.4
.

\mathfrak{M}
is representable by
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h}^{J}(\pi,\beta)^{\text{reg}}/\mathcal{V}
.

The first two conditions of Proposition
A.2
follow from the same argument as in
[
57
, Lemma 3.15, Lemma 3.16]
. It remains to show local representability. The arguments will be very similar to
[
57
]
, the only difference is that we have to deal with boundary nodes.

Remark A.5
.

It might seem strange that, given the existence of codimension
1
strata, we can represent
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h}^{J}(\pi,\beta)^{\text{reg}}
as a rel–
C^{\infty}
manifold over
\mathcal{V}
. The reason for this is that the corners come from deformations of the domain and not of the map. Indeed, our thickening will be a rel–
C^{\infty}
manifold over a base space that
is
a manifold with corners.

A.2.
Local models and universal deformations

In this section we discuss universal deformations of nodal curves with boundary. The classical model for an interior node of a nodal surface is given by

\mathcal{N}_{i}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\mid z_{1}z_{2}=0\}

with deformation given by

\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{i}=\{(t,z)\in\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}\mid z_{1}z_{2}=t\}.

In the case of boundary nodes, we have several cases to distinguish, depending on the type of boundary node. Recall that we have the following types:

•

(E) a boundary circle collapses to a point - it is forgotten when passing to the normalisation

•

(H1) two boundary components intersect - the nodes appears as two points on a boundary circle of the normalised surface

•

(H2) a boundary circle self-intersects and the resulting node is non-separating - the node corresponds to two points on two
distinct
boundary circles of
one
component of the normalisation.

•

(H3) a boundary circle self-intersects and the node is separating - it corresponds to two points on two
distinct
boundary circles of two
distinct
components of the normalisation.

The different symbol for the first singularity follows from the fact that it is an elliptic singularity, while the other singularities are hyperbolic and are locally indistinguishable. We have the local models

\mathcal{N}_{E}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\mid z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2}=0,\,\Im(z_{1})\geq 0,\,\Im(z_{2})\geq 0\}.

with boundary given by
\mathcal{N}_{E}\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}
and with deformation given by

\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{E}=\{(t,z)\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}\mid z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2}=t,\,\Im(z_{1})\geq 0,\,\Im(z_{2})\geq 0\}

mapping to
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
by projection onto the first factor. Note how the boundary over the fibre
t>0
is simply the circle of radius
\sqrt{t}
. If
t<0
, a crosscap appears:

Meanwhile, the local model for a boundary node of type
(H1)
,
(H2)
, or
(H3)
is

\mathcal{N}_{H}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\mid z_{1}^{2}-z_{2}^{2}=0,\,\Im(z_{1})\geq 0,\,\Im(z_{2})\geq 0\},

whose deformation is given by

\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{H}=\{(t,z)\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}\mid z_{1}^{2}-z_{2}^{2}=t,\,\Im(z_{1})\geq 0,\,\Im(z_{2})\geq 0\}.

In this case, passing to
t<0
, a Möbius strip appears:

Suppose
(C,\partial C)
is a stable nodal Riemann surface of genus
g
with
h
ordered boundary components. Denote by
N_{i}
and
N_{b}=N_{b}^{E}\sqcup N_{b}^{H}
the set of interior as well as (elliptic and hyperbolic) boundary nodes. For
x\in N_{i}
fix two cylindrical ends

\psi^{\pm}_{x}\colon[0,\infty)\times S^{1}\to C

and for
y\in N_{b}^{H}
fix two strip-like ends:

\vartheta^{\pm}_{y}\colon[0,\infty)\times[0,1]\to C

with
\vartheta_{y}^{\pm}([0,\infty)\times\{1\})\subset\partial C
. Finally, for each elliptic node
y\in N^{E}_{b}
we fix one cylindrical end

\vartheta_{y}\colon[0,\infty)\times S^{1}\to C.

We choose these ends so that they have disjoint images. We denote by the same symbol the obvious continuous extension to
[0,\infty]\times S^{1}
respectively
[0,\infty]\times[0,1]
.

A.3.
Pre-gluing and anti-gluing

Fix thus
(v_{0},u_{0},e_{0})\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h}^{J}(\pi,\beta)^{\text{reg}}
with
(C_{0},\mathfrak{j}_{0})
the fibre over
v_{0}
with its induced complex structure. Let
N_{i}
be the set of interior nodes of
C_{0}
and
N_{i}=N_{i}^{E}\sqcup N_{i}^{H}
be the set of boundary nodes decomposed further into elliptic and hyperbolic nodes.

Fix cylindrical ends as in §
A.2
.
Let
\mathfrak{j}_{0}
denote the complex structure on
C_{0}
and let
V
be a sufficiently large finite-dimensional vector space and
\{\mathfrak{j}_{v}=\mathfrak{j}(v)\}_{v\in V}
be a smooth family of complex structures on
C_{0}
so that
\mathfrak{j}(0)=j_{0}
and
\mathfrak{j}_{v}
agrees with
\mathfrak{j}_{0}
in a neighbourhood of the images of the cylindrical and strip like ends as well as the boundary.

Given
\alpha\in\mathbb{B}^{N_{i}}
with
\alpha_{x}=e^{-R_{x}-\text{i}\theta_{x}}
and
\beta\in[0,1)^{N_{b}}
with
\beta_{i}=e^{-T_{i}}
, we define the curve
C_{\alpha,\beta}
to be the quotient of the disjoint union

\widehat{C}_{0}\sqcup\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in N_{i}\end{subarray}{*\in\{\pm\}}}{\psi_{x}^{*}([0,R_{x}]\times S^{1})}\sqcup\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in N_{b}^{H}\\
*\in\{\pm\}\end{subarray}}{\psi_{y}^{*}([0,2T_{y}]\times[0,1])}\sqcup\bigsqcup\limits_{y\in N_{b}^{E}}{\psi_{y}(A_{|\beta_{y}|})}

modulo the relations

\psi_{x}^{+}(s,t)\sim\psi_{x}^{-}(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad\begin{cases}s+s^{\prime}=_{x}<\infty,\;t+t^{\prime}=\theta_{x}\\
s=s^{\prime}=R_{x}=\infty\end{cases}

for
x\in N_{i}
, while for
y\in N_{b}^{H}
we have

\psi_{y}^{+}(s,t)\sim\psi_{y}^{-}(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad t=t^{\prime}\text{ and }\begin{cases}s+s^{\prime}=2T_{y}<\infty,\\
s=s^{\prime}=T_{y}=\infty\end{cases}.

Note that we impose no relations on the cylindrical ends at elliptic boundary nodes.

As the cylindrical and strip like ends are holomorphic and by our assumption on the family
\{\mathfrak{j}_{v}\}_{v\in V}
, we see that each
\mathfrak{j}_{v}
descends to
C_{\alpha\beta}
for any
\alpha
and
\beta
. We denote the induced complex structure by
\mathfrak{j}_{v}
as well.

Now define
\mathscr{V}:=V\times\mathbb{B}^{N_{i}}\times[0,1)^{N_{b}}
and let
\uppi\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{V}
be the unique family of curves with fibre over
(v,\alpha,\beta)
being
(C_{\alpha\beta},\mathfrak{j}_{v})
. It is a straightforward verification that
\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{V}
is a family of prestable curves with boundary.

Lemma A.6
.

We can choose
V
above and a map

P^{\prime}\colon E\to C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{o}}\times X,\Omega^{0,1}_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{o}}/\mathscr{V}}\boxtimes_{\mathbb{C}}TX)

so that
\mathfrak{M}^{J}_{P^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\pi})_{\beta}
is representable near
(0,0,u_{0},e_{0})
if and only if
\mathfrak{M}^{J}_{P}(\pi,\beta)
is representable near the point
(v_{0},u_{0},\widetilde{e}_{0})
.

Proof.

The proof of
[
57
, Lemma 4.1]
carries over verbatim.
∎

We assume from now on that
\mathcal{V}=\mathscr{V}
and that
\mathcal{C}=\mathscr{C}
is given by the family of curves constructed above.
Fix a Riemannian metric
g
on
X
so that
L
is totally geodesic with respect to
g
and
g
is flat near
u_{0}(N_{i}\cup N_{b})
. The existence of a metric with the first property is asserted by
[
43
, Lemma 4.3.4]
. Note that the standard metric in the chosen local coordinates of the lemma satisfies Equation (4.3.4) loc. cit., so we may choose this metric to be flat near the given points. Moreover, fix open subsets
\{U_{p}\}_{p\in u_{0}(N_{i})}
and
\{U^{\prime}_{q}\}_{q\in u_{0}(N_{b})}
with pairwise disjoint closure so that

•

p\in U_{p}
and
q\in U^{\prime}_{q}
and

•

(U_{p},g)\cong(B_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2m}),g_{\text{std}})
and
(U^{\prime}_{q},L\cap U^{\prime}_{q},g)\cong(B_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2m}),\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{0\}\cap B_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2m}),g_{\text{std}})
,

•

the image of
u_{0}\circ\psi_{x}^{\pm}
is contained in
U_{u_{0}(x)}
and similarly for
u_{0}\circ\psi^{\pm}_{y}
and
u_{0}\circ\psi_{y}
,

for
p\in u_{0}(N_{i})
and
q\in u_{0}(N_{b})
, respectively, where these isometries map the respective image of the nodal point to
0
. Write

\phi\colon\bigsqcup\limits_{p\in u(N_{i}\sqcup N_{b})}U_{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{2n}

for the induced map. Let
\nabla
be a
J
-linear connection on
TX
that preserves
TL
. Given
x,x^{\prime}\in X
sufficiently close to each other, we denote by

\Phi_{x\to x^{\prime}}\colon T_{x}X\to T_{x}X^{\prime}

the
J
-linear parallel transport along the unique geodesic with respect to
g
from
x
to
x^{\prime}
. By assumption on
g
and
\nabla
,
\Phi_{x\to x^{\prime}}
maps
T_{x}L
to
T_{x^{\prime}}L
if
x,x^{\prime}\in L
.

Fix also two cut-off functions
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
so that

\chi_{1}(s)=\begin{cases}1\quad&s\leq-1\\
0\quad&s\geq 1\end{cases}

we have
-1<\chi_{1}^{\prime}\leq 0
and
\chi_{1}^{2}+\chi_{2}^{2}\equiv 1
.

Definition A.7
.

Given gluing parameters
\alpha
and
\beta
, let

Z_{\alpha_{x}}:=\frac{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times S^{1}\sqcup\mathbb{R}^{-}\times S^{1}}{(s,t)\sim(2R_{x}-s,\theta_{x}-t)},

with the relation holding for
s\in[0,2R_{x}]
if
\alpha_{x}\neq 0
. Similarly, let

\Theta_{\beta_{y}}:=\frac{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times[0,1]\sqcup\mathbb{R}^{-}\times[0,1]}{(s,t)\sim(2T_{y}-s,t)}

with the relation applying to
s\in[0,2T_{y}]
if
\beta_{y}\neq 0
and
y
is hyperbolic and

\Theta_{\beta_{y}}:=[0,\infty)\times S^{1}

when
y
is elliptic.
We define
C_{\alpha\beta}
as above, and set

\widetilde{Z}_{\alpha\beta}:=\prod\limits_{\alpha_{x}\neq 0}{Z_{\alpha_{x}}}\times\prod\limits_{\beta_{y}\neq 0}{\Theta_{\beta_{y}}},

if
(\alpha,\beta)\neq 0
and
\widetilde{Z}_{00}=\emptyset
.

Remark A.8
.

The coordinates
[s,t]
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times S^{1}
extend to
Z_{\alpha_{x}}
via
s^{\prime}=s-2R_{x}
and
t^{\prime}=\theta_{x}-t
on the other component and similarly for
\Theta_{y}
.

Notation
.

Given a continuous vector field
\xi\colon C\to u^{*}TX
, we denote for
w
an interior or hyperbolic boundary node and
y
an elliptic boundary node the vector fields

\xi^{\pm}_{w}:=d\phi(u(\psi_{w}^{\pm}))\xi(\psi_{w}^{\pm})\qquad\qquad\xi_{y}:=d\phi(u(\psi_{y}))\xi(\psi_{y}).

We define the induced maps
u_{w}^{\pm}
and
u_{y}
similarly, while for
\eta\colon\mathscr{C}\to\Lambda^{0,1}_{\mathscr{C},\mathfrak{j}_{0}}\otimes u^{*}TX
, we set

\eta_{w}^{\pm}(s,t):=d\phi(u(w))\Phi_{u^{\pm}_{w}\to u(w)}\eta_{\psi_{x}^{+}(s,t)}(\partial_{s})\qquad\qquad\eta_{y}(s,t):=d\phi(u(y))\Phi_{u^{\pm}_{w}\to u(w)}\eta_{\psi_{y}(s,t)}(\partial_{s}).

Definition A.9

(Pre- and anti-gluing of vector fields)
.

Given gluing parameters
\alpha
and
\beta
and
\xi\in C^{0}(C_{0},u_{0}^{*}(TX,TL))
, define the
pre-glued vector field

\oplus_{\alpha\beta}\xi
on
C_{\alpha\beta}
as follows: it agrees with
\xi
on away from the necks of
C_{\alpha\beta}
that correspond to nodes of
C_{0}
, while over an interior neck associated to
x
we have in the chosen trivialisation

\oplus_{\alpha\beta}\xi(s,t)=\chi_{1}(s-R_{x})\xi^{+}_{x}(s,t)+\chi_{2}(s-R_{x})\xi^{-}_{x}(2R_{x}-s,\theta_{x}-t),

for
s\in[0,2R_{x}]
, while over a boundary strip corresponding to the node
y
,

\oplus_{\alpha\beta}\xi(s,t)=\chi_{1}(s-T_{y})\xi^{+}_{y}(s,t)+\chi_{2}(s-T_{y})\xi^{-}_{y}(2T_{y}-s,t),

for
s\in[0,2T_{y}]
, while over an elliptic disc

\oplus_{\alpha\beta}\xi(s,t)=\chi_{1}(s-T_{y})\xi_{y}(s,t).

The
anti-glued vector field

\ominus_{\alpha\beta}\xi
is the product of maps
Z_{\alpha_{x}}\to TX
and
\Theta_{\beta_{y}}\to TX
given by

[s,t]\mapsto-\chi_{2}(R_{x}-s)(\xi^{+}_{x}(s,t)-[\xi]_{R_{x}})+\chi_{1}(s-R_{x})(\xi^{-}_{x}(2R_{x}-s,\theta_{x}-t)-[\xi]_{R_{x}})

where

[\xi]_{R_{x}}:=\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\int_{S^{1}}\xi^{+}_{x}(R_{x},\cdot)+\xi^{-}_{x}(R_{x},\cdot)\,dt\Bigr),

respectively,

[s,t]\mapsto-\chi_{2}(s-T_{y})(\xi^{+}_{y}(s,t)-[\xi]_{T_{y}}^{\mathbb{R}})+\chi_{1}(s-T_{y})(\xi^{-}_{y}(2T_{y}-s,t)-[\xi]_{T_{y}}^{\mathbb{R}})

where

[\xi]_{T_{y}}^{\mathbb{R}}:=\frac{1}{2}\,\Re\Bigl(\int_{0}^{1}\xi^{+}_{y}(T_{y},\cdot)+\xi^{-}_{y}(T_{y},\cdot)\,dt\Bigr).

For an elliptic boundary node
y
with
\beta_{y}\neq 0
, the map
\Theta_{\beta_{y}}\to TX
is given by

(s,t)\mapsto\xi_{y}(s,t)-\int_{S^{1}}\xi_{y}(T_{y},\cdot)\,dt.

Definition A.10

(Pre- and anti-gluing of maps)
.

Given gluing parameters
\alpha
and
\beta
and a function
v\colon C\to X
, the
pre-glued map

\oplus_{\alpha\beta}v\colon C_{\alpha\beta}\to X

agrees with
v
on away from the necks of
C_{\alpha\beta}
that correspond to nodes of
C_{0}
, while over an interior neck corresponding to
x\in N_{i}
we have

\oplus_{\alpha\beta}v(z)=\exp_{v(x)}((\chi_{1}(s-R_{x})+\chi_{2}(s-2R_{x}))\exp_{v(x)}^{-1}(v(z)))

for
z=\psi^{+}_{x}(s,t)
and
s\in[0,2R_{x}]
.
The definition of
\oplus_{\alpha\beta}v
over a hyperbolic “boundary neck” is analogous. Over an elliptic “boundary disc”, we define

\oplus_{\alpha\beta}v(z)=\exp_{v(y)}(\chi_{1}(s-T_{y})\exp_{v(y)}^{-1}(v(z)))

for
z=\psi_{y}(s,t)
with
s\in[T_{y}-1,2T_{y}]
.

The
anti-glued map
is
\ominus_{\alpha\beta}v
is the product of maps
Z_{\alpha_{x}}\to X
and
\Theta_{\beta_{y}}\to X
given by

(A.3)

[s,t]\mapsto\exp_{v(x)}(-\chi_{2}(s-R_{x})\xi_{x}^{+}(s,t)+\chi_{1}(s-R_{x})\xi_{x}^{-}(2R_{x}-s,\theta_{x}-t))

where
\xi=\exp_{v(x)}^{-1}(v(\cdot))
. The definition of
\ominus_{\alpha\beta}
is analogous for a hyperbolic boundary node, while for an elliptic boundary node
y
the map
\Theta_{\beta_{y}}\to X
is simply
v_{y}
.

Note that

\oplus_{\alpha\beta}u(\partial C_{\alpha\beta})\subset L\qquad\text{and}\qquad\ominus_{\alpha\beta}u(\partial Z_{\alpha\beta})\subset\prod\limits_{\alpha_{x},\beta_{y}\neq 0}{L}

due to our choice of Riemannian metric and connection. The same is true for pre-glued vector fields.

Remark A.11
.

By construction, the exponential map intertwines the two constructions in the sense that

\exp_{\oplus_{\alpha\beta}u_{0}}(\oplus_{\alpha\beta}\xi)=\oplus_{\alpha\beta}\exp_{u_{0}}(\xi)

and similarly for
\ominus_{\alpha\beta}
.

Notation
.

In the coming subsections, we abbreviate
u:=u_{0}
and
u_{\alpha\beta}:=\oplus_{\alpha\beta}u
.

A.4.
Fredholm set-up

We now define the Sobolev spaces we work with and show that the pre-gluing and anti-gluing map together define an isomorphism from sections of
u^{*}TX
to those of
u_{\alpha\beta}^{*}TX
. See
[
36
]
for a similar exposition.

Given
k\gg 1
(e.g.
k\geq 6
) and
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, as well as
\alpha\in\mathbb{B}^{N_{i}}
and
\beta\in[0,1)^{N_{b}}
close to
0
, we define the weighted Sobolev spaces

W^{k,2,\delta}_{TL}(C_{\alpha\beta},u_{\alpha\beta}^{*}TX)\subset\{\xi\in C^{0}(C_{\alpha\beta},u_{\alpha\beta}^{*}TX)\mid\xi(\partial C_{\alpha\beta})\subset u_{\alpha\beta}^{*}TL\}

by the usual Sobolev norm on
\widehat{C}_{0}\subset C_{\alpha\beta}
and by

(A.4)

\lVert\xi\rVert_{k,2,\delta}^{2}=\lVert\xi(x)\rVert^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times S^{1}}\Bigl(\lVert\xi-\xi(x)\rVert^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lVert D^{k}\xi\rVert^{2}\Bigr)e^{2\delta s}dsdt

over each cylindrical end asymptotic to an interior node or an elliptic boundary node
x
, and by

(A.5)

\lVert\xi\rVert_{k,2,\delta}^{2}=\lVert\xi(y)\rVert^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times[0,1]}\Bigl(\lVert\xi-\xi(y)\rVert^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lVert D^{k}\xi\rVert^{2}\Bigr)e^{2\delta s}dsdt

over a strip-like end asymptotic to a boundary node
y\in N_{b}
. Over an interior neck, we define

(A.6)

\lVert\xi\rVert_{k,2,\delta}^{2}=\lVert[\xi]_{R}\rVert^{2}+\int_{[0,2R_{x}]\times S^{1}}\Bigl(\lVert\xi-[\xi]_{R_{x}}\rVert^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lVert D^{k}\xi\rVert^{2}\Bigr)e^{2\delta\min\{s,2R_{x}-s\}}dsdt

while over a hyberbolic boundary neck we have

(A.7)

\lVert\xi\rVert_{k,2,\delta}^{2}=\lVert[\xi]_{T_{y}}^{\mathbb{R}}\rVert^{2}+\int_{[0,2T_{y}]\times[0,1]}\Bigl(\lVert\xi-[\xi]_{T_{y}}^{\mathbb{R}}\rVert^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lVert D^{k}\xi\rVert^{2}\Bigr)e^{2\delta\min\{s,2T_{y}-s\}}dsdt

and over an elliptic boundary neck,

(A.8)

\lVert\xi\rVert_{k,2,\delta}^{2}=\lVert\int_{S^{1}}\xi(\psi_{y}(T_{y},\cdot))\,dt\rVert^{2}+\int_{[0,2T_{y}]\times S^{1}}\Bigl(\lVert\xi-[\xi]_{T_{y}}^{\mathbb{R}}\rVert^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lVert D^{k}\xi\rVert^{2}\Bigr)e^{2\delta\min\{s,2T_{y}-s\}}dsdt

Here we use the trivialisations chosen in §
A.3
to make sense of
\xi-\xi(x)
, respectively,
\xi-[\xi]_{R}
.

We define the weighted Sobolev norm on

W^{k-1,2,\delta}_{TL}(C_{\alpha\beta},\Omega^{0,1}_{\mathscr{C}_{\alpha\beta},\mathfrak{j}_{v}}\boxtimes u_{\alpha\beta}^{*}TX)\subset\{\eta\in C^{0}(C_{\alpha\beta},\Omega^{0,1}_{\mathscr{C}_{\alpha\beta},\mathfrak{j}_{v}}\boxtimes u_{\alpha\beta}^{*}TX)\mid\eta(T\partial C_{\alpha\beta})\subset u_{\alpha\beta}^{*}TL\}

by the usual
(k,2)
-norm on
\widehat{C}_{0}
. Over the ends and necks, we trivialise using the chosen biholomorphisms and charts on
X
and evaluate at
\partial_{s}
, so that we can consider the
(0,1)
-forms as functions valued in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
. We then define the norms by the same expressions as (
A.4
)-(
A.8
) except that we use
\lVert\eta\rVert
instead of
\lVert\eta-\eta(w)\rVert
or
\lVert\eta-[\eta]\rVert
.

Define for
\alpha_{x}\neq 0
, the Sobolev spaces

W_{\alpha_{x}}:=W^{k,2,\delta}(Z_{\alpha_{x}},\mathbb{C}^{n})\qquad\widehat{W}_{\alpha_{x}}:=W^{k-1,2,\delta}(Z_{\alpha_{x}},\Omega^{0,1}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n})

with the norms

(A.9)

\lVert\xi\rVert_{k,2,\delta}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times S^{1}}\Bigl(\lVert\xi-\xi(\infty)\rVert^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lVert D^{j}\xi\rVert^{2}\Bigr)e^{2\delta s}dsdt\\
+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{-}\times S^{1}}\Bigl(\lVert\xi-\xi(-\infty)\rVert^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lVert D^{j}\xi\rVert\Bigr)^{2}e^{2\delta|s^{\prime}|}ds^{\prime}dt

and

(A.10)

\lVert\eta\rVert_{k-1,2,\delta}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\lVert D^{j}\eta\rVert^{2}e^{2\delta|s|}dsdt

Meanwhile, for
\beta_{y}\neq 0
, define

W_{\beta_{y}}:=W^{k,2,\delta}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\Theta_{\beta_{y}},\mathbb{C}^{n})\qquad\widehat{W}_{\beta_{y}}:=W^{k-1,2,\delta}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\Theta_{\beta_{y}},\Omega^{0,1}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n})

with the analogous of the norms (
A.9
) and (
A.10
). Let

\mathcal{V}_{\alpha\beta}\subset\prod\limits_{\alpha_{x}\neq 0}{W_{\alpha_{x}}}\times\prod\limits_{\beta_{y}\neq 0}{W_{\beta_{y}}}

by the subspace of elements with
\xi_{w}(\infty)=-\xi_{w}(-\infty)
for an interior or a hyberbolic boundary node
w
, and define

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{\alpha\beta}:=\prod\limits_{\alpha_{x}\neq 0}{\widehat{W}_{\alpha_{x}}}\times\prod\limits_{\beta_{y}\neq 0}{\widehat{W}_{\beta_{y}}}.

Proposition A.12
.

The complex-linear maps

(A.11)

\boxdot_{\alpha\beta}\colon W^{k,2,\delta}_{TL}(C_{0},u^{*}TX)\to W^{k,2,\delta}_{TL}(C_{\alpha\beta},\oplus_{\alpha\beta}u^{*}TX)\oplus\mathcal{V}_{\alpha\beta}:\xi\mapsto(\oplus_{\alpha\beta}\xi,\ominus_{\alpha\beta}\xi)

and

\widehat{\boxdot}_{\alpha\beta}\colon W^{k-1,2,\delta}_{TL}(C_{0},\Lambda^{1}_{\mathscr{C},\mathfrak{j}_{0}}\otimes\,u^{*}TX)\to W^{k-1,2,\delta}_{TL}(\mathscr{C}_{\alpha\beta},\Lambda^{1}_{\mathscr{C}_{\alpha\beta},\mathfrak{j}_{0}}\otimes\,\oplus_{\alpha\beta}u^{*}TX)\oplus\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{\alpha\beta}

given by

\widehat{\boxdot}_{\alpha\beta}\eta=(\widehat{\oplus}_{\alpha\beta}\eta,\widehat{\ominus}_{\alpha\beta}\eta),

are isomorphisms and there exists a constant
c_{0}
independent of
\alpha
and
\beta
, for
\max\{|\alpha|,|\beta|\}\leq\frac{1}{4}
, so that

(A.12)

\frac{1}{c_{0}}\lVert\xi\rVert_{k,2,\delta}\leq\lVert\boxdot_{\alpha\beta}\xi\rVert_{k,2,\delta}\leq c_{0}\lVert\xi\rVert_{k,2,\delta}

and similarly for
\widehat{\boxdot}_{\alpha\beta}
.

Proof.

This follows from
[
33
, Theorem 2.23 and Theorem 2.26]
adapted to case with boundary as in
[
36
, Proposition 12.0.1]
(with
m=0
). In
[
36
]
,
E
and
F
denote the scale Banach spaces with

\mathcal{X}_{m}=W^{k+m,2,\delta_{m}}_{TL}(C_{0},u^{*}TX)\qquad\qquad\mathcal{Y}_{m}=W^{k-1+m,2,\delta_{m}}_{TL}(C_{0},\Lambda^{0,1}_{C_{0},\mathfrak{j}_{0}}\otimes u^{*}TX),

of which we consider only the case of
m=0
. Meanwhile
G^{a}
loc. cit. corresponds to the right hand side of (
A.11
). There are two more relevant differences: over the necks of
C_{\alpha\beta}
, they use a different norms than (
A.6
)-(
A.8
), which, however, are commensurate to the respective norm used here. Moreover, they use a single cut-off function
\beta
with the same property as
\chi_{1}
and take
1-\beta
instead of requiring
\chi_{1}^{2}+\chi_{2}^{2}\equiv 1
as we do. The proof of the estimate,
[
33
, Lemma 2.27]
, used in both statements, goes through verbatim, observing additionally that over
[0,2R]\times S^{1}
, respectively
[0,2R]\times[0,1]
, the norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{k,2,-\delta}
is commensurate with
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{k,2,\delta}
.
∎

Given
v\in V
sufficiently close to the origin, the map

I(v):=\frac{1}{2}(Id-\mathfrak{j}_{v}\mathfrak{j}_{0})\colon(T\widetilde{C},\mathfrak{j}_{0})\to(T\widetilde{C},\mathfrak{j}_{v})

is an isomorphism which is the identity near the preimages of nodes of
C
. Given
\alpha
and
\beta
close to
0
,
I(v)
thus descends to an isomorphism
I_{\alpha\beta}(v)\colon(T\widetilde{C}_{\alpha\beta},\mathfrak{j}_{0})\to(T\widetilde{C}_{\alpha\beta},\mathfrak{j}_{v})
.

A.5.
Cauchy-Riemann equation

Set

\mathcal{X}:=W^{k,2,\delta}_{TL}(C_{0},u^{*}TX)\qquad\qquad\mathcal{Y}:=W^{k-1,2,\delta}_{TL}(\widetilde{C},\Omega^{0,1}_{\widetilde{C}}\otimes\widetilde{u}^{*}TX).

Define

\mathcal{F}\colon V\times\mathbb{B}^{N_{x}}\times[0,1)^{N_{b}}\times\mathcal{X}\times E\to\mathcal{Y}

by

(A.13)

\mathcal{F}_{v,\alpha\beta}(\xi,e):=\\
\,\widehat{\boxdot}_{\alpha\beta}^{-1}\left[(\Phi_{\exp_{u_{\alpha\beta}}(\oplus_{\alpha\beta}\xi)\to u_{\alpha\beta}}\otimes I_{\alpha\beta}(v))\Bigl(d(\exp_{u_{\alpha\beta}}(\oplus_{\alpha\beta}\xi))^{0,1}_{J,\mathfrak{J}_{v}}+P(e_{0}+e)(\alpha,\beta,\cdot,\exp_{u_{\alpha\beta}}(\oplus_{\alpha\beta}\xi))\Bigr),\bar{\partial}\ominus_{\alpha\beta}\xi\right].

\mathcal{F}
is only defined near the origin and we will implicitly always take
(v,\alpha,\beta,\xi,e)
close to zero. The following result is an adaptation of the gluing analysis in
[
32
]
, see also
[
36
]
. However, we do not use notions from scale calculus beyond the next statement.

Recall that an
sc
-Banach space

\mathcal{Z}
is a sequence
(\mathcal{Z}_{m})_{m\geq 0}
of Banach spaces together with compact embeddings
\mathcal{Z}_{m+1}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{m}
so that
\mathcal{Z}_{\infty}:=\bigcap\limits_{m\geq 0}{\mathcal{Z}_{m}}
is dense in
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. If
U\subset\mathcal{Z}
is an open subset, we call a map
f\colon U\to\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
to another
sc
-Banach space
of class
sc^{0}
if
f(U\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m})\subset\mathcal{Z}_{m}
for any
m\geq 0
and
f
is continuous. We say
f
is
sc^{1}
if there exists a fibrewise-linear
{sc}^{0}
map

Tf\colon TU=U\times\mathcal{Z}_{1}\to T\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}:(x,h)\mapsto(f(x),df(x)h)

so that

\lim\limits_{\lVert h\rVert_{1}\rightarrow 0}{\frac{1}{\lVert h\rVert_{1}}\lVert f(x+h)-f(x)-Tf(x,h)\rVert}=0

for any
x\in U
and
h\in\mathcal{Z}_{1}
. We call
f

of class
\text{sc}^{n}
for
n\geq 1
if
Tf
is of class
sc^{n-1}
and we call it
sc
-smooth if it is of class
sc^{n}
for any
n
.

If
U=B\times U^{\prime}\subset W\times\mathcal{Z}
, where
W
is a finite-dimensional manifold, possibly with boundary, and
\mathcal{Z}
is an
sc
-Banach space, we say
f\colon U\to\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
is
sc
-smooth relative to
B
if the map
f_{b}:=f(b,\cdot)
is
sc
-smooth for any
b\in B
and
b\mapsto T^{k}f_{b}
is continuous for any
k\geq 0
.

Theorem A.13
.

\mathcal{F}
is

sc
-smooth relative to
V\times\mathbb{B}^{N_{x}}\times[0,1)^{N_{i}}
with respect to the filtrations

\mathcal{X}_{m}:=W^{k+m,2,\delta_{m}}_{TL}(C_{0},u^{*}TX)\qquad\qquad\mathcal{Y}_{m}:=W^{k-1,2,\delta}_{TL}(\widetilde{C},\Omega^{0,1}_{\widetilde{C}}\otimes\widetilde{u}^{*}TX)

where
(\delta_{m})_{m\geq 0}
is any strictly increasing sequence with
\delta=\delta_{0}<\delta_{m}<\frac{1}{2}
.

Proof.

This is a generalisation of
[
32
, Proposition 4.21]
to the case with boundary and the same proof goes through, with certain ingredients replaced by the corresponding statement for bordered Riemann surfaces. We will summarise the proof and point out the changes.

Away from the cylindrical or strip-like ends, the proof of (relatives)
sc
-smoothness follows from standard elliptic theory, as well as the fact that the maps involved are the differential operator
d
and pre- or post-composition with maps defined on smooth closed manifolds, respectively their tangent bundles. See also the discussion below
[
32
, Equation (4.16)]
.

Near interior or elliptic boundary nodes, the proof of
[
32
, Proposition 4.21]
goes through verbatim since we defined the gluing and anti-gluing over these nodes in the same way save for the domain. In particular, the proof of
[
32
, Proposition 4.11]
goes through. We note here that we use slightly different cut-off functions, which does not affect the relevant estimates. We use different norms over the necks, which, however, are commensurate with those of
[
32
]
. Finally, the anti-gluing defined here differs from the anti-gluing of
[
32
]
in that we precompose with the parallel transport
\Phi_{u\to u(w)}
for a node
w
. This invertible operator is independent of the element of the domain of
\mathcal{F}
and hence does not affect any estimate apart from increasing certain constants. Finally, the proof of
[
32
, Proposition 4.11]
for gluing and anti-gluing at an elliptic boundary node is in the same vein, in fact, even easier.
∎

A.6.
Gluing map

We need a relative version of (a special case of)
[
31
, Theorem 4.6]
.

Proposition A.14

(Relative
sc
-implicit function theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
be
sc
-Banach spaces and
T
a finite-dimensional manifold, possibly with corners. Suppose
f\colon T\times\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
is an
sc
-smooth Fredholm map relative to
T
, so that for some
t_{0}\in T
and
z_{0}\in f_{t_{0}}^{-1}(\{0\})
, the linearisation
df_{t_{0}}(z_{0})\colon T_{z_{0}}\mathcal{Z}\to T_{f_{t_{0}}(z_{0})}\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
is surjective and there exists a continuous family
M
of
sc
-complements to
\ker(df_{t}(z_{0}))
for
t
near
t_{0}
. Then
S:=f^{-1}(\{0\})
admits a
good parametrisation
near
(t_{0},z_{0})
.

That is, there exists neighbourhoods
W\subset T\times\ker(df_{t_{0}}(z_{0}))
and
U\subset T\times E
near
\{(t_{0},0)\}
, respectively
(t_{0},z_{0})
, as well as an
sc
-smooth map

\Psi\colon W\to M,

compatible with the projection to
T
, so that

(1)

\Psi(t,0)=0
and
d\Psi_{t}(0)=0
;

(2)

the map

\Gamma\colon W\to\mathcal{Z}:w\mapsto(t,z_{0}+w+\Psi_{t}(w))

maps onto
S\cap U
and is compatible with the projection to
T
,

(3)

if
(t,z)=\Gamma(t,w)\in S\cap U
, then the map
\ker(df_{t_{0}}(z_{0}))\to\ker(df_{t}(z))
given by

v\mapsto v+d\Psi_{t}(w)v

is a linear isomorphism.

Proof.

The claim in the case of
T=\text{pt}
given by
[
31
, Theorem 4.6]
. The general case follows from a straightforward adaptation to the relative setting; refer e.g. to the proof of the relative inverse function theorem,
[
57
, Lemma 5.10]
. Denote by
\Psi(\cdot)_{\mathcal{X}}
, respectively
\Psi(\cdot)_{\mathcal{X}}
the composition of
\Psi
with the projection onto the respective vector space.
∎

Lemma A.15
.

There exists a linear subspace
E^{\prime}\subset E
and finitely many points
q_{1},\dots,q_{m}\in\widehat{C}_{0}
and subspaces
V_{i}\subset T_{u(q_{i})}X
so that the map

\displaystyle L\colon\mathcal{X}\oplus E

\displaystyle\to E/E^{\prime}\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{i=1}^{m}{\raisebox{2.04439pt}{$T_{u(q_{i})}X$}\left/\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$V_{i}$}\right.}

\displaystyle(\xi,e)

\displaystyle\mapsto(e+E^{\prime})\oplus(\xi(q_{i})+V_{i})_{i=1,\dots,m}

restricts to an isomorphism on
K_{v,\alpha\beta}
for any
v,\alpha,\beta
close to
0
.

Refer to
[
57
, Lemma 4.10]
for a proof, which carries over verbatim to our setting.

Define

(A.14)

Q_{v,\alpha\beta}\colon\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}:\eta\mapsto(D_{v,\alpha\beta}|_{\ker(L)})^{-1}(\eta).

By Theorem
A.13
,
Q_{v,\alpha\beta}
is strongly continuous in
v,\alpha
and
\beta
. Taking
(t_{0},z_{0})=(0,0,e_{0})
, Proposition
A.14
asserts the existence of maps
\Psi
and
\Gamma
with the properties (
1
)-(
3
).

Definition A.16
.

We define the
gluing map

\varphi=\varphi_{u_{0}}\colon U\subset V\times\mathbb{B}_{\epsilon}^{N_{i}}\times[0,\epsilon)^{N_{b}}\times K_{0,0}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{J}(\pi,X,L)

by

\varphi(v,\alpha,\beta,\xi,e)=\Bigl(v,\alpha,\beta,\oplus_{\alpha\beta}\exp_{u}(\xi+\Psi_{v,\alpha\beta}(\xi,e)_{\mathcal{X}}),\Psi_{v,\alpha\beta}(\xi,e)_{E}\Bigr),

where
U
is a sufficiently small neighbourhood of the origin.

Since
K_{0,0}
is finite-dimensional, the strong operator topology on bounded operators
K_{0,0}\to\mathcal{X}
agrees with the topology induced by the operator norm, so
\Psi
defines a relatively smooth map with respect to the operator norm.

Lemma A.17
.

\varphi
is injective near the origin.

Proof.

Suppose there exists sequences
(v_{j},\alpha_{j},\beta_{j},\xi_{j},e_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(v^{\prime}_{j},\alpha^{\prime}_{j},\beta^{\prime}_{j},\xi^{\prime}_{j},e_{j}^{\prime})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
converging to
0
so that

(v_{j},\alpha_{j},\beta_{j},\xi_{j},e_{j})\neq(v^{\prime}_{j},\alpha^{\prime}_{j},\beta^{\prime}_{j},\xi^{\prime}_{j},e_{j}^{\prime})\qquad\qquad\varphi(v_{j},\alpha_{j},\beta_{j},\xi_{j})=\varphi(v^{\prime}_{j},\alpha^{\prime}_{j},\beta^{\prime}_{j},\xi^{\prime}_{j},e_{j}^{\prime})

for each
j\geq 1
. This implies
(v_{j},\alpha_{j},\beta_{j})=(v^{\prime}_{j},\alpha^{\prime}_{j},\beta^{\prime}_{j})
as well as

\Psi_{v_{j},\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}}(\xi_{j},e_{j})_{E}=\Psi_{v_{j},\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}}(\xi_{j},e_{j})_{E}

and

\exp_{u_{\alpha_{j}\beta_{j}}}(\oplus_{\alpha\beta}(\xi_{j}+\Psi_{v_{j},\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}}(\xi_{j},e_{j})_{\mathcal{X}}))=\exp_{u_{\alpha_{j}\beta_{j}}}(\oplus_{\alpha\beta}(\xi_{j}^{\prime}+\Psi_{v_{j},\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}}(\xi^{\prime}_{j},e_{j}^{\prime})_{\mathcal{X}})).

By the Sobolev embedding theorem, we have

\lVert\oplus_{\alpha\beta}\xi_{j}+\Psi_{v_{j},\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}}(\xi_{j},e_{j})_{\mathcal{X}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq\text{injr}(X,g)

for
j\gg 1
and similarly for
\xi^{\prime}_{j}
, whence

\oplus_{\alpha\beta}\xi_{j}+\Psi_{v_{j},\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}}(\xi_{j},e_{j})=\oplus_{\alpha\beta}\xi^{\prime}_{j}+\Psi_{v_{j},\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}}(\xi^{\prime}_{j},e_{j}^{\prime}).

As
\text{im}(\Psi_{v,\alpha,\beta})\subset\ker(L)
and
L
is injective on
K_{0,0}
, the claim follows by applying
L
.
∎

Lemma A.18
.

\varphi
surjects onto a neighbourhood of
(0,0,0,u_{0},e_{0})
.

Proof.

This follows from the same arguments as in
[
46
, Proposition B.11.5]
. He only has the interior gluing parameters
\alpha
, which does not affect the arguments, and
\kappa
instead of
\xi
. The expression
\oplus_{\alpha\beta}(\xi+\Psi_{v,\alpha\beta}(\xi,e)_{\mathcal{X}})
corresponds to his
\kappa^{\infty}_{v,\alpha}(\xi,e)
.
∎

By
[
46
, Lemma B.12.1]
the map
\varphi
, possibly restricted to an even smaller neighbourhood of the origin, is a homeomorphism onto a neighbourhood of
(0,0,0,u,e_{0})
.

A.7.
Rel–
C^{\infty}
structure

This part of the proof is exactly as in
[
57
, §4.4.]
and only reproduced here for the sake of completeness. Shrinking the domain of
\varphi_{u}
further, we may assume

U_{u}=V_{u}\times\mathbb{B}_{r}^{N_{i}}\times[0,r)^{N_{b}}\times K_{u}

where
V_{u}\subset V
and
K_{u}\subset K
are small neighbourhoods of the respective origin. Let

\pi_{u}\colon U_{u}\to\mathcal{S}_{u}:=V_{u}\times\mathbb{B}_{r}^{N_{i}}\times[0,r)^{N_{b}}

be the projection and let
\mathcal{C}_{u}\to\mathcal{S}_{u}
be the restriction of the universal curve. Define

ev_{u}\colon\mathcal{C}_{u}\times K_{u}\to X:((v,\alpha,\beta,z),(\xi,e))\mapsto\oplus_{\alpha\beta}\exp_{u}(\xi+\Psi_{v,\alpha\beta}(\xi,e)_{\mathcal{X}})(z).

By construction,
ev_{u}(\bar{\partial}\mathcal{C}_{u}\times K_{u})\subset L
.

Let
W_{u}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{P}^{J}(\pi,\beta)^{\text{reg}}
be the open subset given by the image of
\varphi_{u}
. Recall that the topological space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{P}^{J}(\pi,\beta)^{\text{reg}}
represents the functor

\mathfrak{M}^{\text{top}}:Y\mapsto\mathfrak{M}(Y/Y)

(by the arguments of
[
57
, §3.2.2]
). We define the functor
\mathfrak{M}^{W_{u}}
by

\mathfrak{M}^{W_{u}}(Y/T)=\left\{\chi\in\mathfrak{M}(Y/T)\mid\chi^{\text{top}}\in\mathfrak{M}^{\text{top}}(Y)\text{ has image contained in }W_{u}\right\}.

Proposition A.19

(Local representability)
.

The commutative diagram

(A.15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{u}\times K_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev_{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{u}\times\text{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}

defines an element
\widetilde{\varphi}_{u}\in\mathfrak{M}(U_{u}/\mathcal{S}_{u})
lifting
\varphi_{u}\in\mathfrak{M}^{\text{top}}(U_{u})
. The associated natural transformation
U_{u}/\mathcal{S}_{u}\to\mathfrak{M}
induces an isomorphism
U_{u}/\mathcal{S}_{u}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{M}^{W_{u}}
.

Proof.

The first claim follows as soon as we have shown that
ev_{u}\colon\mathcal{C}_{u}\times K_{u}/\mathcal{C}
is relatively smooth. This can be seen from the same arguments as in the proof of
[
57
, Theorem 4.14]
, whose notation we follow closely. The second assertion is our analogue of
[
57
, Theorem 4.16]
, whose proof carries over verbatim. Note that in both cases, we do not have the additional perturbation space
E
since we consider honest pseudo-holomorphic curves, while
[
57
]
allows for perturbations of the Cauchy-Riemann equation.∎

This result relieves us of having to show that the transition functions between the local charts given by
\varphi_{u}\colon U_{u}\to W_{u}
are relatively smooth with respect to
\mathcal{S}
. It completes thus the proof of Theorem
A.4
.

Appendix B
Orientations

We discuss orientations of global Kuranishi charts and summarise how to orient the index bundles of Cauchy–Riemann operators over a bordered surface with relatively spin totally real boundary condition.

B.1.
Orientations of global Kuranishi charts

We use the following conventions for the orientation of quotients and vector bundles.

Definition B.1
.

Suppose a Lie group
G
acts almost freely, properly, and locally linearly on an oriented topological manifold
M
. We orient the quotient
M/G
(over
\mathbb{Q}
) by pulling back the orientation
(-1)^{(\dim(M)-\dim(G))\dim(G)}\mathfrak{o}_{M}
along the homeomorphism (
C.2
).

Definition B.2
.

Given an oriented orbi-bundle
\pi\colon W\to Y
over an oriented orbifold, we orient the total space
W
via the Thom isomorphism
H^{\dim(W)}(W;\mathbb{Q})\cong H^{\dim(Y)}(Y;\mathbb{Q})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\langle{\tau_{W}}\rangle
.

Remark B.3
.

If
M
and
Y
are smooth, then this can be said more concisely. That is, letting
q
be the quotient map, we orient
T(M/G)
and
TW
via the isomorphisms

TM\cong q^{*}T(M/G)\oplus\mathfrak{g}\qquad\text{and}\qquad TW\cong\pi^{*}TY\oplus\pi^{*}W.

Definition B.4
.

For a rel–
C^{\infty}
global Kuranishi chart
\mathcal{K}=(G,\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{B},\mathcal{E},s)
for
Z
, we define its
determinant

(B.1)

\det(\mathcal{K}):=\det(T\mathcal{T})\otimes\det(\mathfrak{g})^{\vee}\otimes\det(\mathcal{E})^{\vee},

which we consider as a vector bundle germ near
s^{-1}(0)
. An
orientation
of
\mathcal{K}
is a section of its
orientation line

(\det(\mathcal{K})\setminus\{0\})/\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

As was already observed in
[
3
]
, a global Kuranishi chart might be orientable even though its thickening and obstruction bundle are not. However, in this case one can always stabilise by the obstruction bundle to obtain a global Kuranishi chart whose thickening and obstruction bundle are orientable.

Definition B.5
.

If
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is a global Kuranishi chart for
i=\{0,1\}
, equipped with an orientation
\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{K}_{i}}
, then we orient the
product global Kuranishi chart

\mathcal{K}_{0}\times\mathcal{K}_{1}=(G_{0}\times G_{1},\mathcal{T}_{0}\times\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{0}\boxplus\mathcal{E}_{1},s_{0}\boxtimes s_{1})

via the
product orientation

\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{K}_{0}}\times\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{K}_{1}}:=\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{K}_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{K}_{1}}
.

Remark B.6
.

Given orientations of thickening, covering group and obstruction bundle, another natural choice of orientation of
\mathcal{K}_{0}\times\mathcal{K}_{1}
would have been

(\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{K}_{0}}\times\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{K}_{1}})^{\prime}:=\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathfrak{g}_{1}}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathfrak{g}_{0}}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{E}_{1}}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}^{\vee}.

The advantage of Definition
B.5
is that it does not require a choice of the components of the global Kuranishi charts and the property
\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{K}_{1}}\times\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{K}_{0}}=(-1)^{\text{vdim}(\mathcal{K}_{0})\text{vdim}(\mathcal{K}_{1})}\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{K}_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{K}_{1}}
.

More generally, we define the fibre product of global Kuranishi charts and its orientation as follows, recovering Definition
C.15
in case both obstruction bundles are trivial.

Definition B.7
.

Suppose
\mathcal{K}_{0}
and
\mathcal{K}_{1}
are oriented global Kuranishi charts, each equipped with a
G_{i}
-invariant rel–
C^{\infty}
submersion
f_{i}\colon\mathcal{K}_{i}\to Y
to a smooth oriented manifold
Y
. We equip the
fibre product global Kuranishi chart

\mathcal{K}_{0}\times_{Y}\mathcal{K}_{1}:=(G_{0}\times G_{1},\mathcal{T}_{0}\times_{Y}\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{0}\times\mathcal{E}_{1}|_{\mathcal{T}_{0}\times_{Y}\mathcal{T}_{1}},\mathfrak{s}_{0}\times\mathfrak{s}_{1}|_{\mathcal{T}_{0}\times_{Y}\mathcal{T}_{1}})

with the unique orientation
\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{K}_{0}\times_{L}\mathcal{K}_{1}}
so that the canonical isomorphism

\det(\mathcal{K}_{0}\times_{Y}\mathcal{K}_{1})\otimes\det(TY)\xrightarrow{\cong}\det(\mathcal{K}_{0})\otimes\det(\mathcal{K}_{1})

maps it to
(-1)^{\text{vdim}(\mathcal{K}_{1})\dim(Y)}\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{K}_{0}}\times\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{K}_{1}}
.

For the next lemma, we think of the thickenings as the orbifolds
\mathcal{T}/G
as this is how we will use it in the main application, and thus just write
\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{T}}
instead of
\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{T}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\vee}
.

Lemma B.8
.

Let
\mathcal{K}_{0}
and
\mathcal{K}_{1}
be as in Definition
B.7
with submersion to
Y
. Then the obstruction bundle of
\mathcal{K}_{0}\times_{Y}\mathcal{K}_{1}
is given by
\mathcal{E}_{0}\boxplus\mathcal{E}_{1}
. Suppose
\mathcal{E}_{0}
and
\mathcal{E}_{1}
are orientable and endow their direct sum with the direct sum orientation. Then, write
\widetilde{\mathfrak{o}}_{\mathcal{T}_{0}\times_{Y}\mathcal{T}_{1}}
for the induced orientation of the thickening and
\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{T}_{0}\times_{Y}\mathcal{T}_{1}}
for the standard fibre product orientation, we obtain that

\widetilde{\mathfrak{o}}_{\mathcal{T}_{0}\times_{Y}\mathcal{T}_{1}}=(-1)^{\text{rank}(\mathcal{E}_{0})(\dim(Y)+\text{vdim}(\mathcal{K}_{1}))}\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{T}_{0}\times_{Y}\mathcal{T}_{1}}.

Proof.

Let
n=\dim(Y)
and
m_{i}=\text{rank}(\mathcal{E}_{i})
. We have

\widetilde{\mathfrak{o}}_{\mathcal{T}_{0}\times_{Y}\mathcal{T}_{1}}\wedge(\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{E}_{1}})^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{Y}=(-1)^{\text{vdim}(\mathcal{K}_{1})n}\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}^{\vee}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{E}_{1}}^{\vee}

whence

\displaystyle(-1)^{n(m_{0}+m_{1})}\widetilde{\mathfrak{o}}_{\mathcal{T}_{0}\times_{Y}\mathcal{T}_{1}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{Y}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\text{vdim}(\mathcal{K}_{1})n+m_{0}\text{vdim}(\mathcal{K}_{1})}\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\text{vdim}(\mathcal{K}_{1})n+m_{0}\text{vdim}(\mathcal{K}_{1})+n\dim(\mathcal{T}_{1})}\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{T}_{0}\times_{Y}\mathcal{T}_{1}}\wedge\mathfrak{o}_{Y}.

∎

Lemma B.9
.

Suppose we have transverse fibre products
X_{i}\times_{Z_{i}}Y_{i}
for
i=\{0,1\}
and
Y_{1}\times_{W}Y_{2}
of orbifolds so that
(X_{1}\times_{Z_{1}}Y_{1})\times_{W}(X_{2}\times_{Z_{2}}Y_{2})
is transverse as well. If all orbifolds are oriented, then

\displaystyle(X_{1}\times_{Z_{1}}Y_{1})\times_{W}(X_{2}\times_{Z_{2}}Y_{2})=(-1)^{\epsilon}(X_{1}\times_{W}X_{2})\times_{Z_{1}\times Z_{2}}(Y_{1}\times_{W}Y_{2})

\displaystyle\text{with }\quad\epsilon=(\dim Y_{1}-\dim Z_{1})(\dim X_{2}-\dim Z_{2}-\dim W)

The same result holds for fibre products of global Kuranishi charts, replacing dimension by virtual dimension.

Proof.

As the question is pointwise, we may assume that all orbifolds are manifolds (in fact, vector spaces) and
X_{i}=X^{\prime}_{i}\times Z_{i}
and
X^{\prime}_{1}=X^{\prime\prime}_{1}\times W
and
X^{\prime}_{2}=W\times X^{\prime\prime}_{2}
. Then

X_{i}\times_{Z_{i}}Y_{i}\cong X^{\prime}_{i}\times Y_{i}\qquad\qquad(X^{\prime\prime}_{1}\times W)\times_{W}(W\times X^{\prime\prime}_{2})=X^{\prime\prime}_{1}\times W\times X^{\prime\prime}_{2}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle(X_{1}\times_{Z_{1}}Y_{1})

\displaystyle\times_{W}(X_{2}\times_{Z_{2}}Y_{2})=(X^{\prime\prime}_{1}\times W\times Y_{1})\times_{W}(W\times X^{\prime\prime}_{2}\times Y_{2})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\dim Y_{1}\dim X^{\prime}_{1}}Y_{1}\times(X^{\prime\prime}_{1}\times W\times X^{\prime\prime}_{2})\times Y_{2}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\dim Y_{1}\dim X^{\prime\prime}_{2}}(X^{\prime\prime}_{1}\times W\times X^{\prime\prime}_{2})\times(Y_{1}\times Y_{2})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\dim Y_{1}\dim X^{\prime\prime}_{2}}(X^{\prime\prime}_{1}\times W\times X^{\prime\prime}_{2})\times Z_{1}\times Z_{2}\times_{Z_{1}\times Z_{2}}(Y_{1}\times Y_{2})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\dim Y_{1}\dim X^{\prime\prime}_{2}+\dim Z_{1}\dim X^{\prime}_{2}}((X^{\prime\prime}_{1}\times Z_{1}\times W\times X^{\prime\prime}_{2}\times Z_{2})\times_{Z_{0}\times Z_{1}}(Y_{1}\times Y_{2})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\dim Y_{1}\dim X^{\prime\prime}_{2}+\dim Z_{1}\dim X^{\prime}_{2}}((X^{\prime\prime}_{1}\times Z_{1}\times W)\times_{W}X_{2})\times_{Z_{1}\times Z_{2}}(Y_{1}\times Y_{2})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\dim Y_{1}\dim X^{\prime\prime}_{2}+\dim Z_{1}\dim X^{\prime\prime}_{2}}(X_{1}\times_{W}X_{2})\times_{Z_{1}\times Z_{2}}(Y_{1}\times Y_{2})

so that

\epsilon\equiv(\dim Y_{1}+\dim Z_{1})(\dim X_{2}-\dim Z_{2}-\dim W).

∎

B.2.
Cauchy–Riemann operators on bordered surfaces

This subsection is a brief summary of the definition of
[
9
]
, describing how a relative spin structure defines an orientation on Cauchy-Riemann problem on a surface with boundary. Proposition
B.11
is the analogue of
[
58
, Theorem 4.3.3(c)]
using the constructions and conventions of
[
9
]
. Concretely, it describes how orientations compare as a surface develops a boundary node of type (H1). To maximise compatibility with
[
9
]
we identify a surface of boundary with the symmetric surface given by taking its complex double with the induced real structure.
5
5

5

As we consider any surface with boundary to be endowed with an ordering of its boundary components, this induces a decoration on its complex double in the sense of
[
9
]
.

Spin structures:
Recall that
\text{Spin}(n)
is the universal cover of
\text{SO}(n)
and that a spin structure on an oriented vector bundle
E\to B
is a lift of the classifying map
B\to B\text{SO}(n)
to
B\to B\text{Spin}(n)
.
Since
\pi_{2}(B\text{Spin}(n))=0
and a nullhomotopy of
B\to B\text{SO}(n)
determines a (homotopy class) of trivialisation, this definition can be rephrased if
B
is a CW complex and
n\geq 3
. In this case, such a lift is equivalent to a choice of homotopy class of trivialisations of
E
restricted to the
2
-skeleton of
B
, cf.
[
9
, Definition 1.2]
. In particular, it yields a choice of homotopy class of trivialisation of
\gamma^{*}E
for any map
\gamma\colon S^{1}\to B
. It is exactly this property of yielding ‘coherent
choices
of trivialisations’ that will be crucial for the construction of orientations of Cauchy-Riemann operators below.

Definition B.10
.

An
OSpin structure

\mathfrak{o}\mathfrak{s}
on a real vector bundle pair
(V,\varphi)
over a symmetric surface
(C,\sigma)
is an orientation
\mathfrak{o}
on
V^{\varphi}
together with a spin structure
\mathfrak{s}
on
V^{\varphi}\to C^{\sigma}
lifting
\mathfrak{o}
.

On a smooth surface:
Suppose
(V,\varphi)
is a real vector bundle pair of rank
n
over a decorated symmetric surface
(C,\sigma)
equipped with an OSpin structure
(\mathfrak{o},\mathfrak{s})
. Suppose
D
is a real Cauchy–Riemann operator on
(V,\varphi)
. Let
\{U_{i}\}_{i\leq r}
be a set of
\sigma
-invariant tubular neighbourhoods of the boundary components so that
\overline{U_{i}}\cap\overline{{U_{j}}}=\emptyset
if
i\neq j
and
L|_{\overline{U_{i}}}
is trivial. Let
\gamma_{i}^{\pm}:=\partial^{\pm}U_{i}
and let
C_{0}
be the surface obtained from
C
by collapsing all loops
\gamma_{i}^{\pm}
. Let
\mathcal{C}\to Q:=\mathbb{B}^{2r}\subset\mathbb{C}^{2r}
be the associated family of deformations so that
C=\mathcal{C}|_{\{\mathbf{t}_{1}\}}
and
C_{0}=\mathcal{C}|_{\{\mathbf{0}\}}
and let
(\mathcal{V},\widetilde{\varphi})
be an extension of
(V,\varphi)
over all of
\mathcal{C}
. As the base of the deformation is connected,
(\mathfrak{o},\mathfrak{s})
determines an OSpin structure on
(V_{\mathbf{t}},\varphi_{\mathbf{t}})=(\mathcal{V},\widetilde{\varphi})|_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{t}}}
for each
\mathbf{t}\in Q
. It remains to associate to
\mathfrak{o}_{x_{\mathbf{0}}}
an orientation on
C_{0}
. Pulling
(V_{0},\widetilde{\varphi}_{0})
back to
\widetilde{C}_{0}
and using the exact triple

0\to D_{0}\to\widetilde{D}_{0}\to\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{z\in\mathcal{N}_{C_{0}}}(V_{0})_{z}\to 0

of Fredholm operators, it remains to fix an orientation of
\det(\widetilde{D}_{0})
. The normalisation
\widetilde{C}_{0}
is given by a disjoint union of a closed surface
\Sigma
and several copies of the disc labelled by
\{1,\dots,r\}
according to the given ordering of the boundary circles, each of which is attached to
\Sigma
by an interior node
z_{i}
. This yields an isomorphism

(B.2)

\det(D_{0})\otimes\bigotimes\limits_{1\leq i\leq r}(V_{0})_{z_{i}}\cong\bigotimes\limits_{1\leq i\leq r}\det(\widetilde{D}_{0}|_{\mathbb{D}_{i}})\otimes\det(\widetilde{D}_{0}|_{\Sigma})

We endow
\det(\widetilde{D}_{0}|_{\Sigma})
with the complex orientation if and only if
\langle{w_{\mathfrak{s}_{0}}(L_{0}),[\Sigma]}\rangle=0
. Meanwhile, we use the trivialisation of the real bundle pair over each disc induced by the spin structure to identify
\det(\widetilde{D}_{0}|_{\mathbb{D}_{i}})
with
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, e.g., given by evaluating at a point on the boundary and equipping it with the canonical induced orientation. Transporting the induced orientation of
\det(D_{0})
back to
\mathcal{C}_{1}
, we obtain the
intrinsic orientation
of
\det(D_{(L,\phi)})
.

On a nodal surface:
Let
\mathbf{C}
be a decorated symmetric nodal Riemann surface and suppose
(V,\varphi)
is a real vector bundle pair of rank
n
over the underlying symmetric surface
(C,\sigma)
equipped with an OSpin structure. Suppose
D_{V}
is a real Cauchy-Riemann operator on
V
. Let
(\widetilde{C},\widetilde{\sigma})
be its normalisation and let
(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{\varphi})
and
D_{\widetilde{V}}
be the pullback of
(V,\varphi)
and
D_{V}
to the normalisation. Then we obtain a short exact sequence

0\to D_{V}\to D_{\widetilde{V}}\to\bigoplus\limits_{x\in\mathcal{N}^{i}_{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}})_{x}\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{x\in\mathcal{N}^{b}_{C}}V_{x}\to 0

of Fredholm operators, where
\mathcal{N}^{i}_{C}
and
\mathcal{N}^{b}_{C}
are the sets of interior and boundary nodes of
C
. The last map is induced by the evaluation map
\xi\mapsto(\xi(x_{+})-\xi(x_{-}))_{x\in\mathcal{N}_{C}}
. By
[
63
]
, we obtain a unique isomorphism

\det(D_{V})\otimes\bigotimes\limits_{x\in\mathcal{N}^{i}_{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}})_{x}\otimes\bigotimes\limits_{x\in\mathcal{N}^{b}_{C}}V_{x}\cong\det(D_{\widetilde{V}})

so an orientation of
\det(D_{\widetilde{V}})
and an orientation of
V_{x}
for each
x\in\mathcal{N}_{x}
induces an orientation of
\det(D)
. If the orientation of
V
and
\det(D_{\widetilde{V}})
is induced by the pullback of an OSpin structure
\mathfrak{o}\mathfrak{s}
on
(V,\varphi)
, we call the induced orientation
\mathfrak{o}_{\text{int}}(V,\varphi;\mathfrak{o}\mathfrak{s})
of
\det(D)
the
intrinsic orientation
of
(V,\varphi)
induced by
\mathfrak{o}\mathfrak{s}
.

On the other hand, letting
\pi\colon\mathcal{C}\to B
be a flat deformation of
(C,\sigma)
over a connected base
B
and endowing the fibres of
\pi
with the induced decoration, we can extend
(V,\varphi)
to a real bundle pair
\mathcal{V}
over
(\mathcal{C},\widehat{\sigma})
. Let
\mathbf{C_{1}}
be the fibre of
\pi
over a regular value
b_{1}
and let
(V_{1},\varphi_{1})
be the restriction of
\mathcal{V}
to
C_{1}
. Any OSpin structure
\mathfrak{o}\mathfrak{s}
on
(V,\varphi)
extends to an OSpin structure on
\mathcal{V}
, which in turn restricts to an OSpin structure
\mathfrak{o}\mathfrak{s}_{1}
on
(V_{1},\varphi_{1})
. Thus, it induces an orientation on
\det(D_{V_{1}})
via the construction for smooth surfaces. Using any path from
b_{1}
to the image of
C
under
\pi
, we obtain a homotopy of Fredholm operators and, in particular, an induced orientation on
\det(D_{V})
. Following
[
9
]
, we call this orientation the
limiting orientation

\mathfrak{o}_{\lim}(V,\varphi;\mathfrak{o}\mathfrak{s})
of
D_{V}
.

Proposition B.11
.

Suppose
\mathbf{C}
is a decorated symmetric surface with a unique node
nd
of type (H1). Let
(V,\varphi)
be a real bundle pair over
\mathbf{C}
of rank
n
equipped with a relative OSpin structure
\mathfrak{o}\mathfrak{s}
. Then, the intrinsic and the limiting orientation on
D_{(V,\varphi)}
agree if and only if

(B.3)

n(|\pi_{0}(C^{\sigma})|-i)+\frac{n(n-1)}{2}\in 2\mathbb{Z},

where
i
is the position of the singular component of
C^{\sigma}
.

Proof.

Changing the order of the boundary components, we may assume by
[
9
, CROrient
1\mathfrak{o}\mathfrak{s}
(2)]
that the singular boundary component comes last. Collapsing the boundary of a tubular neighbourhood of the singular component of
C
and applying
[
9
, CROrient 7C(a)]
, we may assume that
C
is a cylinder with an (H1) node and that
(V,\varphi)
is the trivial real bundle pair, with the trivialisation obtained from the OSpin structure
(\mathfrak{o},\mathfrak{s}).

There are two ways of proving the desired statement, either by adapting the construction of
[
9
, §8.1]
to the case of the cylinder using
[
43
, Theorem C.1.10]
or by leveraging the results of
[
9
, §8]
. We will pursue the latter approach.

Let
(\mathcal{C}\to[0,2],\widetilde{\sigma})
be a flat deformation of
C=C_{0}
to a smooth cylinder
C_{1}=\mathcal{C}|_{\{1\}}
and further to a cylinder
C_{2}
with one conjugate (i.e., interior) node.
Extend the decoration of
(C,\sigma)
and the real vector bundle pair
(V,\varphi)
to a fibrewise decoration and a real vector bundle pair
(\mathcal{V},\widehat{\varphi})
on
\mathcal{C}
.
By
[
9
, CROrient 7C(a)]
, the intrinsic orientation of
D_{V_{2}}
agrees with the limiting orientation coming from the intrinsic orientation of
D_{V_{1}}
. Thus we may compare the intrinsic orientation of
D_{V_{0}}
with the orientation induced by this homotopy and the intrinsic orientation of
D_{V_{2}}
. The normalisations
\widetilde{C}_{0}
and
\widetilde{C}_{2}
are a disc and a disjoint pair of discs
\mathbb{D}_{1}\sqcup\mathbb{D}_{2}
, respectively. Recall that the orientation on
\det(D_{0})
and
\det(D_{2})
is induced by the exact sequences of Fredholm operators

(B.4)

0\to D_{0}\to\widetilde{D}_{0}\to V^{\varphi}\to 0,

respectively,

(B.5)

0\to D_{2}\to\widetilde{D}_{2}\to V\to 0,

where we identify the finite-dimensional vector spaces with the Fredholm operator given by the map to the trivial vector space. Since the trivial Cauchy–Riemann operator on the disc is unobstructed, we have that
H^{0}(\widetilde{D}_{0})\cong V^{\varphi}
and
H^{0}(\widetilde{D}_{2})=(V^{\varphi})^{\oplus 2}
. It follows that (
B.4
) yields the long exact sequence

(B.6)

0\to\ker(D_{0})\to V^{\varphi}\xrightarrow{v\mapsto v-v}V^{\varphi}\to\text{coker}(D_{0})\to 0,

whence
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(D_{0})=\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(V^{\varphi})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(V^{\varphi})^{\vee}\cong\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(0)
. The isomorphism is orientation-preserving since the zero map
V^{\varphi}\to V^{\varphi}
is homotopic to the identity. Meanwhile (
B.5
) is isomorphic to

(B.7)

0\to\ker(D_{2})\to V^{\varphi}\oplus V^{\varphi}\xrightarrow{(v,w)\mapsto w-v}V\to V/V^{\varphi}\to 0,

where we identify the vector bundles with the trivial vector space (using the spin structure). We can homotope the map
V^{\varphi}\oplus V^{\varphi}\xrightarrow{(v,w)\mapsto w-v}V
to the map
(v,w)\mapsto w
. Then, using that the canonical orientation of
V
as a complex vector space is given by
v_{1},\text{i}v_{1},v_{2},\dots,\text{i}v_{n}
for any basis
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
of
V^{\varphi}
, we obtain that the induced isomorphism

\det(D_{2})\oplus V\cong V^{\varphi}\oplus V^{\varphi}\circ V/V^{\varphi}

has orientation sign
(-1)^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}
with respect to the canonical orientation of
V^{\varphi}=\det(D_{2})
.
∎

For later use, we record the following statement about perturbed Cauchy–Riemann operators.

Lemma B.12
.

[
7
, Lemma 3.2]
Suppose
D\colon V\to W
is a Fredholm operator and
\nu\colon Z\to W
is a linear map from a finite-dimensional vector space so that
D+\nu
is surjective. Then there exists a canonical isomorphism

\det(D+\nu)\cong\det(D)\otimes\Lambda^{\max}Z

given by

v\wedge(v_{k+1},z_{1})\wedge\dots\wedge(v_{n},z_{n-k})\mapsto v\otimes([\nu(z_{n-k})]\wedge\dots\wedge[\nu(z_{m})])^{\vee}\otimes z_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge z_{m}

for any
v\in\Lambda^{\max}\ker(D)
and any basis
z_{1},\dots,z_{m}
of
Z
, where we identify
v=v_{1}\wedge\dots v_{k}
with the vector
(v_{1},0)\wedge\dots\wedge(v_{k},0)\in\Lambda^{k}\ker(D+\nu)
.∎

Appendix C
Rel–
C^{\infty}
structures and relative smoothings

C.1.
The category of rel–
C^{\infty}
manifolds

We recall the definition of a rel–
C^{\infty}
manifold from
[
57
]
. We write
Y/S
for the datum of a continuous map
p\colon Y\to S
, whenever
p
is clear from the context.

Definition C.1

(Rel–
C^{\infty}
maps)
.

Given spaces
S
and
T
and integers
m,n\geq 0
, suppose
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times S
and
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\times T
are open subsets. A
rel–
C^{\infty}
map

\varphi=(\tilde{\varphi},\bar{\varphi})\colon U/S\to V/T
consists of a continuous map
\varphi\colon S\to T
and a continuous map
\tilde{\varphi}\colon U\to V
having the form

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}(x,s)=(F(x,s),\varphi(s)),

where
F\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is such that all its partial derivatives
D^{\alpha}_{x}F\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
with respect to
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
are defined and continuous as functions on
U
.

Definition C.2

(Rel–
C^{\infty}
manifolds)
.

For a continuous map of spaces
p\colon Y\to S
and an integer
n\geq 0
, a
chart
of relative dimension
n
is an open subset
U\subset Y
together with an open embedding
\varphi\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\times S
which is compatible with the projection maps to
S
. Two charts
(U,\bar{\varphi})
and
(V,\psi)
of relative dimension
n
for
Y/S
are said to be
rel–
C^{\infty}
compatible
if

\displaystyle(\psi\circ\varphi^{-1},\text{id}_{S})\colon\varphi(U\cap V)/S\to\psi(U\cap V)/S

is a
rel–
C^{\infty}
diffeomorphism
, i.e., a rel–
C^{\infty}
map with a rel–
C^{\infty}
inverse.

A
rel–
C^{\infty}
structure
on
Y/S
is a maximal atlas of rel–
C^{\infty}
compatible charts for
Y/S
. In this situation, we refer to
Y/S
with this atlas as a
rel–
C^{\infty}
manifold
or we say that
Y
has the structure of a rel–
C^{\infty}
manifold over
S
.

Definition C.3
.

Given rel–
C^{\infty}
manifolds
Y/S
and
Z/T
, a
rel–
C^{\infty}
map
\varphi=(\tilde{\varphi},\bar{\varphi})\colon Y/S\to Z/T
is a pair of continuous maps intertwining the structure maps so that, in any local chart belonging to the maximal atlases of
Y/S
and
Z/T
, the pair
(\tilde{\varphi},\bar{\varphi})
induces a rel–
C^{\infty}
map between open subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times S
and
\mathbb{R}^{m}\times T
for some integers
m,n\geq 0
.

The
category of rel–
C^{\infty}
manifolds

(C^{\infty}/\cdot)
has as objects rel–
C^{\infty}
manifolds and as morphisms rel–
C^{\infty}
maps.

Definition C.4
.

A
rel–
C^{\infty}
vector bundle
on
Y/S
is a topological vector bundle
E
on
Y
along with a maximal atlas of local trivialisations whose transition functions are rel–
C^{\infty}
maps to
\text{GL}_{\mathbb{K}}(\text{rank})
for
\mathbb{K}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
.

As,
E/S
is naturally a rel–
C^{\infty}
manifold, there is a well-defined notion of rel–
C^{\infty}
sections of a rel–
C^{\infty}
vector bundle.

Example C.5
.

The pullback of a continuous vector bundle
E\to S
is a rel–
C^{\infty}
vector bundle on
Y/S
.

Example C.6
.

Any rel–
C^{\infty}
manifold
Y/S
has a well-defined
vertical tangent bundle
denoted by
T_{Y/S}
. This is a rel–
C^{\infty}
real vector bundle on
Y/S
.

Definition C.7
.

A morphism
f\colon Y^{\prime}/S\to Y/S
in
(C^{\infty}/\cdot)
, where the underlying map
S\to S
is the identity, is called a
(rel–
C^{\infty}
) vertical submersion
if the differential
df\colon T_{Y^{\prime}/S}\to f^{*}T_{Y/S}
is surjective.

Rel–
C^{\infty}
submersions are given by coordinate projections in suitable local coordinates just like
C^{\infty}
submersions. This follows from the inverse function theorem with parameters,
[
57
, Lemma 5.10]
. Similarly, we have a well-behaved notion of
vertical transversality
for rel–
C^{\infty}
maps, which is the analogue of usual transversality formulated using the vertical tangent bundle.

Definition C.8
.

Let
(\widetilde{C}^{\infty}/\cdot)
be the category whose objects are rel–
C^{\infty}
-manifolds
Y\xrightarrow{p}S
, together with the structure of a smooth manifold with corners on
S
, and whose morphisms are rel–
C^{\infty}
morphism
(\widetilde{\varphi},\bar{\varphi}
, where
\bar{\varphi}
is required to be smooth.

There is a canonical forgetful functor
(\widetilde{C}^{\infty}/\cdot)\to(C^{\infty}/\cdot)
. By abuse of notation, we call objects of
(\widetilde{C}^{\infty}/\cdot)
also rel–
C^{\infty}
manifolds with the understanding that the base will from now on always admits a smooth structure.

Remark C.9
.

There also exists a forgetful functor from
(\widetilde{C}^{\infty}/\cdot)
to the category of topological manifolds.

Definition C.10
.

The
tangent bundle
of
Y\xrightarrow{p}S
in
(\widetilde{C}^{\infty}/\cdot)
is the rel–
C^{\infty}
vector bundle

TY:=T_{Y/S}\oplus p^{*}TS.

Definition C.11
.

An
orientation
of
Y/S
is an orientation of the tangent bundle
TY
.

Clearly, any morphism
\varphi=(\widetilde{\varphi},\bar{\varphi}\colon Y/S\to Y^{\prime}/S^{\prime}
has a well-defined differential

(C.1)

d\varphi\colon TY\to TY^{\prime}:(y,\hat{y},v)\mapsto(\widetilde{\varphi}(y),d\widetilde{\varphi}(y)\hat{y},d\bar{\varphi}(p(y))v)

which defines a rel–
C^{\infty}
map
TY/S\to TY^{\prime}/S^{\prime}
. If
\varphi
is an isomorphism, its
orientation sign
is
\pm 1
, depending on whether it is orientation-preserving or orientation-reversing. For the next definition, we have to be careful in the case where the base has corners. Following
[
55
]
, we say a smooth map
S\to S^{\prime}
between manifolds with corneres is a submersion if it is locally diffeomorphism to a projection
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n-k}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}
.

Definition C.12
.

We call
\varphi\colon Y/S\to Y^{\prime}/S^{\prime}
a
rel–
C^{\infty}
submersion
if the map
S\to S^{\prime}
is a submersion and
d^{v}\varphi\colon T^{v}Y\to T^{v}Y^{\prime}
is fibrewise surjective.
